A CHRONOLOGY OF PRINCIPAL EVENTS RELATING TO ARMS CONTROL AND DISARMAMENT, NOVEMBER 1964 

I. 

HIGHLIGHTS 

Major developments during the month focused on the problem of the proliferation of nuclear weapons. The 

White House on November 1 announced that President Johnson had appointed former Deputy Secretary of Defense Roswell L.

Gilpatric to head a special panel to study means to prevent the spread of nuclear weapons. AEC Chairman Seaborg on 

November 22, in a letter to the New York Times, upheld the efficacy of IAEA safeguards and argued against a suggestion that inspection responsibilities be assigned to an arms control agency other than the IAEA. Earlier, on November 15, he had announced that the first safeguards inspection of the 

Yankee nuclear power reactor, at Rowe, Mass., by the IAEA would begin on November 16.

In several speeches, Prime Minister Shastri defended the Indian government's decision not to manufacture nuclear weapons. He said his government was not unmindful of the recent Chinese nuclear test, but rather than compete with the great powers in the nuclear weapons field or accept their protection under a nuclear umbrella India would pursue its opposition to nuclear proliferation (November 8, 23, 24). 

Replying to Chou's October 17 letter proposing a world summit conference on disarmament, Ghanaian President Nkrumah "sympathized" with the proposal but deplored its transmittal on the heels of the Chinese nuclear test. Nkrumah proposed that the original sponsors of the nonaligned conference "initiate and promote" a five-nation nuclear dialog 

(November 5). On November 22, the Peiping People's Daily rejected such a dialog. It criticized the ENDC for not having "solved a single question of substance" and ruled out participation in that forum until the United Nations restored Peiping's "legitimate rights as the sole legal" representative of all China. 1090233

- 2 - November 1, 1964 

II. CHRONOLOGY 

The White House announced that President Johnson had appointed former Deputy Secretary of Defense Roswell L. 

Gilpatric to head a special panel to study new policies to prevent the spread of nuclear weapons. The President requested the panel "to explore the widest range of measures that the United States might undertake in conjunction with other governments or by itself to accomplish this end." 

The announcement stated further that "full consideration will be given to the adequacy of existing policies designed to limit the spread of nuclear weapons" and that the task force also "will examine the implications of the development of peaceful uses of atomic energy and safeguards associated with this program." 

November 5, 1964

President Nkrumah of Ghana, in reply to Chinese Communist 

Premier Chou En-loi's October 17 call for a world summit meeting on disarmament, proposed that the original sponsors of the nonaligned conference Yugoslavia, Indonesia, the UAR, India, and Ghana -- "initiate and promote" a conference among the nuclear powers -- the United States, Britain, France, the Soviet Union and China ... to consider practical measures for the complete abandonment of nuclear weapons. 11 In his message, Nkrumah "sympathized" with Chou's proposal, but stated that the manner in which that appeal was so closely followed by the Chinese nuclear test had created "a certain amount of resistance to an excellent proposal" (New York Times, November 6, 1964; Peiping NCNA, November 5, 1964). 

November 6, 1964

In a report delivered on the eve of the 47th anniversary of the Bolshevik Revolution, First Secretary of the CPSU 

Leonid Brezhnev stated, inter alia, that the Soviet Union still advocated the termination of the arms race and the conclusion of an agreement on disarmament. Reaffirming

-- - 3 - Soviet opposition to nuclear proliferation, he criticized the West's plan for a multilateral nuclear force and charged that this would give the Federal Republic of Germany access to nuclear weapons (New York Times, November 7, 1964). 

November 8, 1964

In a speech before the All-India Congress Committee, Prime Minister Shastri of India defended his government's decision not to manufacture nuclear weapons and stated that India would still pursue its policy of non-alignment. He emphasized that India was not unmindful of the threat posed by Communist China's development of the atomic bomb. Rather than compete with the great powers in the manufacture of nuclear weapons or accept protection under a "nuclear umbrella," however, he said that India should "arouse the conscience of the nations against nuclear weapons, pecially in the forum of the United Nations (Delhi Information 

Service, November 8, 1964). 

November 9, 1964 es- In its 1964 financial report, the U.S. Atomic Energy 

Commission stated that the costs of weapons development and fabrication during the current year had increased to $805 million, 15 percent above those for 1963, "mainly as a result of costs related to the safeguards commitments in connection with the test ban treaty including increased underground testing, maintenance of weapons laboratories, and developing a readiness capability for the conduct of atmospheric tests" (U.S. Atomic Energy Commission, 1964 

Financial Report, p. 5). 

November 10, 1964

The economic impact of defense spending was discussed by Adam Yarmolinsky, special assistant to Secretary of Defense McNamara, in his address before the Science Bureau of the Metropolitan Washington Board of Trade.

CHRONOLOGY 

November 1, 1964 The White House announced that President Johnson had appointed former Deputy Secretary of Defense Roswell L. 

Gilpatric to head a special panel to study new policies to prevent the spread of nuclear weapons. The President requested the panel "to explore the widest range of measures that the United States might undertake in conjunction with other governments or by itself to accomplish this end." 

The announcement stated further that "full consideration will be given to the adequacy of existing policies designed to limit the spread of nuclear weapons" and that the task force also "will examine the implications of the development of peaceful uses of atomic energy and safeguards associated with this program." 

November 5, 1964 

President Nkrumah of Ghana, in reply to Chinese Communist 

Premier Chou En-loi's October 17 call for a world summit meeting on disarmament, proposed that the original sponsors of the nonaligned conference Yugoslavia, Indonesia, the UAR, India, and Ghana -- "initiate and promote" a conference among the nuclear powers the United States, Britain, France, the Soviet Union and China ... to consider practical measures for the complete abandonment of nuclear weapons." 

In his message, Nkrumah "sympathized" with Chou's proposal, but stated that the manner in which that appeal was so closely followed by the Chinese nuclear test had created "a certain amount of resistance to an excellent proposal" (New York Times, November 6, 1964; Peiping NCNA, November 5, 1964). 

November 6, 1964

In a report delivered on the eve of the 47th anniversary of the Bolshevik Revolution, First Secretary of the CPSU 

Leonid Brezhnev stated, inter alia, that the Soviet Union still advocated the termination of the arms race and the conclusion of an agreement on disarmament. Reaffirming

-- 
-- - 3 - Soviet opposition to nuclear proliferation, he criticized the West's plan for a multilateral nuclear force and charged that this would give the Federal Republic of Germany access to nuclear weapons (New York Times, November 7, 1964). 

November 8, 1964

In a speech before the All-India Congress Committee, Prime Minister Shastri of India defended his government's decision not to manufacture nuclear weapons and stated that India would still pursue its policy of non-alignment. He emphasized that India was not unmindful of the threat posed by Communist China's development of the atomic bomb. Rather than compete with the great powers in the manufacture of nuclear weapons or accept protection under a "nuclear umbrella," however, he said that India should "arouse the conscience of the nations against nuclear weapons pecially in the forum of the United Nations (Delhi Information 

Service, November 8, 1964). 

November 9, 1964 11 In its 1964 financial report, the U.S. Atomic Energy 

Commission stated that the costs of weapons development and fabrication during the current year had increased to $805 million, 15 percent above those for 1963, "mainly as a result of costs related to the safeguards commitments in connection with the test ban treaty including increased underground testing, maintenance of weapons laboratories, and developing a readiness capability for the conduct of atmospheric tests" (U.S. Atomic Energy Commission, 1964 

Financial Report, p. 5). 

November 10, 1964

The economic impact of defense spending was discussed by Adam Yarmolinsky, special assistant to Secretary of Defense McNamara, in his address before the Science Bureau of the Metropolitan Washington Board of Trade. 

Yarmolinsky described the U.S. Government's activities in this sphere and especially noted the studies it is conducting Mr.

4 

with the view toward setting up an "early warning system" for major geographical areas affected by cutbacks in defense spending, to determine what assistance can be given to industry to deal with such shifts, and to study what happens to employees laid off as a result of such cutbacks (Washington Evening Star, November 11, 1964). 

November 15, 1964

U.S. Atomic Energy Commission Chairman Dr. Glenn T. 

Seaborg announced that the first safeguards inspection of the Yankee nuclear power reactor at Rowe, Mass., by the IAEA would begin on November 16. Three IAEA reactor experts from Yugoslavia, Sweden, and Japan were to undertake the first inspection (AEC press release, G-258, November 15, 1964). 1/ 

November 16, 1964

The U.S. Atomic Energy Commission announced that it had "recorded seismic signals from an event in the Soviet nuclear testing area in the Semipalatinsk region" (AEC press release G-262, November 16, 1964).

U.S. Department of State press spokesman Robert J. 

McCloskey said that he had no information concerning reports that Indonesia would detonate a nuclear device next year. 

Indonesia had obtained a small atomic reactor from the United States under a 1960 agreement, and it became operational in October of this year. Mr. McCloskey said the Indonesian reactor was "incapable of producing materials for weapons or of generating power on a commercial basis." Under the 1960 agreement, the United States had the right to inspect the reactor (Washington Evening Star, November 17, 1964). 

1/ For background, see Chronology entries for March 5 and June 11, 1964.

November 22, 1964 

In a letter to the New York Times, Dr. Glenn T.

Seaborg, Chairman of the U.S. Atomic Energy Commission, stated that "international controls can make the greatest contribution to preventing the proliferation of nuclear weapons, since they provide a single consistent system whose results will be credible to all countries." It was for this reason, he said, that the United States strongly favored the adoption by all countries of IAEA controls. 

Commenting on a New York Times suggestion that inspection responsibilities might be assigned to an arms control agency other than the IAEA,, Dr. Seaborg stressed the efficacy of the system of safeguards applied by the IAEA as being compatible with the latter's role in encouraging the peaceful use of nuclear energy. He advised against any attempt "to create and substitute a new and untested agency for one which is gradually acquiring technical competence and ... respect of its members both suppliers and recipients of nuclear assistance alike" (New York Times, November 22, 1964).

An editorial in the Peiping People's Daily rejected the suggestion that Communist China participate in a fivenation nuclear dialog and instead reiterated Chou's 

October 17 proposal calling for a world summit conference on disarmament. The editorial downgraded UN efforts toward reaching an accord on disarmament and especially criticized the Eighteen Nation Disarmament Committee for not having "solved a single question of substance."

Regarding participation in the ENDC, the editorial stated that the Geneva disarmament talks were within the framework of the United Nations and that Peiping would have nothing to do with the latter so long as it failed "to restore to the CPR her legitimate rights as the sole legal government representing the Chinese people" (New York Times, November 23, 1964).

November 23-24, 1964 

Speaking in Parliament, Prime Minister Shastri of 

India once again reaffirmed his government's present policy of developing atomic energy for peaceful purposes. 

He stated that the Indian government was cognizant of the implications of the recent nuclear test conducted by Communist China and would consider steps to be taken at the appropriate time. Prime Minister Shastri reportedly rejected suggestions that India scuttle this policy and instead declared that it was in India's best interests to muster world opinion against the use or manufacture of atomic bombs. He reiterated that the United Nations was the proper forum to discuss the matter and indicated that India would request that an item be placed on the agenda of the General Assembly concerning the prevention of the spread of nuclear weapons. 

November 23-27, 1964

Seventeen Latin American Republics held a preliminary meeting at Mexico City on the denuclearization of Latin 

America. The meeting unanimously reaffirmed the proposed denuclearization of Latin America as a goal and set up a Preparatory Commission to study the question and prepare a preliminary draft treaty (A/5824, December 3, 1964). 

November 1964 Writing in the unofficial Bulletin of the Atomic 

Scientists, Alexander Rich, professor of biophysics at M.I.T. and Alexander P. Vinogradov, professor of geochemistry at

Moscow University and member of the Soviet Academy of Sciences, privately proposed that the United States and the Soviet Union conclude a treaty under which specific disarmament measures might be applied to the entire Arctic zone or limited to either Alaska and eastern Siberia or 

Greenland, Alaska, and eastern Siberia. Under the proposal, both signatories would be prohibited from stationing nuclear weapons or nuclear delivery vehicles, long-range bombers or missiles in the region, but would be permitted to retain certain defensive installations. This plan, the writers suggested, could facilitate the establishment of a reliable inspection system and lead to the development of a more comprehensive disarmament plan. (This article did not appear in the Soviet press in 1964).

White House Press Release 

UNCLASSIFIED

December 30, 1964 

Office of the White House Press Secretary 

(Austin, Texas)

THE WHITE HOUSE TEXT OF LETTERS FROM PRESIDENT JOHNSON TO ANASTAS MIKOYAN, CHAIR- MAN OF PRESIDIUM OF SUPREME SOVIET OF U.S.S.R., AND ALEKSEY NIKOLAYEVICH KOSYGIN, CHAIRMAN OF COUNCIL OF MINISTERS OF U.S.S.R. 

Dear Mr. Chairman: 

Personally, and on behalf of the American people, I extend to you and the Soviet people greetings and best wishes for the New Year. 

The year just ending has produced significant accomplishment in some areas of endeavor. But major international problems are unresolved and the most urgent business for all of us remains strengthening the foundation of world peace. In this task, our two governments bear great responsibilities and it is my earnest wish that in the coming year we can make substantial progress. 

Arms control remains especially urgent; nothing can contribute more to the hopes of mankind for the future. During the months ahead I hope we can work for practical agreements to this end. We can and should move to limit the spread of nuclear weapons; to achieve a verified worldwide comprehensive test ban; to make a cut-off of fissionable material production for weapons coupled with measures to safeguard the peaceful uses of nuclear power; and to agree on a verified freeze in existing offensive and defensive strategic nuclear delivery systems. 

By progress in this critical area, our governments can help to make this a happier and safer world for all peoples. You may be certain that the American people and their government will never be second in this effort. 

Sincerely yours, Lyndon B. Johnson # # # # #

BACKGROUND INFORMATION ON THE NON-DISSEMINATION OF NUCLEAR WEAPONS 

General

The U.S. Atomic Energy Act prohibits the transfer of nuclear weapons to other nations. A 1958 amendment permits the transfer to other nations that have made "substantial progress in the development of atomic weapons" of nonnuclear parts of atomic weapons and special nuclear materials if such transfer promotes the common defense and security.1 

The United Kingdom is the only country which has benefited from the 1958 amendment.2 Accordingly, the 

United States has refrained from assisting the nuclear programs of its allies. Stocks of U.S. atomic weapons abroad remain under U.S. control, even though some of these weapons are intended for allied use in event of war. 

The Soviet Union has also pursued a policy of refraining from the transfer of nuclear weapons to its allies. The Chinese Communists have attacked the Soviet 

Union for refusing to give China a sample atomic bomb and technical data on its manufacture. 3 

The first Western non-transfer measure was part of a package proposal submitted to the five-nation Subcommittee of the Disarmament Commission on August 29, 1957. From the time of the fissionable materials production cutoff, each party would undertake "not to transfer out of its control any nuclear weapons, or to accept transfer to it of such weapons, except under arrangements which would assure their use only for defensive purposes. This clause was linked with a proposed pledge not to use weapons if an armed attack had not placed a party "in a situation of individual or collective self-defense. 114 V. A. Zorin, the Soviet representative, construed this proposal to mean that a state could claim it had been attacked and borrow nuclear weapons from its allies. 

Other Soviet statements charged that the United States had already transferred nuclear weapons to the Federal Republic of Germany. 

172 Stat. 276. 

2See Anglo-American agreements of 1958 (9UST 1028) and 1959 (10 UST 1274). 

3Documents on Disarmament, 1963, pp. 363-364. 

4Ibid., 1945-1959, vol. II, pp. 870-871. 

5DC/SC.1/PV.153, p.53.

Mr. Stassen denied these charges and defended the nontransfer provisions as a "definite step towards bringing the nuclear weapons situation under control."1 

The Soviet Union, however, continued to attack NATO nuclear policy. A Soviet Government statement of September 20, 1957, proposed an agreement "not to allow the installation of any atomic military units or of any types of atomic or hydrogen weapons beyond their national frontiers and not to place these weapons at the disposal of any other States or commands of military blocs."2

Shortly after making this statement, the Soviet Union announced that it would no longer participate in the Disarmament Subcommittee. There were no further general disarmament negotiations until 1960, when they resumed in the Ten Nation Disarmament Committee. 

Secretary of State Herter expressed continued interest in measures to halt the spread of nuclear weapons in an address of February 18, 1960. He cited diffusion as one of the two chief risks of the continuing arms race (the other was war by miscalculation). He feared that proliferation would enable irresponsible countries to obtain nuclear weapons, and he thought that the chances of triggering nuclear war by "blind folly" increased as more nations entered the nuclear club.3 The United 

States did not, however, propose any specific non-transfer measures in the Ten Nation Disarmament Committee. 

The first Soviet proposal for general and complete disarmament, submitted to the General Assembly in September 1959, did not contain a non-transfer clause. However, the 

Soviet proposal of June 2, 1960, later submitted to the Ten Nation Disarmament Committee, included a first-stage ban on the transfer of nuclear weapons or "information necessary for their manufacture."4 The same provision was retained in the revised Soviet proposal submitted to the 

General Assembly on September 23, 1960.5

In the meantime, the Soviet Union had walked out of the 

Ten Nation Disarmament Committee. General disarmament negotiations were again interrupted for nearly two years. 

On September 25, 1961, the United States submitted a program for general and complete disarmament to the General Assembly. This program included a stage I ban on relinquishing control of nuclear weapons to states not owning them and on the acquisition or production of nuclear weapons by such states.1 Stage I provisions a against transfer or acquisition were also included in the U.S. treaty outline submitted to the Eighteen Nation 

Disarmament Committee on April 18, 1962.2

The Soviet draft treaty of March 15, 1962, included stage I provisions banning dissemination of nuclear weapons by the nuclear powers and acquisition by other states. The non-acquisition clause required states not only to refrain from producing or acquiring weapons but also to refuse to admit foreign weapons to their territories.3

The Soviet Union also proposed non-dissemination, as a collateral measure, and it was listed as a Soviet item on the agenda of the Committee of the Whole, where it was paired with an American item on measures to reduce the risk of war. 

4 At the last meeting of this committee (July 19, 1962), Ambassador Zorin discussed non-dissemination and Ambassador Dean talked about the American item. 

Ambassador Zorin attacked the Federal Republic of Germany and proposed an agreement among the four nuclear powers "not to deliver nuclear weapons, control over them or information necessary for their manufacture to States which at the present time do not possess them."5 In reply, Ambassador Dean cited the 1954 FRG pledge not to manufacture nuclear weapons on its territory and pointed out that FRG defense was completely integrated into the NATO framework. He denied that the FRG had control of any nuclear weapons or was seeking to obtain nuclear weapons for itself.6

In his message of July 14, 1962, to the ENDC, President Kennedy said that the United States would continue to seek agreement to meet the danger of "the spread of nuclear weapons into the arsenals of a widening number of countries. "1

The subject was not pursued at Geneva at that time because of a decision to hold informal bila teral talks elsewhere.

In his message of January 21, 1964, to the ENDC, President Johnson reaffirmed previous American proposals for a cutoff on the production of fissionable materials for weapons purposes. He also urged the following steps to stop the spread of nuclear weapons to countries which did not already possess them: (A) That nuclear weapons not be transferred into the national control of states which do not now control them, and that all transfers of nuclear materials for peaceful purposes take place under effective international safeguards; 

3) That the major nuclear powers accept in an increasing number of their peaceful nuclear activities the same inspection they recommend for other states; and (c) On the banning of all nuclear weapons tests under effective verification and control.2

American efforts to implement the Johnson program are discussed below. The United States has continued to try to reach a non-proliferation agreement with the Soviet Union, based on the Irish resolution which both countries supported, but Soviet opposition to the proposed multilateral nuclear force has so far blocked agreement.

Irish resolution and a Non-Proliferation Agreement

Since 1958 Ireland has sponsored a number of General Assembly resolutions to prevent further nuclear dissemination.1 

The latest of these resolutions, unanimously adopted by the General Assembly on December 4, 1961, calls upon all states, particularly the nuclear powers, their best endeavours to secure the conclusion of an international agreement containing provisions under which the nuclear States would undertake to refrain from relinquishing control of nuclear weapons and from transmitting the information necessary for their manufacture to States not possessing such weapons, and provisions under which States not possessing nuclear weapons would undertake not to manufacture or otherwise acquire control of such weapons. 

On February 6, 1964, Mr. Foster told the ENDC that the United States would have private discussions with the Soviet Union in an effort to reach agreement on a declaration based on the Irish resolution. Such a declaration, covering both non-dissemination and non-acquisition, would be subject to accession by nuclear and non-nuclear states. "As an immediate step and to facilitate progress in these discussions," Mr. Foster said, "the United States, for its part, does not intend to take any actions inconsistent with the terms of the Irish resolution. That is the declared policy of the United States."3

The Soviet Union also favored a non-dissemination agreement. It had supported the Irish resolution, and non-dissemination was the sixth item in its nine-point memorandum of January 28, 1964. But it insisted that a non-dissemination agreement should be broad enough to prevent establishment of the multilateral nuclear force which the United States and some of its allies were discussing. 

Soviet opposition to the MLF centered on the participation of the Federal Republic of Germany. 

1See Documents on Disarmament, 1945-1959, vol. II, pp. 1185-1186, 1547; 1960, p. 373. 

2Ibid., 1961, p. 694. 

3ENDC/PV.164, p. 8. 4ENDC/123.

1  A Soviet note to the United States (July 11, 1964) charged that FRG "military and political circles" regarded the MLF as only a beginning and sought a dominant role in the project. For its part, the Soviet Union still advocated measures to prevent nuclear dissemination and supported proposals for nuclear-free zones. In the Soviet view, those who favored both the MLF and non-proliferation were trying to reconcile the irreconcilable: 

On this matter, which vitally affects the vital interests of all States and peoples, there are only two possible positions which must be clearly distinguished from one another either actively to follow the principle of preventing the dissemination of nuclear weapons in any form or to take the course of making these weapons available to other States, and then the question of the form and the manner in which they are made available has no serious significance. But those who take the second course would inevitably have to reckon with the dangerous consequences to which this might lead for the cause of peace and, not least, for themselves.1

In its reply, the United States maintained that the 

MLF was entirely defensive. It reaffirmed opposition to "the proliferation of national nuclear weapons capabilities" and maintained that the MLF was "specifically designed to be consistent with this position. It pointed out that the MLF could be used only by decision of its governing body and that safeguards would prevent accidental or unauthorized use.

The United States was still ready to conclude an agreement in conformity with the Irish resolution. If the USSR sincerely believed that the MLF was a stepping stone to transferring nuclear weapons to a specific nonnuclear power, such an agreement would give the USSR additional assurance. It 1ENDC/137.

-- 
-- PP The United States was "ready at all times to discuss this urgent issue, but with the clear understanding that such discussions cannot be used to frustrate security arrangements for the defense of the United States and its allies." 

It regretted the "unrealistic and unrestrained charges" the USSR had made against the FRG.1 

A similar exchange of views on the MLF took place in the ENDC, where Mr. Foster said that no single participant would be able to fire the missiles, since "firing of missiles in wartime would be by decision of the United 

States and an agreed number of other participants."2

Reiterating U.S. support for the Irish resolution, Mr. Foster stated: ...In keeping with that call, the United

States has been seeking, and will continue to seek, an international agreement under which the nuclear powers would commit themselves not to transfer nuclear weapons into national control of states not now possessing them, as well as not to assist such states in manufacturing nuclear weapons. Such an agreement would facilitate a parallel undertaking by non-nuclear powers not to manufacture such weapons and to refrain from acquiring control over such weapons and from seeking or receiving assistance in manufacturing them.3

The Baruch plan 

The Baruch plan (1946) was a proposal for an international authority which would own or have managerial control of all "dangerous" atomic materials from the mines to the finished products. The international authority would be empowered to impose sanctions for minor violations, and a veto-free Security Council would punish major offenders.1 Since the United States was the only nation then possessing atomic weapons, adoption of the plan would have prevented diffusion by making it impossible for any country to manufacture them unless it was willing to take the risk of openly violating the treaty and inviting UN reprisal. The then small stock of U.S. weapons was to be disposed of after the control system was set up. The Baruch plan was endorsed by the majority of UN members. 

The Soviet Union rejected the plan because it objected to the ownership, staging, and enforcement provisions. The Soviet counterproposals (1947) left atomic energy resources in national hands and gave the international control organ the power only to conduct periodic 2 or special inspections. At first, the Soviets apparently contemplated banning atomic weapons before setting up the control system. Later, they stated that prohibition and control should be simultaneous. By 1952 they were willing to accept continuous inspection.3

Although the United States dropped its demand for abolition of the Security Council veto in 1954,4 it was still impossible to reach agreement on control. A 

Soviet proposal of 1955 admitted that it was not feasible to guarantee the elimination of stocks or prevent secret production of weapons. Because of the stockpile, problem the United States abandoned the Baruch plan on September 

6, 1955.6

Bomber destruction

The American proposal for the destruction by the United States and the Soviet Union of an equal number of B-47s and TU-16s had non-proliferation implications. 

This proposal, previously discussed informally with the Soviets, came before the ENDC in 1964, when the USSR proposed the abolition of all bombers.1 When he presented the proposal to the ENDC on March 19, 1964, Deputy ACDA 

Director Fisher stated that destruction of the B-47s and

TU-16s would insure that they could not be transferred to other nations and thus "impose an important restraint on the proliferation of highly-sophisticated weapon systems." 

By taking this simple step, we could "begin to reduce the destructive capability present in the world and lessen the dangers of its proliferation" and move towards reducing the nuclear threat which hangs over all mankind."2

The Soviet Union has rejected the American proposal on the ground that destruction of planes that are already being phased out is not real disarmament.3 

Cutoff of fissionable materials production

The United States dropped the Baruch plan in 1955 and did not again advocate the total elimination of nuclear weapons stocks until 1960, after the shift to general and complete disarmament. In the meantime, it developed a new proposal to halt the growth of stockpiles and inhibit the spread of nuclear weapons. 

In a letter of March 1, 1956, to Premier Bulganin, President Eisenhower stated that the United States "would be prepared to work out, with other nations, suitable and safeguarded arrangements so that future production of fissionable materials anywhere in the world would no longer be used to increase the stockpiles of explosive weapons. 14

In 1957 this proposal for a cutoff of production for weapons uses was combined with a proposal to transfer fissionable materials for peaceful purposes. On July 5, 1957, Mr. Stassen proposed in the Disarmament Subcommittee that 50 percent of the first transfer for peaceful uses after the cutoff should come from the United States 1ENDC/123. 

2ENDC/PV.176, p. 8. 

3ENDC/PV.180, pp. 26-29. 

4Documents on Disarmament, 1945-1959, vol. I, pp. 593-594.

and the other 50 percent from the Soviet Union. If it was so desired, however, the United States would be willing to make 53 percent of the transfer as against 47 percent from the Soviet Union. 

The Western proposal of August 29, 1957, included a proposal for a cutoff, an experts meeting to work out an inspection system, and successive transfers to peaceful uses of fissionable materials produced in the past, to begin "at agreed dates and in agreed quantities" following the cutoff.2 The Soviet Union, however, attacked the cutoff proposal for failing to provide for a ban on the use of nuclear weapons or to eliminate them from the armaments of states. It also noted that states would still be able to manufacture nuclear weapons from fissionable materials already stockpiled.3 

On January 12, 1958, President Eisenhower wrote to

Premier Bulganin that existing nuclear weapons stocks, after a cutoff, could be steadily reduced by transfers to peaceful uses. Since the American stockpile was presumably larger, the United States would be willing to make proportionately greater transfers than the USSR, and the President invited Soviet suggestions for an equitable ratio.4

No such suggestions were forthcoming, either then or later.

The cutoff and transfer were included in the Western proposal submitted to the Ten Nation Disarmament Committee on March 16, 1960, where the cutoff was to be preceded by a joint study. It was also conditional on satisfactory progress in conventional disarmament.5 The U.S. paper of 

June 27 contained similar provisions.6 

At the August 16 meeting of the UN Disarmament

Commission, Ambassador Lodge suggested a new approach. 

1DC/SC.1/PV.130, pp. 5-6. 

2Documents on Disarmament, 1945-1959, vol. II, p. 870. 3Ibid., pp. 878-879. 

4Ibid., p. 939. 

5Ibid., 1960, p.70. 

6Ibid., p. 130.

. 

As an alternative to the blanket cutoff, he suggested a reciprocal plant-by-plant shutdown of equivalent production facilities in the United States and the Soviet Union. He also said that the United States would be willing, after the cutoff became effective, to set aside for peaceful purposes 30,000 kilograms of weapons-grade

U-235 if the Soviet Union made an equal contribution. 1 

In an address of September 22, 1960, to the General Assembly, President Eisenhower proposed an immediate experts meeting to design an inspection system for a cutoff. 

He did not link the cutoff with conventional disarmament, as had been done in earlier American proposals. He repeated Lodge's proposals for the transfer and a plantby-plant shutdown.2

The cutoff and transfer again appeared as stage I measures in the U.S. program of September 25, 1961. 

This program also specified that peaceful transfers of fissionable materials between countries would be "subject to appropriate safeguards to be developed in agreement with the IAEA."3 At the opening of the ENDC negotiations in March 1962, Secretary of State Rusk proposed that the United States and the USSR each follow the cutoff by transferring 50,000 kilograms of weapons-grade

U-235 to peaceful uses. 4 He later suggested that the cutoff could be put into effect separately from the program for general and complete disarmament.5 

The U.S. treaty outline of April 18, 1962, included the cutoff and transfer as stage I measures. The transfers of fissionable materials for peaceful uses within the producing country would be placed under IDO safeguards, while transfers between states would be subject to safeguards developed in agreement with the IAEA.6 

1Ibid., pp. 222-223. 

2Ibid., p. 228. 

3Ibid., 1961, p. 478. 

4Ibid., 1962, vol. I, pp. 146-147. 

5Ibid., pp. 198-199. 

6Ibid., pp. 358-359.

In April 1963 the American delegation informally discussed the cutoff and transfer with the Soviet delegation at Geneva. The Americans then indicated that the United States would be prepared to consider a larger amount of material for the transfer, and they offered a differential rate: 60,000 kilograms by the United States and 40,000 kilograms by the Soviet Union. The Soviet reaction was negative. On August 14, 1963, the United

States tabled draft treaty language leaving the amounts blank. 2 

Ambassador Tsarapkin opposed the cutoff proposal because it would not reduce weapons stockpiles, and he claimed that existing supplies of fissionable materials would suffice to manufacture "many hundreds or even thousands of new nuclear warheads."3

The cutoff and transfer were included in the Johnson program, and the United States gave renewed emphasis to them in the 1964 negotiations. On February 6 

Mr. Foster reaffirmed the cutoff and transfer "as a contribution to the objective of restricting dissemination of nuclear weapons. 4 One week later he stressed the flexibility of the American approach and stated that the United States was prepared either to halt production at once or over a period of time. He also said that the 

United States was prepared to consider any reasonable Soviet counterproposals on the amount of the transfers. 

Inspection of declared facilities could be carried out by the IAEA, but insurance against clandestine production at undeclared facilities could be obtained by reciprocal inspection. Mr. Foster believed it possible to devise an inspection system that "would not be onerous. On June 

25 the United States submitted a working paper on inspection of a cutoff.6 115

Ambassador Timberlake again discussed the cutoff in the ENDC on August 13, when he pointed out that it could open the door to reducing fissionable materials stocks. 

He said that it would "definitely decrease the pressure other nations may now feel to produce or acquire nuclear weapons. With the transfer, it would also "restrict proliferation by limiting presently-available amounts of fissionable materials. The United States would consider the possible accession of non-nuclear states to a cutoff agreement. 1 It

The Soviet representative, who had previously refrained from commenting on the cutoff and transfer in the 1964 negotiations, criticized the proposals on the following grounds: (1) they required "control without disarmament, (2) verification would affect the security of states by disclosing the location of production facilities and making it possible to ascertain the size of weapons stockpiles, and (3) the withdrawal provisions were unsatisfactory. For these reasons, he doubted that the problem could be solved outside the framework of general and complete disarmament.2

While it has not been possible to agree on a verified production cutoff, unilateral steps have been taken to decrease production of fissionable materials for weapons purposes. In his State of the Union message to Congress (January 8, 1964), President Johnson announced that the

United States was cutting back production of enriched uranium by 25 percent and shutting down four plutonium piles.3 On April 20 he announced that a further reduction in the production of enriched uranium. Combined with the previous cutback, this would reduce production by 40 percent over a four-year period. 4 On the same date

Premier Khrushchev stated that the Soviet Union was discontinuing construction of two large reactors for plutonium production and would substantially reduce its uranium- 235 production for weapons use during the next several years.5

The British representative on the ENDC stated that the United Kingdom was pursuing a similar policy. 

The ENDC noted these statements with "great satisfaction. "7

IAEA safeguards 

The International Atomic Energy Agency was proposed by President Eisenhower in 1953. In making this proposal, the President noted that "the knowledge now possessed by several nations will eventually be shared by others." 

The Agency was intended to make available for peaceful purposes materials contributed by the nuclear powers from their stockpiles. 1

The Statute of the IAEA (1956) provided for safeguards used in Agency projects "or other arrangements where the Agency is requested by the parties concerned to apply safeguards. #12

The United States, the Soviet Union, and many other states belong to the IAEA. Communist China is not a member.

The IAEA adopted safeguards for reactors of less than 100,000 watts in 1961, 3 and the safeguards system was extended to larger reactors in 1964.4 This developing safeguards system has been taken into account in American disarmament policy. A stage I provision of the U.S. program of September 25, 1961, stated that fissionable materials transferred between countries for peaceful purposes should be subject to "appropriate safeguards to be developed in agreement with the IAEA."5 The U.S. treaty outline of April 18, 1962, contained a similar provision.6

As we have seen, the United States has proposed IAEA inspection of declared production facilities in connection with the fissionable materials cutoff. 11 The United States has also advocated IAEA safeguards on peaceful activities as a separable arms control measure to prevent diffusion. In his message of January 21, 1964, to the ENDC, President Johnson proposed that all transfers of nuclear materials for peaceful purposes take place under effective international safeguards" and that the nuclear powers increasingly accept for their peaceful activities "the same inspection they recommend for other states."7

Nuclear-free zones

Another way to limit the spread of nuclear weapons would be to prohibit production or stockpiling in specific geographic areas. This has already been done in the case of Antarctica. 1 The United States has stated that such zones are desirable if they satisfy the following criteria: the initiative should come from the area concerned; (2) the zone should include all states in the area, particularly any whose non-participation would make the zone ineffective; (3) there should be appropriate provisions for verification; and (4) nuclear weapons do not form part of existing security arrangements in the area. 

The United States has opposed proposals for nuclear-free zones in areas where nuclear weapons are required for security and the establishment of such zones would consequently upset the military balance.

Nuclear-free zones have been proposed for many regions: Africa, the Balkans, the Balkan-Adriatic area,

Central Europe, the Far East and Pacific, the Mediterranean, the Middle East, and Scandinavia. The three zones which have attracted the most attention are discussed below.

Perhaps the best known of these proposals is the Rapacki plan for a nuclear-free zone in Central Europe. 

The introduction of this plan followed a series of developments with respect to disarmament, German reunification, and European security. At the Geneva summit conference of 

1955, Prime Minister Eden proposed an agreement limiting forces and armaments in Germany and neighboring countries, and he also suggested the possibility of a demilitarized area between East and West.2 In their agreed directivė, the four Heads of Government directed their Foreign Ministers to consider various proposals for European security and German reunification, including the establishment of a zone "between East and West."3 

On October 27, 1955, at the Geneva meeting of Foreign

Ministers, the Western powers proposed the limitation of forces and armaments in a zone on both sides of the frontier between a reunified Germany and her Eastern neighbors. 

1Documents on Disarmament, 1945-1959, vol. II, pp. 1550-1556. 

21bid., bol. I, p. 480. These proposals should not be confused with a simultaneous British suggestion for joint inspection of the "forces now confronting each other in Europe" (ibid., pp. 488-489). 

3Ibid., pp. 492-494.

- 5 The establishment of this zone was expressly linked with the Eden plan for the reunification of Germany.1

Foreign Minister Molotov, though interested in Eden's zonal suggestion, was not willing to accept his plan for German reunification. He took the position that the Western proposal did not correspond to the summit directive or to "what everybody knows to be the actual state of affairs." In his view, the zone should cover the German 

Federal Republic, the German Democratic Republic, and at least some bordering states.2

The Soviet partial disarmament plan of March 27, 1956, submitted to the Disarmament Subcommittee, included a proposal for a zone "including both parts of Germany and of States adjacent to them. Force levels would be limited and nuclear weapons prohibited in the zone, and the agreement would be policed by joint inspection. At the same time, the Soviets proposed withdrawal of atomic weapons from Germany as a separable measure.3 

Adam Rapacki, the Polish Foreign Minister, initiated the plan which bears his name in an address of October 2, 1957, when he proposed a denuclearized zone covering both Germanies and his own country. The GDR and Czechoslovakia subsequently expressed willingness to participate. 

Later, in a memorandum of February 14, 1958, to the United States, Poland spelled out the plan in more detail and outlined ed "broad and effective control" for the proposed zone. The American reply (May 3) recognized that the 

Polish move was inspired by a desire for peace but stated that it was "too limited in scope to reduce the danger of nuclear war or provide a dependable basis for the security of Europe," since it did not affect the "central sources" of nuclear power and "would perpetuate the basic cause of tension in Europe by accepting the continuation of the division of Germany."5 lIbid., pp. 531-532. Eden had originally proposed his German reunification plan at the Berlin

Foreign Ministers Meeting (1954). 

21bid., p. 607. 

3Ibid., vol. II, pp. 891-892. 

Ibid., pp. 944-948. 5Ibid., pp. 1023-1025

At a press conference of November 4, Foreign

Minister Rapacki introduced certain changes in his plan. 

He now suggested that it could be carried out in two stages. In the first stage, nuclear weapons production would be prohibited in the area, and armies which did not already possess nuclear weapons would be forbidden to acquire them. The second stage would be preceded by talks on force reductions. 1

The most recent version of the Rapacki plan, submitted to the ENDC Committee on the Whole on March 28, 1962, followed the general lines laid down by Rapacki at his 1958 press conference. Nuclear weapons and delivery vehicles would be frozen and their production prohibited in the first stage, and stocks eliminated in the second stage. Conventional forces and armaments would also be reduced in the second stage. A special control body would administer a system of "strict international control and inspection on the ground and in the air. "2

The Department of State expressed the following objections to the plan:

The principal objections of the United States to the Rapacki Plan, which purports to be a confidence-building measure, have been, and remain: (1) that the measures envisaged do not address themselves to the nuclear weapons located in the Soviet Union, the use of which against Western Europe has been repeatedly threatened by Soviet spokesmen; (2) that the plan would therefore result in a serious military imbalance; (3) that consequently, while creating an illusion of progress, it would in reality endanger the peace of the world rather than contribute to mainrather taining it...3

More recently, Poland has introduced a new proposal which appears to be a modified version of the first stage of the Rapacki plan. In a public address of December 28, 1963, Wladyslaw Gomulka, the Polish Communist leader, lIbid., pp. 1217-1219. 

2Ibid., 1962, vol. I, pp. 201-205. 

3Ibid., pp. 277-278.

11 proposed a freeze of nuclear weapons in Central Europe, with an "appropriate inspection system. Poland later circulated a memorandum to other governments outlining the Gomulka plan in more detail. The memorandum included a provision stating that participating nations would "undertake obligations not to introduce or import, not to transfer to other parties in the area or to accept from other parties in the area...nuclear or thermonuclear weapons."2

The African states do not possess nuclear weapons, and they have made various proposals to make their continent a nuclear-free zone. On December 5, 1960, seven 

African states introduced a draft resolution in the First Committee of the General Assembly requesting all states to refrain from nuclear or "ballistic" weapons tests in Africa and inviting all states to "regard and respect the African continent as a nuclear-free zone. "3 

This resolution never came to a vote.

The next General Assembly, however, approved a resolution calling on UN members (a) To refrain from carrying out or continuing to carry out in Africa nuclear tests in any form; (b) To refrain from using the territory, territorial waters or air space of Africa for testing, storing or transporting nuclear weapons; (c) To consider and respect the continent of Africa as a denuclearized zone. This resolution was adopted by a vote of 55 to 0, with 44 abstentions. The Soviet Union supported the resolution and the United States, the United Kingdom, France, and Italy abstained. The African states themselves split, 14 voting in favor and 11 abstaining. 5

It is the position of my Government that once the African States themselves have arrived at regional agreements or regional arrangements, it would be appropriate for the United Nations to request that all of its members observe and co-operate in the fulfilment of such arrangements.

On May 25, 1963, the Addis Ababa Summit Conference of Independent African States decided to "affirm and respect the principle of declaring Africa a Denuclearized Zone."2 

More recently, the Cairo summit conference of the Organization of African Unity (July 17-21, 1964) 

adopted a resolution on the denuclearization of Africa. 

In this resolution, the OAU leaders not only reaffirmed the Addis Ababa and the General Assembly resolutions, but also declared their readiness to conclude a nonacquisition treaty and called on all peace-loving nations to adhere to the proposed treaty. The African states have requested that this resolution be placed on the agenda of the 19th General Assembly. 3

principle and suggested that Latin America could be one of them.1 The Cuban crisis, as he later told the First

The Brazilian draft resolution recommended that the Latin American countries negotiate arrangements by which they would: a) Agree not to manufacture, receive, store or test nuclear weapons or carrying devices; (b) Agree to dispose forthwith of any nuclear weapons or nuclear delivery vehicles which may now be in their territory; (c) Agree to make provision for verification of these arrangements in order to ensure that they are in fact being observed. 3 

The Cuban representative called the Brazilian proposal a "very laudable idea" but interposed certain conditions. 

He insisted on a guarantee by the nuclear powers that they would not use their nuclear weapons against Latin America. 

He wanted the zone extended to include Puerto Rico and the Panama Canal zone, and he demanded the suppression of foreign military bases in the Western Hemisphere, especially the 

American base at Guantanamo.

- 5 Paris agreements (1954) As a result of the Paris agreements of 1954, the Federal Republic of Germany is committed not to manufacture atomic, biological, or chemical weapons on its territory. Previously, there had been an attempt to establish a European Defense Community which would include France, the FRG, and other West European states. The draft EDC treaty required licensing by the EDC Commissariat of the production, import, and export of atomic, biological, and chemical weapons, and it provided that such licenses could not be granted without the unanimous approval of the EDC Council.1

In 1954, the French refused to ratify the EDC treaty and arrangements were worked out to admit the FRG to the 

Western European Union and NATO. The Paris protocols of 

October 23, 1954, contained a statement by Chancellor

Adenauer declaring that the FRG would not manufacture any atomic, biological, or chemical weapons on its territory. 

An Agency for the Control of Armaments, responsible to the Council of the Western European Union, supervises observance of this pledge.2

The Western peace plan submitted to the Geneva meeting of Foreign Ministers (1959) referred to the FRG pledge and proposed arrangements "to secure similar measures of renunciation in the remainder of Germany and in other 

European countries to the East."3 

Postwar settlements

Certain nations are already bound by treaty obligations prohibiting them from acquiring nuclear weapons. The 1947 treaties of peace with Italy, 4 

Bulgaria,5 Finland, 6 Hungary,7 and Rumania8 forbid these nations to possess, construct, or experiment with any atomic weapon, guided missiles, or apparatus connected with their discharge.

Similarly, the Austrian state treaty (1954) states that Austria shall not possess, construct or experiment with - a) Any atomic weapon, b) any other major weapon adaptable now or in the future to mass destruction and defined as such by the appropriate organ of the United Nations, c) any self-propelled or guided missile or torpedoes, or apparatus connected with their discharge or control..."1

The Japanese peace treaty of 1951, however, does not contain any nuclear prohibitions. At the San Francisco conference of that year, Foreign Minister Gromyko attempted to introduce certain amendments to the American draft treaty, including a new article providing that "Japan shall not possess, construct, or experiment with: (I) any atomic weapon and other means of mass destruction of human life, including bacteriological and chemical weapons; (II) any self-propelled or guided missiles or appratus connected with their discharge..."2 Secretary of State

Acheson, who was presiding, ruled the proposed amendments out of order and the majority upheld his ruling.3 The report of the Senate Foreign Relations Committee on the treaty noted the absence of any restrictions on atomic research and stated: ...On questioning by the committee, Ambassador Dulles indicated there was no intention to place any restriction whatsoever on Japan in this respect. Some committee members expressed the view that any restriction would be impractical if not unwise as an interference with Japanese sovereignty. 

Obviously, however, the United States would be very much concerned if such research turned in the direction of weapons of destruction. 4

At the Geneva Foreign Ministers meeting of 1959, Foreign 

Minister Gromyko introduced a draft treaty which included an article forbidding Germany to "possess, produce, acquire, or experiment with (a) any form of nuclear armament or other means of mass destruction, including biological or chemical weapons; (b) any form of rockets or guided missiles or any apparatus or installations connected with the launching or control of such weapons... "1 As we have seen, the Western peace plan of 1959 provided for the extension to East Germany and other Eastern European countries of the FRG pledge not to manufacture nuclear weapons.2 

Swedish resolution the Sweden was sympathetic to nuclear-free zones, Irish resolution, the test ban, and other efforts to halt the spread of nuclear weapons.

At the 16th General 

Assembly her Foreign Minister, Osten Unden, suggested that the non-nuclear countries might take the initiative themselves and form a non-nuclear "club."3

The Swedish resolution, approved by the General 

Assembly on December 4, 1961, requested the UN Secretary General to make an inquiry "into the conditions under which countries not possessing nuclear weapons might be willing to enter into specific undertakings to refrain from manufacturing or otherwise acquiring such weapons and to refuse to receive, in the future, nuclear weapons in their territories on behalf of any other country. 

The resolution was adopted by a vote of 57 to 12, with 32 abstentions. The United States, 10 other NATO countries, and Spain opposed the resolution. All the Commumist members of the UN voted in favor of it. The United States opposed the resolution because it would prejudice existing defensive arrangements. As the 

U.S. representative told the First Committee: 

1Senate Foreign Relations Committee, Documents on Germany, 1944-1961 (87th Cong., 1st sess.), pp.482. 

2see above, p23 

Documents on Disarmament,, 1961, pp. 

1961, pp. 632-634. 

Ibid., p. 693.

- ...the Swedish proposal seeks to shift the emphasis in this matter entirely to non-nuclear Powers receiving nuclear weapons on their territory in behalf of any other country, and thus to prejudice existing defensive arrangements. 

We believe the answer to this aspect of the inquiry called for by this draft resolution is so obvious as to render the inquiry academic. 

Quite clearly, the conditions which created the need for defensive arrangements will have to be removed before these defensive arrangements can be terminated. Until general disarmament is achieved or until political and military threats cease to exist once and for all, the United States must continue to give its allies the military support which they request and which they consider necessary for collective selfdefense.

It seems to us that this draft resolution, in its reference to the disposition of nuclear weapons, calls into question the right of free nations to join together in collective selfdefense, including the right of self-defence with nuclear weapons if need be.1

In his reply to the Secretary-General's inquiry, Acting Secretary of State Ball reaffirmed American opposition to the "development of national nuclear weapons capability by any additional nation." He added, however, that the Western defense system included both conventional and nuclear weapons and that the latter were "a necessary deterrent to a potential aggressor who is armed with such weapons and openly threatens the free world." The United

States believed that general and complete disarmament under effective international control was the only sure way to remove nuclear weapons. lIbid., pp. 691-692.

-- 
--  While it could not speak for other states, it did not think that nations would be willing to surrender vital elements of their security until they were sure that their security was "adequately guaranteed by effective disarmament and peacekeeping measures." Finally, the Swedish resolution had been passed before the ENDC was established, and the United States believed that the ENDC was the appropriate forum to discuss it in.1

Foreign Minister Unden informed the Secretary-General that Sweden was willing to join a nuclear-free zone comprising the non-nuclear states in Central and Northern Europe. The Swedes would reserve their position if such a zone could not be set up by the end of 1963. They thought the agreement for the proposed zone should be limited to five years duration.

At the same time, they believed that the nuclear powers should undertake the following commitments with respect to the zone: "not to export nuclear weapons to the countries concerned, not to supply technical information or equipment for the production of nuclear weapons and, possibly after an appropriate transitional period, not to stockpile nuclear weapons on the territories of such countries and not to equip their armed forces stationed in these countries with nuclear weapons. 11 

The Swedes also expected the nuclear powers to maintain the nuclear status quo by concluding a test-ban agreement, and they made the zonal proposal contingent on this. Finally, they suggested that the nuclear powers might undertake not to make a nuclear attack on any of the countries in the zone. They did not, however, make 2 this an indispensable condition for the zone. 

Many nonaligned countries have continued to take an interest in the goals of the Swedish resolution. As we have seen, the OAU summit conference declared African readiness to conclude a non-acquisition treaty and called on all peace-loving states to adhere to the proposed pact.3

The heads of state or government also declared "their own readiness not to produce, acquire, or test any nuclear weapons" and called on all countries to "enter into a similar undertaking and to take the necessary steps to prevent their territories, ports, and airfields from being used by nuclear powers for the deployment or disposition of nuclear weapons. 

This undertaking should be the subject of a treaty to be concluded at an international conference convened under UN auspices and open to accession by all states."l 

Tests of nuclear weapons

So far every country which has achieved a national nuclear capability has found it necessary to test nuclear weapons. Consequently, the prohibition of tests would serve to inhibit the proliferation of weapons to countries not already possessing them. This was one of the main 

American motives in seeking a test-ban agreement. As 

President Kennedy told his news conference on February 21, 1963, proliferation involved the risk of the acquisition of nuclear weapons by irresponsible governments and increased the danger of accidental nuclear conflict. Although a test-ban agreement would not actually guarantee an end to proliferation, he did not think the Soviet Union would agree to a test ban unless it shared the American view that a test ban "might be" a weight in the scale against proliferation.2

The test-ban negotiations for the period preceding September 1962 have been reviewed in detail in two white papers.3 Negotiations since that time are summarized below. 

On August 27, 1962, the United States and the United

Kingdom submitted two alternative draft treaties at Geneva. 

1Cairo radio broadcast, October 11, 1964. 

2Documents on Disarmament, 1963, pp. 59-60. 

3Geneva Conference on the Discontinuance of

Nuclear Weapon Tests: History and Analysis of 

Negotiations (Department of State pub. 7258,1961): and International Negotiations on Ending Nuclear

Weapon Tests, September 1961-September 1962 

(ACDA pub. 9, 1962).

-  29 Ce 34 The first, a draft treaty banning tests in all environments, provided for control by an international commission which would supervise national control posts and conduct a limited but unspecified number of on-site inspections each year of unidentified seismic events.1 The United 

States and the United Kingdom preferred this comprehensive treaty, but, in case the Soviet Union continued to refuse to accept any form of international control, they offered as an alternative the second treaty, which banned tests only in the atmosphere, in outer space, and underwater. 

This limited treaty did not require any international control or inspection, since the Western powers believed that observance could be monitored by national detection systems.2 

While the United States and the United Kingdom considered international control required for the underground environment alone, the Soviet Union claimed that underground tests could also be detected and identified by national systems and that no on-site inspections were necessary. First Deputy Foreign Minister Kuznetsov immed iately rejected the comprehensive treaty as merely a revamped form of previous Anglo-American proposals. He showed more interest in the partial treaty but criticized it for omitting underground tests. He proposed instead a definitive agreement prohibiting tests in the atmosphere, in outer space, and underwater, accompanied by an undertaking on underground testing which "would remain in force until it was replaced by a permanent solution."3 

The Soviet proposal for an undertaking to stop underground testing was not acceptable to the United States, which recalled the "moratorium" of 1958-1961, terminated by the Soviet resumption of testing. As President Kennedy put it, "we must have workable international agreements; gentlemen's agreements and moratoria do not provide the it 4 type of guarantees that are necessary. 

1Documents on Disarmament, 1962, vol. II, pp. 792-804. 

21bid., pp. 804-807. 3Ibid., pp. 820-829. 

4Ibid., p. 830.

Negotiations remained deadlocked until the Cuban crisis was settled. The Soviet Union then espoused a suggestion by American and Soviet scientists at the unofficial Pugwash Conference for the use of automatic seismic stations to supplement national posts. This idea was developed in an article by Soviet seismologists in Izvestiya (November 11), where it was claimed that, in spite of difficulties in distinguishing earthquakes from explosions, reinforcement of national systems with automatic stations would "guarantee complete reliability of detection of underground tests without any on-site inspection."2

The Soviet delegation immediately began to discuss the automatic stations at Geneva. On December 10 Ambassador Tsarapkin said that the USSR would accept one automatic station in each of three seismic regions, and he suggested specific sites. He also said that international personnel would be permitted to deliver the apparatus to the stations and retrieve records from them, travelling in Soviet planes under appropriate safeguards. 3 He made it clear, however, that the Soviets remained adamantly opposed to on-site inspections. They still regarded national detection systems as adequate and suggested the automatic stations only as a political concession to the 

Western powers. 

Ambassador Dean questioned the locations Tsarapkin had suggested but proposed a scientific discussion. He said that the Soviet proposal could be significant if the USSR accepted on-site inspections.5 Ambassador Tsarapkin, however, was not willing to discuss technical details until agreement was reached in principle.6 

During the General Assembly, Ambassador Dean had informally discussed the inspection problem with Kuznetsov. 

According to the American version of these talks, Ambassador Dean indicated that the United States would accept an annual quota of 8-10 on-site inspections. However, in a letter of December 19 to President Kennedy, Premier

Khrushchev stated that Dean had offered Kuznetsov an annual quota of 2-4 on-site inspections. The Soviet Union was now willing to accept an annual quota of 2-3 inspections, along with the 3 automatic seismic stations it had previously proposed. lIbid., p. 830. 

21bid., pp. 863-865 3Ibid pp. 1184-1185. 

4ENDC/SC.1/PV. 48, pp. 12, 15. 

SENDC/SC.I/PV. 49, pp. 8-10.

ENDC/SC.I/PV.50, p. 4

The new proposal was made as a political concession and did not alter the previous Soviet position that existing national systems were capable of detecting underground tests without inspection.1

In reply, President Kennedy welcomed Soviet acceptance of on-site inspection but denied the Soviet version of the Dean-Kuznetsov talks. Although he questioned the locations proposed for the automatic stations, he did not believe that any of the problems were insoluble, and he proposed discussions getween a Soviet representative and 

ACDA Director Foster. 

He 

Premier Khrushchev agreed to the proposed talks. was flexible on the locations of the automatic stations but not on the number of on-site inspections, for he still maintained that the Soviet proposal virtually coincided with previous American proposals.3

In the informal talks (January 14-31, 1963), in which the United Kingdom joined, both sides agreed that a system of nationally owned and operated seismic stations would report to an international data-collection center. The 

United States no longer demanded international supervision of manned stations. Both sides also agreed that national systems would be supplemented by automatic stations. It was impossible to reach agreement, however, because the Soviet delegation insisted on an annual quota of only 2-3 inspections and refused to accept more than 3 automatic stations. Finally, the talks were ended at Soviet request and the test-ban question was again referred to the ENDO. 

In the ENDC, the United States offered to agree on seven automatic stations and a like number of on-site inspections. 4. The United States and the United Kingdom discussed the modalities of inspection and summed up their views in a memorandum of April 1, 1963. This memorandum outlined a system of reciprocal inspection, described criteria and procedures, and discussed ways of setting up the automatic stations.5 

1Documents on Disarmament, 1962, vol. II, pp. 1239–1242. 

21bid., pp. 1277-1279. 3Ibid., 1963, pp. 1-4. 

4Ibid., p. 76. 

5Ibid., pp. 141-145.

The Soviet Union continued to adhere to the position it had taken in the informal talks. First Deputy Foreign 

Minister Kuznetsov emphatically declared that the number of on-site inspections and automatic stations was not a subject for bargaining. He insisted that the question of numbers of inspections and stations must be settled before dealing with inspection modalities.1

Paradoxically, the negotiations thus became deadlocked when the two sides had finally reached basic agreement on the general nature of the control system. The 

United States and the United Kingdom, through diplomatic channels, obtained Soviet agreement to tripartite talks in Moscow. President Kennedy announced the talks in his

American University speech of June 10 and stated that the 

United States would not conduct atmospheric tests as long as other states did not do so. 2 The Kennedy speech was followed by a change in Soviet policy toward a limited treaty. On July 2 Premier 

Khrushchev stated that the West had not properly evaluated his December proposals and accused the West of demanding excessive inspection for espionage purposes. Since these 

Western demands, in his view, impeded conclusion of a comprehensive treaty, the Soviet Union was now willing to conclude a treaty limited to the atmosphere, outer space, and under water, where no inspections were needed.3 

The Moscow negotiations were successful, and a limited treaty was signed August 5, 1963.4 The vast majority of nations have adhered to it. Among those which refused to sign the treaty were France, which was developing a national nuclear capability, and Communist China, which wished to achieve nuclear status. 

Nearly five years before the treaty was signed, Ambassador Moch had warned the United Nations that France would develop and test nuclear weapons unless the three nuclear powers agreed to stop productions and reduce stockpiles. 

1Ibid., pp. 60-72. 

21bid. pp. 220-221. 

3Ibid., pp. 244-246. 

4Ibid., pp. 291-293

--  He then stated that France would not be bound by any agreement reached by the nuclear powers to stop testing unless they also took these other steps. 

After the treaty was initialed, President De Gaulle stated that it in no way altered the nuclear threat. 

In his view, it had "only limited practical importance." 

Although he welcomed the treaty, he would not accede to it. He added, however, that France would abandon her efforts to secure nuclear weapons if the Americans and Soviets ever agreed on the "controlled destruction and banning of their nuclear means."2

The Chinese Communists denounced the treaty as a fraud. They proposed a world summit conference to discuss measures to outlaw the manufacture, possession, use, and testing of nuclear weapons. Their step by step" program included the following measures: (1) dismantling all foreign bases and withdrawing all nuclear weapons and delivery vehicles from foreign countries; (2) nuclearfree zones in the Asian-Pacific area (including the United States, the Soviet Union, China, and Japan), Central 

Europe, Africa, and Latin America; (3) refraining from importing or exporting nuclear weapons or data for their manufacture; and (4) a comprehensive test ban.3 Τη 

October 1964, after exploding their first nuclear device, the Chinese Communists proposed a world summit disarmament conference to discuss the prohibition and destruction of nuclear weapons and, as a first step, to agree not to use nuclear weapons. On November 22 they repeated this proposal and attacked proposals for a comprehensive test ban and the destruction of nuclear delivery vehicles. 

Since the signature of the limited test-ban treaty, the United States, the United Kingdom, and the Soviet Union have expressed themselves in favor of a comprehensive test ban on a number of occasions. The Soviet Union has withdrawn the Khrushchev offer of 2-3 inspections and restated its claim that no inspections are needed." The 

United States still maintains that some inspections are necessary. In the recent ENDC discussions, there was no change in the position of either side.

Use of nuclear weapons

Those states that support the Ethiopian proposal for to outlaw the use of nuclear weapons in war generally do so on moral and humanitarian grounds, but some also argue that adoption of the proposal would increase international confidence and thus reduce the incentive for additional nations to develop nuclear weapons. The United States opposes the Ethiopian proposal.

Over American opposition, the General Assembly on November 24, 1961, approved a resolution cosponsored by Ethiopia and seven other African and Asian states. This resolution declared that the use of nuclear and thermonuclear weapons was a "direct violation" of the UN 

Charter and requested the Secretary-General to consult member states on the possibility of convening a special conference for signing a convention to prohibit the use of nuclear and thermonuclear weapons. 1

In 1962 and 1963 the Secretary-General requested the views of the member states. The majority of the replies favored the proposed conference, but the United States and many other countries opposed it.2 In his reply, Secretary of State Rusk reaffirmed American adherence to the goal of nuclear disarmament. While the United States deplored the necessity to arm with weapons of mass destruction," he declared that a ban on their use, "unaccompanied by measures leading to the attainment of general and complete disarmament in a peaceful world," could not "provide any real or lasting protection to potential victims of nuclear attack. Indeed, a convention which would be merely an expression of the desire to eliminate nuclear weapons or prevent their spread would not in itself establish the conditions of confidence necessary for universal renunciation of such weapons. Accordingly, the United States saw no use in holding the proposed conference. 11
