SUMMARY OF DEVELOPMENTS IN THE EIGHTEEN NATION COMMITTEE ON DISARMAMENT (1964) 

In 1964, the Eighteen Nation Committee on Disarmament (ENDC) continued to be the center for international negotiations on arms control and disarmament. Although there were no new agreements, President Johnson observed that the ENDC received more concrete proposals for safeguarded and realistic disarmament in 1964 than had been made in any other year since World War II. The Soviet Union, however, took a more negative view of the work of the ENDC and declared that its accomplishments equaled "zero." 

The ENDC held two sessions in 1964: from January 21 

to April 28, and from June 9 to September 17. It consisted of the original 18 members: Brazil, Bulgaria, Burma, Canada, Czechoslovakia, Ethiopia, France, India, Italy. 

Mexico, Nigeria, Poland, Rumania, Sweden, the USSR, the

United Arab Republic, the United Kingdom, and the United States. France, however, has never participated because she prefers direct negotiations among the nuclear powers. 

William C. Foster, Director of the Arms Control and

Disarmament Agency (ACDA), again led the U.S. delegation. 

In his absence, the United States was represented by 

Adrian S. Fisher, Deputy Director of ACDA, or Ambassador 

Clare H. Timberlake.

The principal Committee discussions again took place in the plenary meetings, which were held on Tuesdays and 

Thursdays. For the most part, the Tuesday plenaries were devoted to the question of general and complete disarmament and the Thursday plenaries to collateral measures. 

In June, the Committee agreed to an agenda for the discussion of collateral measures. The U.S. and Soviet representatives continued to serve as permanent Co-Chairmen and held a number of meetings in this capacity. And on the closing day of the second session (September 17) the

Committee adopted a report to the U.N. Disarmament Commission and the General Assembly on the 1964 negotiations. 

The report stated that "the questions before the Committee were discussed in a thorough and concrete manner" and that, although no specific agreements had been reached, the Committee hoped these "useful discussions" would "facilitate agreement in the further work of the Committee. On that day also the Committee adjourned until after termination of consideration of the question of disarmament at the 

19th session of the General Assembly.

Negotiations for General and Complete Disarmament

In the discussion of general and complete disarmament the ENDC returned to the problem of reducing and eliminating nuclear delivery vehicles. In its original draft treaty (March 1962), the Soviet Union had proposed that all nuclear delivery vehicles be eliminated in Stage I. 

In September 1962 Foreign Minister Gromyko announced that the Soviet Union, owing to Western objections, was prepared to allow the United States and the Soviet Union to retain "a strictly limited and agreed number of global interc on tinental missiles, anti-missile missiles, and antiaircraft missiles of the ground-to-air type" to the end of Stage II. In September 1963 he said that the two countries could keep this "nuclear umbrella" to the end of Stage III.

At the beginning of the 1964 negotiations, the Soviet delegation amended its draft treaty to reflect these changes.

The Soviet Union had thus compromised, it argued, and the West should feel obliged to accept its proposal "in principle" as the basis for negotiation. In June, it recommended establishment of a working group to study the problem, but here too, although for a while it showed some flexibility, it finally made clear its position that "the "nuclear umbrella" would have to be accepted "in principle" as the basis for the group's work. 

The Western position on the question was that reductions of nuclear delivery vehicles should be accompanied by reductions in other armaments. There should be "cuts across the board," it was sometimes expressed. Proportionate reductions in all types of armaments through all three stages of the disarmament process would serve to maintain the rough military balance between the two sides and thus be in accord with the Joint Statement of Agreed Principles. The balance might be upset, the West stated, if only certain categories of armaments were eliminated, because some countries more than others relied for their defense on certain categories of armaments. Since the 

Western countries relied more than the Soviet Union on nuclear delivery vehicles, their elimination, if unaccompanied by elimination of other types of armaments, would be greatly to the advantage of the Soviet Union. The 

West by no means opposed the eventual elimination of nuclear delivery vehicles. Although it favored the retention of some of them throughout the disarmament process, it proposed their elimination "at the earliest practicable time consistent with the Joint Statement of Agreed Principles."

The Western powers wished to discuss the problem of eliminating and reducing nuclear delivery vehicles, and they welcomed the Soviet recommendation for establishing a working group, but they refused to accept the Gromyko proposal as the only basis for the group's work. They argued that a study of the technical and practical issues involved in the reduction and elimination of nuclear delivery vehicles would be useful, but only if all relevant proposals, including the U.S. and Soviet plans, were considered. They also pointed out that implementation of the Soviet proposal would require elimination of about 97 to 98 percent of all nuclear delivery vehicles in 18 months and they believed that this would result in a "radi- cal shift" in the present rough military balance that would favor the Soviet Union. They noted too that the proposal was linked with the withdrawal of troops from foreign countries and the dissolution of foreign bases in Stage I of the Soviet disarmament plan and would thus be advantageous to the Soviet Union because its forces and conventional armaments were geographically close to Western Europe. And they declared that the proposal lacked sufficient provision for verification of remaining vehicles and failed to guard against clandestine stockpiles or production at undeclared locations. 

There were numerous efforts by the Eight (those ENDC members not belonging to NATO or the Warsaw Pact) to effect a compromise between the West and the Soviet Union on the nuclear delivery vehicles problem. In March, for example, India urged that the West accept the "nuclear umbrella" in principle as the basis for negotiations. 

India hoped that this could lead to a detailed study of the proposal in order to narrow the gap between East and 

West on the issue.

Regarding the working group, the Eight sought terms of reference that would combine the U.S. insistence that the group be free to discuss all nuclear delivery vehicles proposals with the Soviet demand for nuclear delivery vehicles reduction to a minimum level in Stage I. India, Nigeria, and the UAR each proposed compromise terms of reference. Sweden suggested that the work plan for dealing with the problem be turned upside down: 1.e., instead of dealing first with the problems of Stage I, the working group should begin with the problems of Stage III--with the type of "nuclear shield" that should exist at the end of the disarmament process. Only later should it attack

Stage II. In September

They also pointed out that implementation of the Soviet proposal would require elimination of about 97 to 98 percent of all nuclear delivery vehicles in 18 months and they believed that this would result in a "radical shift" in the present rough military balance that would favor the Soviet Union. They noted too that the proposal was linked with the withdrawal of troops from foreign countries and the dissolution of foreign bases in Stage I of the Soviet disarmament plan and would thus be advantageous to the Soviet Union because its forces and conventional armaments were geographically close to Western Europe. And they declared that the proposal lacked sufficient provision for verification of remaining vehicles and failed to guard against clandestine stockpiles or production at undeclared locations. 

There were numerous efforts by the Eight (those ENDC members not belonging to NATO or the Warsaw Pact) to effect a compromise between the West and the Soviet Union on the nuclear delivery vehicles problem. In March, for example, India urged that the West accept the "nuclear umbrella" in principle as the basis for negotiations. 

India hoped that this could lead to a detailed study of the proposal in order to narrow the gap between East and 

West on the issue.

Regarding the working group, the Eight sought terms of reference that would combine the U.S. insistence that the group be free to discuss all nuclear delivery vehicles proposals with the Soviet demand for nuclear delivery vehicles reduction to a minimum level in Stage I. India, Nigeria, and the UAR each proposed compromise terms of reference. Sweden suggested that the work plan for dealing with the problem be turned upside down: 1.e., instead of dealing first with the problems of Stage I, the working group should begin with the problems of Stage III--with the type of "nuclear shield" that should exist at the end of the disarmament process. Only later should it attack

the problem of how the "shield" should be reached. Sweden also stated that the working group should be free to discuss the "mix" of elements for the "shield," and that it might use modern technical capabilities and become the first real international exercise in strategic programming. 

In spite of these various efforts to reach agreement on the basis for negotiating the reduction and elimination of nuclear delivery vehicles, no such agreement was reached in 1964.

Negotiations on Collateral Measures The principal U.S. collateral proposals discussed at the 

1964 sessions were the strategic vehicles freeze, a fisionable materials cutoff, the destruction of U.S. B-47's and Soviet TU-16's, and various measures to halt nuclear dissemination. The Soviet Union concentrated on reductions in military expenditures, the destruction of all bombers, and non-dissemination. 

On January 21, at the opening of the 1964 ENDC sessions, President Johnson proposed five collateral measures: (1) prohibition of the threat or use of force, directly or indirectly--whether by aggression, subversion, or the clandestine supply of arms--to change boundaries or demarcation lines; to interfere with access to territory; or to extend control or administration over territory by displacing established authorities; (2) a verified freeze of the number and characteristics of strategic nuclear offensive and defensive vehicles; (3) a verified agreement on the cessation of the production of fissionable materials for weapons use; (4) creation of a system of observation posts to further reduce the danger of war by accident, miscalculation, or surprise attack; and (5) agreements to halt the spread of nuclear weapons. 

On January 28, the Soviet Union placed before the 

Committee a memorandum outlining nine collateral measures: (1) withdrawal of foreign troops from the territory of other countries, (2) reduction of armed forces, (3) reduction of military budgets, (4) a non-aggression pact between the countries of NATO and the Warsaw Pact, (5) establishment of denuclearized zones, (6) prevention of the spread of nuclear weapons, (7) a network of observation posts, (8) elimination of bomber aircraft, and (9) prohibition of underground nuclear tests.

All of these Soviet measures, except the one for bomber elimination, had been proposed before. Although this was the first time the elimination of bombers had been proposed formally, the United States had previously suggested in private talks with the Soviet Union the destruction of B-47 and TU-16 bombers. Later in the

Conference, the United States publicly discussed this proposal.

Although no agreements were reached, there were significant Committee developments regarding these and other collateral measures. The Committee noted the announced Soviet and U.S. reductions in military expenditures and the Soviet, U.K., and U.S. cutbacks in production of fissionable materials. The United States advocated exploration of the possibility of applying 

International Atomic Energy Agency (IAEA) safeguards to all transfers of fissionable materials, and it added the 

Yankee Power Reactor at Rowe, Massachusetts to the list of U.S. reactors open to IAEA inspection, expressing the hope that this would induce other nations to open their reactors to IAEA inspection and thus serve to strengthen that Agency's safeguards system.

Strategic Vehicles Freeze and Fissionable Materials Cutoff 

The major collateral measure proposed by the United States in 1964 was a freeze of the number and characteristics of strategic nuclear offensive and defensive delivery vehicles. The United States said it would cover five categories of missiles, bombers, and anti-missile missile systems. Production of these vehicles would be stopped, except as required for maintenance and replacement of missiles expended in allowed firings for confidence and training purposes. The freeze would be embodied in a formal treaty, which would contain a withdrawal clause similar to the one in the test-ban treaty, and a provision for a conference to review the treaty periodically or at the request of any party. 

As a companion measure to the freeze, the United

States again proposed a cutoff of production of fissionable materials for weapons use and again expressed its willingness to agree to an immediate, total shut-down of production of fissionable materials, or to a more gradual approach involving a systematic closing of production centers one by one over a period of time.

It

In submitting detailed proposals for verification of the freeze and the cutoff, the United States emphasized that a great deal of effort and millions of dollars had gone into making the required verification for these and other proposals as unintrusive as possible yet at the same time capable of providing sufficient security. pointed out that the plan for verification of the cutoff did not call for inspection of stockpiles, mines, or refineries, and did not require disclosure of information on the storage and deployment of nuclear weapons. It stated that for inspection of some plants, access to their interior would not be necessary. And it said that only programs producing for peaceful purposes would be disclosed. 

It introduced a working paper on verification of the cutoff. 

For verifying the freeze, it explained that there would be continuing inspection of declared production facilities, an annual quota of inspections to check undeclared locations for possible violations, observation of space launchings and allowed missile firings, and observation of the destruction of vehicles and launchers being replaced. It emphasized, however, that the procedures would not involve a check on existing levels, would not disturb the security of sensitive installations, and would be less intrusive than those required for general and complete disarmament.

The Soviet Union refused to accept the freeze and the cutoff on the ground that they would not reduce delivery vehicles or nuclear weapons stocks. In its view, they were not disarmament but control measures designed for espionage. 

Bomber Destruction

In the 1964 ENDC sessions, both the United States and the Soviet Union submitted proposals for the destruction of bomber aircraft. Previously, the United States had informally suggested that the possibility of the mutual destruction of bombers be explored. The Soviet memorandum of January 28 proposed the elimination of all bombers. In March the Soviet Union stated that the elimination could be effected in a short period of time ("for instance, in one year"), and the United States submitted a proposal to destroy equal numbers of U.S. B-47's and 

Soviet TU-16's over a period of two years at the rate of 20 each per month. The United States also announced at the time its willingness to destroy an agreed number of the same type of aircraft then "stored and preserved for emergency mobilization."

11 The United States said that the Soviet proposal raised a "series of complex questions," and the United Kingdom said the immediate destruction of all bombers would be impractical. The United States pointed out that its own proposal was a step toward the Soviet proposal insofar as the destruction of B-47's and TU-16's would be included "within any possible interpretation of the Soviet plan. It also noted that verification would include no more than observation of the destruction of the bombers and that implementation of the proposal would be consistent with the agreed principle that the military balance should not be upset by any disarmament measure. The 

Soviet Union asserted that the proposal was not a real disarmament measure and that it merely reflected U.S. plans to phase out obsolescent aircraft. 

The Spread of Nuclear Weapons Much of the ENDC 1964 discussion of collateral measures was devoted to the problem of the spread of nuclear weapons.

The United States favored a non-dissemination agreement based on the Irish resolution and stated that it would not take any actions inconsistent with that resolution. The Soviet Union also advocated a non-dissemination agreement, but none could be concluded because it insisted that the proposed multilateral nuclear force (MLF) would constitute dissemination of nuclear weapons and would have to be abandoned before it could consent to a non-dissemination agreement. 

The Communist countries characterized the MLF as a plan to give the Federal Republic of Germany (FRG) access to nuclear weapons. They rejected the Western argument that the MLF would actually help to limit the spread of nuclear weapons to individual nations by offering an alternative to the development of national nuclear programs. 

They accused the FRG of wanting to participate in "pulling the trigger" of the MLF and, "by the law of chain reaction, in pulling the trigger of the United States strategic nuclear force." They charged that the proposed arrangements for the MLF would be changed and recalled that the Western European Union had decided in 1958-1959 to allow the FRG to produce anti-tank and anti-aircraft missiles although production of such items had originally been banned by the Paris Agreements of 1954. They repeated previous Soviet charges that FRG industry had experimented with tactical missiles and was ready to produce them. They attacked the West for permitting the FRG to develop a navy which could be equipped with missiles and for allowing the Bundeswehr to have tactical missiles.

The West argued that the MLF would be compatible with a non-dissemination agreement because it would not enhance national nuclear capabilities. It also stated that by providing an alternative to national nuclear capabilities, the MLF would actually increase the possibility of limiting them. The nuclear weapons in the MLF, it pointed out, would be "manned, manned, owned and controlled multilaterally," no single member would be able by itself to fire them, and the decision to fire in wartime would be made by the United States and "an agreed number" of members. No participating nation, moreover, could withdraw its MLF elements and place them under national control. The West reminded the Committee that the MLF had been devised in order to enable NATO members to deal with a number of threats with which they might have to contend, including hundreds of Soviet rockets arrayed against Europe.

During the ENDC discussion India announced that under no circumstances would she use her nuclear capabilities for non-peaceful purposes. The United States declared that this announcement was an immense contribution toward preventing the spread of nuclear weapons, and it expressed the hope that all ENDC members would welcome it.

Reductions in Military Expenditures 

Another collateral measure that received considerable attention in the ENDC was the reduction of military expenditures. Both the United States and the Soviet Union had announced reductions before the ENDC met. On January 28 the Soviets suggested a 10-15 percent cut. In February, Brazil tabled a working paper requesting all governments to reduce their military budgets and recommending that not less than 20 percent of the reductions be credited to a fund for assisting the less developed areas. 

The paper also called for immediate establishment within the ENDC framework of a working group to study the question and to make recommendations to the Committee. Shortly after

Brazil submitted its working paper, the Soviet Union proposed that the ENDC issue an appeal calling upon the states of the world to follow the example of the United States and the Soviet Union by reducing their military expenditures. 

Among the Western countries, Italy agreed that some of the savings resulting from reductions in military expenditures could be used for developing countries, and she supported efforts by the United Kingdom, Canada, and Sweden as well as Brazil to set up a working group to study the expenditures reduction problem. In discussing the Soviet

proposal for a 10-15 percent cut, the Western powers noted that in 1955-1960 the Soviets built up their missile strength while at the same time reducing their military budget. They asked whether the Soviet budget carried military expenditures under non-military headings. And they asked how the reductions could be verified. The 

United States and Canada questioned the value of the appeal proposed by the Soviet Union. 

The Communist countries welcomed Brazilian support for the reduction of military expenditures and said they favored allocation of some of the savings to technical aid, but they opposed establishing a working group unless the West accepted the Soviet proposal in principle. 

Otherwise, the Soviet Union asserted, a technical study group could serve only as a "smoke screen" for warlike preparations.

Among the Eight there was some support for the Soviet and Brazilian proposals, notably from Burma and Nigeria.

But Burma suggested that the Soviet proposal be linked with the U.S. proposal for a freeze of strategic nuclear delivery vehicles and with the Soviet proposal for the elimination of bombers. Mexico favored waiting till the 

19th General Assembly for discussion of the Brazilian proposal. Both Mexico and the UAR seemed to feel that reductions were for the great powers rather than for themselves. India pointed out that it was faced with the threat of aggression and could not reduce military expenditures.

Other Collateral Measures Resolutions approved by the 18th General Assembly requested the ENDC to study the problems of prohibiting the use of nuclear weapons and banning all nuclear tests. 

The resolution on the use of nuclear weapons called for study of the question of convening a conference for the purpose of signing a convention on the prohibition of the use of nuclear and thermonuclear weapons. The Communist countries and some of the Eight supported the resolution. 

Ethiopia and India said a ban would require the support of all states, and Nigeria said the major world powers possessing nuclear weapons would have to participate in the conference. Mexico, however, said the time for convening the conference was not then propitious. It was the 

Mexican view that the question of prohibiting the use of nuclear weapons should be treated within the context of

1 general and complete disarmament, and not as a single measure. The Western countries opposed the resolution, pointing out that the real threat to peace was the use of force for aggressive purposes, regardless of the type of weapons used, and therefore that a ban on the use of nuclear weapons, if not accompanied by their elimination, could not provide any real or lasting assurance against nuclear warfare. They also stated that a mere declaration against using nuclear weapons would be ineffective and even dangerous if it created the impression that aggression could be committed without fear of risking nuclear war.

A more realistic way to reduce the risk of nuclear war, the Western Four asserted, would be to adopt measures to prevent the further dissemination of nuclear weapons. 

One such measure, they noted, would be a comprehensive test ban, which was proposed in another General Assembly resolution. They again declared their support for such a ban, but they reiterated that on-site inspection was still needed in order to identify as well as detect underground nuclear explosions. The United States noted that much of its Vela research program, which cost more than $50,000,000 a year, had been devoted to research on the detection and identification of underground nuclear tests.

Toward the end of the 1964 ENDC sessions, the Eight submitted memoranda summarizing their 1964 proposals and suggestions, urging all states to adhere to the partial test-ban treaty, and advocating a comprehensive test ban.

ENGLISH ORIGINAL: 

RUSSIAN LETTER DATED 31 MARCH 1965 FROM THE PERMANENT REPRESENTATIVE OF THE UNION OF SOVIET SOCIALIST REPUBLICS TO THE UNITED NATIONS, ADDRESSED TO THE SECRETARY-GENERAL REQUESTING A MEETING OF THE DISARMAMENT COMMISSION 

On the instructions of the Government of the Union of Soviet Socialist Republics I hereby request you to convene the United Nations Disarmament Commission.

As is stated in the report transmitted by the Eighteen-Nation Committee on Disarmament to the United Nations Disarmament Commission and to the nineteenth session of the United Nations General Assembly (A/5731, DC/209), protracted discussion on disarmament questions in the Committee did not lead to the attainment of any specific agreement either on questions of general and complete disarmament or on measures aimed at the lessening of international tension. On adjourning its proceedings in September 1964, the Eighteen-Nation Committee decided to resume its meetings "as soon as possible after the termination of the consideration of disarmament at the nineteenth session of the General Assembly, on a date to be decided by the two Co-Chairmen after consultation with the members of the Committee". For known reasons, however, it proved impossible for the Eighteen-Nation Committee's report and the state of the disarmament negotiations as a whole to be discussed at the nineteenth session of the General Assembly. Nevertheless, the course of international events requires the adoption of effective measures in the matter of disarmament and limitation of the arms race.

The The problem of disarmament affects the vital interests of all States. peoples are entitled to expect the United Nations to make a thorough examination of the disquieting state of disarmament negotiations in the Eighteen-Nation Committee and to help in charting specific courses along which progress can be mace in those negotiations. Since no such discussion took place in the United 

Nations General Assembly, the Soviet Government considers it necessary that the 65-06105 /...

DC/English

Page 2 United Nations Disarmament Commission, on which all States ilembers of the United Nations are represented, should be convened. 

The Soviet Government considers it desirable that the United Nations Disarmament Commission should begin work as soon as possible, in the first half of April 1965.

Please circulate this letter as an official document of the United Nations 

Disarmament Commission. 

(Signed) N. FEDORENKO Permanent Representative of the 

USSR to the United Nations

UNITED STATES MISSION TO THE UNITED NATIONS FOR THE PRESS 

Press Release No. 4517 

March 31, 1965

Statement by Ambassador Adlai E. Stevenson, United States 

Representative to the United Nations.

The United States believes that disarmament is of such primary importance that it should have continuous attention. And that is why we have been pressing vigorously to reconvene the 18-Nation Disarmament Committee in Geneva, which in our judgment is the best forum in which to reach agreement on concrete disarmament proposals. 

While we would prefer a negotiating committee to a debating committee, we have no objection to convening the whole membership of the United Nations and affording everyone an opportunity to express his views. So if a majority approves such a meeting, the United 

States would participate constructively and hope that from the ensuing discussion will come useful and constructive ideas which might then be considered in detail by the 18-Nation Disarmament Committee.

January-February 1965 

UNCLASSIFIED

JANUARY-FEBRUARY 1965 I. HIGHLIGHTS 

The principal arms control and disarmament developments for January and February 1965 were an Atomic Energy Commission announcement of planned cutbacks in the production of fissionable materials, an announcement of planned reductions in U.S. military expenditures, and a Soviet underground nuclear test from which some radioactive debris leaked into the atmosphere. 

The Atomic Energy Commission announced plans to reduce the rate of production of enriched uranium beyond the decreases announced in early 1964. The new decreases would become effective in steps beginning in mid-1966 and, when completed in 1969, would leave diffusion plant operating power about 60 percent below the 4,850 megawatt level planned prior to the 1964 cutbacks (February 15).

In his defense message to the Congress, the President stated that estimated U.S. defense expenditures for fiscal year 1965 ($49.3 billion) would be about $2 billion less than those for fiscal 1964 and that estimated defense expenditures for fiscal 1966 would be reduced another $300 million. He also announced plans to phase out B-52B bombers and to realign the Army Reserves and the National Guard (January 18).

The Soviet Union conducted an underground nuclear test which vented (January 15). In reply to U.S. requests for information, the Soviet Union stated that the radioactive debris released by the venting was so insignificant that the possibility of a violation of the partial test-ban treaty was excluded (January 25). In testimony before two 

Congressional committees, ACDA Director Foster said that the test may have constituted a technical violation of the treaty. He went on to give his opinion that "on the basis of what is now known it does not represent a threat to U.S. national security nor by itself does it represent a resumption of testing prohibited by the treaty. He also pointed out, however, that because of the importance of the subject, the matter was under continuing study (January 26 and February 22). ... 11

II. 

CHRONOLOGY January 12, 1965. At the Nuclear Rocket Development 

Station in Nevada, scientists of the Los Alamos Scientific

Laboratory destroy a modified Kiwi reactor in order to gain information on the behavior of nuclear rocket reactors when subject to accidents.

January 15, 1965. The President requests the Congress to authorize appropriations for the U.S. Arms Control and 

Disarmament Agency totaling $55 million for the four fiscal years 1966 through 1969.

At its Semipalatinsk site, the Soviet Union conducts an underground nuclear test with a yield in the intermediate range. 

January 18, 1965. In his defense message to Congress, the President states that estimated U.S. defense expenditures for fiscal year 1965 (#49.3 billion) will be about $2 billion less than those for fiscal 1964 and that estimated defense expenditures for fiscal 1966 will be reduced another $300 million. 

He announces that two squadrons of B-52B bombers--the earliest and least effective B-52's--will be eliminated, outdated Atlas and Titan I missiles will be retired this year, and the remaining B-47's will be phased out in fiscal 1966. Steps will be taken to realign the Army Reserves with the National Guard and to eliminate unnecessary units, resulting in annual savings of about $150 million. The recently announced consolidation, reduction, or discontinuance of Defense activities in some 95 locations, when added to those previously completed, will produce annual savings of over $1 billion every year in Defense Department operations.

In notes to the United States, the United Kingdom, France, and the Federal Republic of Germany, the Soviet Union warns that execution of an alleged plan to establish an "atomic mine belt" along the eastern borders of the Federal Republic would aggravate the European political situation, heighten international tensions, and increase the danger of the outbreak of nuclear war. It also charges that the plan would run counter to the Potsdam agreement and other World War II agreements.

-- 
--  

January 19, 1965. The Atomic Energy Commission announces that the United States has detected a certain amount of venting from the Soviet underground nuclear test of January 15. In view of the partial test-ban treaty, the Department of State has asked the Soviet Union for information on the event. 

January 21, 1965.

In a communique issued at Warsaw, the Warsaw Pact countries declare that plans for a multilateral nuclear force (MLF) and an Atlantic nuclear force (ANF) constitute a grave danger to the peace of Europe and the world. Execution of these plans, they say, would give the 

Federal Republic of Germany access to nuclear weapons and thus encourage its revanchist tendencies. They warn that they will take "necessary defense measures" to ensure their 

5 own security if these plans are implemented. 

They also declare their support for: (1) a denuclearized zone in Central Europe, (2) the Polish proposal for a conference of European states to discuss European security, (3) a non-aggression pact between the NATO and Warsaw Pact countries, (4) a German peace settlement, (5) the Chinese proposal for a world summit conference on the banning and destruction of nuclear weapons, and (6) the nonaligned proposal for a world disarmament conference.

January 25, 1965. The Soviet Ambassador orally informs the Department of State that some radioactive debris from the January 15 Soviet test leaked into the atmosphere.

In the Soviet view, however, the amount is so insignificant that it excludes the possibility of a violation of the limited test-ban treaty." 

January 26, 1965. 

1965. Appearing before the House Foreign 

Affairs Committee, ACDA Director Foster states that the

Soviet test of January 15 may have been a technical violation of the partial test-ban treaty. On the basis of what is known to the United States, however, it appears to be accidental, and he does not regard it as a threat to U.S. national security or to the purposes of the treaty. He adds that this is the considered opinion of the Committee of Principals. He states that, because of the importance of the subject, the matter is under continuing study.

February 8, 1965. During Congressional hearings, the Atomic Energy Commission announces a new policy of requiring tighter control over agreements between private American companies and foreign companies or governments to provide information on the technology of nuclear propulsion. Under the new policy, the Atomic Energy Commission and the Departments of State, Defense, and Commerce will review export licenses for companies participating in foreign maritime nuclear programs. 

February 15, 1965. The Atomic Energy Commission announces it will further reduce the rate of production of enriched uranium. The new reduction will be gradually carried out from 1966 to 1969. At the end of this period, power consumption in the three AEC gaseous diffusion plants will reach a level of 2,000 megawatts, a decrease of 970 megawatts. When all power reductions are completed, the diffusion plant operating power will be about 60 percent below the 4,850-megawatt level planned prior to the 1964 cutbacks. The AEC emphasizes, however, that the lower level of production will meet currently projected military and civilian requirements.

February 16, 1965. The Department of State announces it has reason to believe that Communist China is preparing for another nuclear test. It also deplores the apparent 

Chinese intention to continue atmospheric testing in the face of world-wide condemnation. 

February 18, 1965. In his report to Congress on defense policy, Secretary of Defense McNamara states that the possibility of a nuclear catastrophe, whether by design or acciden becomes even more real as the arms race runs on and weapons multiply and become more swift and deadly. He also says that more armaments, either offensive or defensive, cannot solve the dilemma. Because the United States and the Soviet 

Union are the two nations most endangered by these weapons, they share a common interest in seeing that they are never used, and the United States intends to take every possible step that might lead to greater understanding with the Soviet Union.

He asserts that the United States will stand firm against Communist China until that country appreciates that its security can be better promoted by a peaceful policy.

E Among the cutbacks in U.S. defense spending announced by Mr. McNamara is the decision not to build 200 previously planned Minutemen because improvements have given these missiles greater destructive capability than originally expected. 

The UN General Assembly adjourns. 

February 22, 1965.

In testimony before the Senate 

Foreign Relations Committee, ACDA Director Foster repeats most of his comments of January 26 on the Soviet nuclear test of January 15. He adds that the matter is still under continuing study and that this overall course of conduct has been approved by the President as well as the Committee of Principals. 

February 24, 1965. Finnish President Kekkonen says his country will continue to pursue a neutral policy. 

Under the Finnish-Soviet pact of 1948, however, "Finland will defend its territory if Finland or the USSR via Finnish territory become the object of armed attack by Germany or states allied with it, and Finland may in precisely defined conditions undertake this with the military cooperation of the USSR." He states that although the MLF may be intended to enable Germany to defend itself and at the same time prevent it from obtaining a nuclear force of its own, the Soviet Union and other Warsaw Pact countries regard German participation in the force as endangering the peace of Europe. If the force is established, he concludes, the peace of Northern Europe would be so endangered that Finland, in its own national interest, has the right to express its views on the matter. He adds that taking such a stand does not conflict with Finland's neutral policy.

THE UN DISARMAMENT COMMISSION 

The Disarmament Commission was established by the General Assembly on January 11, 1952. It replaced two earlier commissions, the UN Commission on Atomic Energy and the Commission for Conventional Armaments. The work of the previous commissions had come to a standstill in 1950, when the Soviets walked out over the 

Chinese representation issue. 

As initially established, the Disarmament

Commission comprised the 11 members of the Security Council and Canada. From 1954 to 1957 the principal negotiations were carried on in a smaller body, the five-nation Subcommittee (the United States, the United 

Kingdom, France, Canada, and the Soviet Union). At the

General Assembly of 1957 the Soviets refused to participate any longer in the Subcommittee or the Disarmament Commission as then constituted. The General Assembly attempted to solve the problem by adding 14 members to the Disarmament Commission, but the composition of the enlarged organ was also unsatisfactory to the Soviet Union since it did not meet the Soviet demand for parity (equal representation of communist and Western states). 

The Disarmament Commission did not meet in 1958, and that fall the General Assembly enlarged it to include all 

UN members.

In its expanded form, the Disarmament Commission has met only once. This occurred in 1960, after the 

Soviet Union had walked out of the Ten Nation Disarmament Committee in the aftermath of the U-2 incident. 

The Western powers left their delegations in Geneva for several weeks and attempted to get negotiations moving again through diplomatic channels. After these efforts failed, the United States proposed a meeting of the Disarmament Commission. The Soviets, who had previously submitted the disarmament question to the forthcoming General Assembly, opposed the American proposal and charged that it was undertaken for domestic political reasons. They countered with a suggestion that all heads of government personally attend the General Assembly. 

The Disarmament Commission nevertheless met in August 1960, and the Soviets attended. This session was marked by a new American cutoff proposal (August 16).

The Origin of the Measures in the Soviet Memorandum of December 7, 1964 to the United Nations

The attached paper on the Soviet memorandum of December 7, 1964 has been prepared by the Reference Research Staff. It is circulated for background purposes and is not an official statement of policy. 

Attachment: 

As stated.

Medora M. Holm 

Documents Officer

Room 5666 NS Ext. 2761 UNCLASSIFIED

THE ORIGIN OF THE MEASURES IN THE SOVIET MEMORANDUM OF DECEMBER 7, 1964, TO THE UNITED NATIONS 

On December 7, 1964, the Soviet Union submitted to the President of the UN General Assembly a Memorandum on Measures for the Further Reduction of International Tension and Limitation of the Arms Race. 1/ None of the 11 measures were new, and the Soviet Union had previously espoused them all. All except the test ban (item 8) and reduction of forces (item 11) were originated by the Soviet Union. 1. 

Reduction of military budgets. In its December 7 memorandum the Soviet Union proposed a 10-15 per cent reduction in the military budgets of the major Powers. It also declared that if an agreement on such a reduction were reached, it would be willing to provide for using some of the resources saved to help the developing countries. 

The Soviet Union first proposed reductions in military budgets on July 26, 1948, when it introduced in the Working Committee of the Commission for Conventional Armaments proposals for the general regulation and reduction of armaments and armed forces. One of these proposals called for the "reduction of war budgets and State expenditure on production of armaments." 2/

The Soviet Union was not the first to propose the use of savings from disarmament to help developing countries. 

On October 24, 1950, President Truman stated that "real disarmament" would make possible a "greatly enlarged program of mutual aid." 3/ The Soviets have, however, advocated this for many years. Their proposal of March 27, 1956, provided that savings from disarmament should be used either domestically or to help the developing countries. 4/ 2. 

Withdrawal or cutback of foreign troops stationed in alien territories. In its December 7 memorandum the Soviet 

Union proposed conclusion of an agreement to withdraw all foreign troops to within their national frontiers. It suggested that a first step in this direction could be a cutback, and it said it was prepared to cut back its troops 1/ A/5827. 

2/ Documents on Disarmament, 1945-1959, vol. I, pp. 173-174. 

3/ Ibid. 9 p. 259. / Ibid., p. 604.

- in East Germany and the other European countries if the Western Powers cut back theirs in West Germany and other countries. This could be done, it added, by way of mutual example.

As noted below, the Soviet Union has been demanding the elimination of all foreign bases since 1951. Soviet proposals for general and complete disarmament (1959-1964) have also included provisions for the withdrawal of foreign troops.

Long before this, however, the Soviet Union had called for the withdrawal of certain troops from certain foreign territories. On January 21, 1946, it charged in the Security 

Council that the presence of British troops in Greece constituted interference in the internal affairs of that country and contributed to tension. 1/ On August 29, 1946, it proposed that the Security Council study the question of the presence of Allied forces on the territory of states other than ex-enemy countries. 2/ In its comprehensive proposal introduced in the Disarmament Subcommittee May 10, 1955, it proposed that the four Powers withdraw their occupatio troops from Germany to within their national borders except for "strictly limited contingents" to be left there pending conclusion of an agreement for final withdrawal. 3/ 

The Soviet memorandum of December 7, 1964, does, however, mark the first time the Soviet Union proposed the withdrawal of foreign troops "by way of mutual example," although in its January 28, 1964, memorandum to the ENDC it announced a readiness to agree to reduce foreign troops on a "basis of reciprocity" so that the process of complete withdrawal could be executed "gradually, step by step." 4/ 

3. Dismantling of foreign military bases. In its 

December 7 memorandum the Soviet Union called for the dismantling of foreign military bases. 

1/ The United States and the United Nations: Report by the 

President to the Congress for the Year 1946, p. 35. 

2/ Documents on Disarmament, 1945-1959, vol. I, p. 43 (footnote) 

3/ Ibid. p. 459. ENDC/123.

A Soviet proposal of November 8, 1951, called on the General Assembly to declare that the maintenance by certain states of military bases on foreign territory was incompatible with membership in the United Nations. 1/

Two years later the Soviet Union wished to have the General Assembly recommend Security Council action to bring about the "elimination of military bases in the territories of other States." 2/ 

4. Prevention of the further spread of nuclear weapons.

In its December 7 memorandum the Soviet Union proposed conclusion of an agreement to prevent the non-nuclear states from gaining direct or indirect access to nuclear weapons, "either directly or through military alliances. 

Probably the first specific Soviet proposal to prevent the spread of nuclear weapons was contained in the Soviet memorandum on partial measures submitted to the General Assembly on September 20, 1957. In that memorandum the 

Soviet Union declared it was necessary for states possessing nuclear weapons to agree not to install them on foreign soil and not to put them at the disposal of other states "or commands of military blocs." 3/ 

5. Pronibition of the use of nuclear weapons. In its 

December 7 memorandum the Soviet Union proposed conclusion of an international agreement banning the use of nuclear weapons.

The Soviet Union first called for concluding such an agreement on June 19, 1946, when Ambassador Gromyko proposed a convention prohibiting the use of nuclear weapons "in any circumstances whatsoever. 11 4/ 6.

Establishment of denuclearized zones. In its 

December 7 memorandum the Soviet Union declared its support for the establishment of denuclearized zones in various parts of the world. It cited specifically Central Europe, the 

Mediterranean, Northern Europe, the Balkans, Africa, the 

Indian Ocean, and the Near and Middle East. 

1/ Documents on Disarmament, 1945-1959, vol. I, p. 288. 

2/ Ibid. p. 391. / Ibid. 

4/ Ibid., vol. II, p. 878. vol. I, p. 21.

The Soviet Union first proposed a denuclearized zone on March 27, 1956, when it submitted to the Disarmament Subcommittee a draft agreement on the Reduction of Conventional Armaments and Armed Forces. The agreement called for creation of a "zone of limitation and inspection" in Europe, including both parts of Germany and the countries adjacent thereto, in which the stationing of nuclear forces or weapons would be prohibited. 1/ 

7. Destruction of bombers. In its December 7 memorandum the Soviet Union proposed the destruction of bombers. It spoke of all bombers, but it expressed a willingness to destroy them in phases and to have the large Powers be the first to eliminate them.

The Soviet Union first proposed the elimination of bombers as a separable measure in its January 28, 1964, memo to the ENDC. It proposed to eliminate all bombers. 2/ 

Previously, however, the United States had informally suggested to the Soviet Union the destruction of U.S. B-47 and Soviet TU-16 bombers. 3/ 

8. Banning of underground nuclear weapon tests. In its December 7 memorandum the Soviet Union proposed that all nuclear weapon tests be banned and said it was prepared to reach agreement prohibiting underground tests based on the use of national means for controlling the ban. 

1/ Documents on Disarmament, 1945-1959, vol. I, p. 607.

For a summary of other zonal proposals, see "Background Information on the Soviet Eight-Point Memorandum of 

September 26, 1961" (Disarmament Document Series, REF 

206), pp. 11-14, and "Background Information on the Non 

Dissemination of Nuclear Weapons" (Disarmament Document 

Series, REF 380), pp. 16-22. 

2/ ENDC/123. 

3/ ENDC/PV.160, p. 33.

203 The Soviet Union first called for the banning of nuclear weapon tests on May 10, 1955, when it submitted to the Disarmament Subcommittee its comprehensive proposal of that date. Included in this proposal among the measures suggested for execution in 1956 was a stipulation that states possessing nuclear weapons discontinue tests of these weapons. 1/

The Soviet Union was not the first, however, to propose a cessation of nuclear weapon testing. On April 2, 1954,

Prime Minister Nehru, in an address to the Indian Parliament, suggested a "standstill agreement" to stop testing pending progress toward the prohibition and elimination of weapons of mass destruction. 2/

The first Soviet action advocating the test ban as a separable measure came on November 2, 1955, in the General 

Assembly. 3/ 

9. Non-aggression pact between the NATO States and the 

Warsaw Treaty States. In its December 7 memorandum the Soviet 

Union proposed conclusion of a non-aggression pact between the NATO and Warsaw Pact countries as a first step toward a comprehensive system of collective security for Europe. 

The Soviet Union first proposed a non-aggression pact between the NATO and Warsaw Pact countries on July 21, 1955, at the Geneva summit meeting. The Soviet Union here called upon the NATO and Warsaw Pact countries to refrain from using armed force against each other and to consult in the event of a threat to peace in Europe. The pact was to be expressly "of a provisional nature." 4/

It should be noted that the Soviet Union has always regarded such a pact as a transitional measure toward the goal of a comprehensive European security system and that Soviet proposals for this security system are older than those for the non-aggression pact. The Soviet Union first 

Documents on Disarmament, 1945-1959, vol. I, p. 462. 

Ibid., p. 410. 

Geneva Conference on the Discontinuance of Nuclear Weapon

Tests: History and Analysis of Negotiations, pp. 4-5. 

4/ Documents on Germany, 1944-1961, pp. 183-184.

proposed a comprehensive European security system on 

February 10, 1954, at the Berlin Foreign Ministers Meeting.

The proposed treaty to establish this system would have obligated the parties to refrain from committing aggression against each other and to refrain from using or threatening to use force in their international relations. 1 

10. Prevention of surprise attack. In its December 7 memorandum the Soviet Union proposed three measures to reduce the danger of surprise attack: (1) reduction of foreign troops, (2) denuclearization of West and East Germany, and (3) stationing of observation posts in NATO and Warsaw Pact countries. It stated that establishing the observation posts would be useful only if combined with the other two measures. The proposals to reduce foreign troops and denuclearize Germany have been dealt with above (items 2 and 6). 

The Soviet Union first proposed establishment of observation posts on May 10, 1955. Its comprehensive plan introduced in the Disarmament Subcommittee on that date provided that for the purpose of preventing surprise attack, the international control organ should have on the territories of all countries involved "control posts" at "large ports, at railway junctions, on main motor highways and at aerodromes. 2/ 

11. Reduction of total forces. In its December 7 memorandum the Soviet Union proposed reductions in the total forces of all states, either by the conclusion of agreements or by the policy of mutual example. 

The Soviet Union joined other nations in supporting the 

General Assembly resolution of December 14, 1946, recommend ing "the general regulation and reduction of armaments and armed forces." This resolution was the outcome of a debate initiated by the Soviet Union on the presence of foreign troops in certain countries. In its final form, the resolution was based on an American draft and represented the work of a special subcommittee of 20 members, including both the United States and the Soviet Union. During the previous debate the Soviet Union had tabled a proposal for the reduction of armaments and for the submission of information on forces as well as armaments, but it had not explicity proposed the reduction of forces. 3/ 

Documents on Germany, 1944-1961, p. 153. 

Documents on Disarmament, 1945-1959, vol. I. p. 466. 

Documents on Disarmament, 1945-1959, vol. I. pp. 42, 47-49;

The United States and the United Nations: Report by the 

President to the Congress for the Year 1946, p. 95.

The Soviet Union first proposed force reductions on May 21, 1947, when it introduced in the Commission for Conventional Armaments a draft plan of work calling for the establishment of general principles for the reduction of armaments and armed forces and for the reduction of war production. 1/ 11 The Soviet Union has long advocated force reductions by "mutual example. On May 14, 1956, the Soviet Government announced a decision to reduce forces by 1,200,000 men, and stated that it would be willing to make further reductions if the Western Powers followed this example. 2/ On January 15, 1960, the Supreme Soviet approved a law on the reduction of Soviet forces to 2,423,000 men, accompanied by an appeal to foreign parliaments to take similar action. 3/ 

Documents on Disarmament, 1945-1959, vol. I, pp. 84-85. 

2/ Ibid. pp. 638-639. 

Ibid., 1960, pp. 16-22.

SC IDOL LIBRARY

REF #388 

April 30, 1965

Disarmament Chronology - March 1965 

There is attached the March 1965 chronology of disarmament developments prepared by the Reference 

Research Staff.

These chronologies are for background information purposes only and do not in any way constitute official statements of policy. 

Medora M. Holm 

Documents Officer

Room 1886 NS 

Ext. 4583 

Attachment: 

Chronology March 1965 

---

I. 

MARCH 1965

HIGHLIGHTS 

The principal arms control and disarmament development in March 1965 was the Soviet request for a meeting of the 

UN Disarmament Commission in early April because (1) the

ENDC had reached no agreement on concrete disarmament proposals, (2) the 19th General Assembly had been unable to discuss disarmament, and (3) all UN members should have an opportunity to express their views (March 31). Although the 

United States would have preferred to reconvene the ENDC, which in its view was better suited for the negotiation of concrete disarmament proposals, it had no objection to the proposed meeting (March 31).

Another noteworthy development of the month was the issuance of a final act by the Preparatory Commission on Latin American Denuclearization. The act established a 

Coordinating Committee to coordinate the efforts of three Working Groups, responsible for defining the denuclearized zone, securing the cooperation of Latin American countries not associated with the Preparatory Commission, obtaining the consent of states having territories within the zone to respect it, studying required verification measures, and seeking to obtain the pledge of the nuclear powers to respect the zone (March 22).

- 

II. CHRONOLOGY 

March 6, 1965. In a speech in Cleveland, Secretary of St 

Rusk declared that the spread of national ownership and control of nuclear weapons could threaten the peace and Western unity. He pointed out, however, that other countries sought to play a responsible role in their nuclear defense. 

The challenge was to to enable them to play that role througn joint instead of national programs.

He recalled that to meet this challenge, taree U.S. administrations had offered to join other NATO countries in creating a seaborne missile force that would help to meet NATO's need for medium-range missiles to offset hundreds of Soviet missiles arrayed against Western Europe. He noted that the British proposal for expanding the proposed force to include Polaris submarines and V-bombers was being discussed. by several governments. He said the United States hoped there would be progress in these talks that would lead to fruitful multilateral discussions. 

The Secretary also stated that the United States and its European allies sought closer contacts with the Eastern European countries and the Soviet Union in order to bring about a lasting. settlement in Central Europe. He added that the United States was continuing to hold discussions with the United Kingdom, France, and Germany on how to achieve a settlement in the interests of European security and "satisfaction of the legitimate aspiration of the German people to shape their own destiny. 

March 9, 1965. A spokesman for the Department of State again declared that on the basis of what was known then, the Soviet nuclear test of January 15 did not represent a threat to U.S. national security or to the test-ban treaty, nor did it, standing alone, indicate resumption of testing prohibited by the treaty. He added, however, that the event might have constituted a technical violation of the treaty. 

Because of this, the United States had asked the Soviet Union for further clarification, and the matter was under continuing study.

-- · - 

March 18. 1965. The Department of Defense announced plans to detonate a nuclear device of low intermediate yield deep underground on Amchitka Island in the Aleutians late this year. It said this experiment would be part of a continuing program to improve long-range techniques for detecting, locating, and identifying underground nuclear tests. It also advised that studies and explorations made in the vicinity of Amchitka Island in 1964 had satisfied scientists that a seismic experiment of the required size could be conducted without danger to humans or wildlife populations in the area.

March 22, 1965. The Preparatory Commission on Latin 

American Denuclearization unanimously approved a final act establishing a Coordinating Committee to supervise the efforts of three Working Groups: 

The first Working Group would: (1) define the zone of the proposed denuclearization, (2) secure cooperation from the Latin American republics not presently associated with the Preparatory Commission, and (3) obtain agreement of other continental or extracontinental states having responsibility over territories within the zone to accept for those territories the commitments made by the Latin American republics. 

The second Working Group would study measures of "verification, inspection and control" required to ensure strict compliance with the denuclearization pact. 

The third Working Group would endeavor to gain the pledge of the nuclear powers to respect the denuclearization. 

The Working Groups were instructed to submit their preliminary reports by July 1, and the second meeting of the Preparatory Commission was scheduled for August 23. 

March 24, 1965. Secretary of State Rusk stated that the gases used in Vietnam, which were of the tear gas family, were not prohibited by the Geneva protocol of 1925 or any other agreement concerning the use of gas.

-- 
-. 

March 31, 1965. The Soviet representative at the 

United Nations asked theSecretary General to convene the UN Disarmament Commission in the first half of April. 

In a public statement following his meeting with the Secretary General, the Soviet representative stated that the request was made because: (1) the ENDC had reached no agreement on concrete disarmament proposals, (2) the 19th General Assembly had not been able to discuss disarmament, and (3) it was appropriate for all UN members to have an opportunity to express their views. 

The U.S. representative at the United Nations, Ambassador 

Stevenson, said that although the United States would prefer to reconvene the ENDC, which in the U.S. view was a better forum for negotiating concrete disarmament proposals, it had no objection to the proposed meeting and to giving all UN members an opportunity to express their views. He stated that if a majority supported such a meeting, the United States would participate constructively and hope for the emergence of useful and constructive ideas that could then be considered in detail in the ENDC.
