Ext. 4583 

UNCLASSIFIED

A CHRONOLOGY OF DISARMAMENT DEVELOPMENTS 

1964 

Reference Research Staff United States Arms Control and Disarmament Agency 

April 1965

January 8, 1964. In his State of the Union message, President Johnson announces that U.S. production of enriched uranium will be reduced by 25 percent and that the Atomic Energy Commission will close down 4 of its 14 reactors producing plutonium for weapons. He calls on the Soviet Union to take similar steps. 

January 18, 1964. President Johnson, in his reply to 

Premier Khrushchev's letter of December 31, 1963, urging the peaceful settlement of territorial disputes, calls for a deemphasis of East-West differences and appeals to the Soviet Union to support concrete steps to strengthen peace, namely: (1) prevention of the spread of nuclear weapons, (2) cessation of the production of fissionable materials for weapons uses, (3) the transfer of large amounts of fissionable materials to peaceful uses, (4) the prohibition of all nuclear tests, (5) (5) limitations on nuclear weapons systems, (6) reduction of the risk of war by accident or design, and (7) progress toward general disarmament. 88 

Commenting on Khrushchev's proposal regarding the peaceful settlement of territorial disputes, the President emphasizes that the principle of peaceful settlement should apply to all wars and proposes the following guidelines: (1) All governments should abstain from the direct or indirect threat or use of force to change international boundaries, other territorial or demarcation lines established by international agreement or practice, the dispositions of truce or military armistice agreements, or arrangements or procedures concerning access or passage across certain areas. Nor should any government attempt to enlarge its territory by overthrowing established authorities. (2) These limitations should apply regardless of the direct or indirect form that a threat or use of force might assume, including the clandestine supply of arms. (3) The parties to any dispute should seek a peaceful solution by peaceful means--negotiation, mediation, judicial settlement, action by an appropriate regional or UN organ, or other peaceful means of their own choice. (4) These obligations should be generally observed, and the inherent right of self-defense that is recognized in the UN Charter would remain fully operative. 

January 21, 1964. The Eighteen Nation Disarmament

Committee (ENDC) reconvenes in Geneva. In a message to the 

Conference, President Johnson proposes five collateral measures:

) prohibition of the threat or use of force, directly or indirectly--whether by aggression, subversion, or the clandestine supply of arms--to change boundaries or demarcation lines, interfere with access to territory, or extend control over territory by displacing established authorities; (2) a verified freeze of the number and characteristics of strategic nuclear delivery vehicles; (3) a verified agreement on the cessation of the production of fissionable materials for weapons uses; (4) creation of a system of observation posts to further reduce the danger of war by accident, miscalculation, or surprise attack; and (5) in order to halt the spread of nuclear weapons, agreements that: (a) nuclear weapons not be transferred into the national control of states that do not now control them, (b) all transfers of nuclear materials for peaceful uses take place under effective international safeguards, (c) the major nuclear powers accept increasingly for their peaceful nuclear activities the same inspection they recommend for other states, and (a) all nuclear weapons tests be banned under effective verification and control.

In his budget message to the Congress, President Johnson announces that U.S. defense expenditures for fiscal year 1965 ($51.2 billion) will be $1.1 billion less than those for fiscal year 1964. 

January 22, 1964. In a speech at Barnard College,

Secretary of State Rusk states that the NATO multilateral nuclear force could and would be protected under a verified freeze of strategic nuclear delivery vehicles. 

January 24, 1964. Ceylon declares it will deny entry to its ports or airports to any foreign ship or aircraft without prior assurance that it is not carrying nuclear weapons and is not equipped for nuclear warfare. Ceylon says this measure is intended to prevent the further spread of nuclear weapons and to promote the establishment of nuclear-free zones. 

January 28, 1964. At the ENDC, the Soviet Union submits a memorandum proposing 9 measures to reduce tensions and to ease the arms race: (1) foreign troop withdrawals, (2) reduction of forces, (3) a 10-15 percent reduction of military budgets, (4) a nonaggression pact between the NATO and Warsaw Pact countries, (5) denuclearized zones, (6) non-dissemination of nuclear weapons, (7) measures to prevent surprise attack, (8) elimination of all bombers, and (9) prohibition of underground tests.

-- 
--  - The Department of State notes the Soviet bomber proposal and recalls an earlier informal U.S. proposal for the phased destruction of U.S. B-47's and Soviet TU-16's. 

January 30, 1964. The British representative suggests that the ENDC set up an expert working group to examine the implications of budget cuts and the problems of verifying them. 

February 4, 1964.

At the ENDC, the Soviet Union submits amendments to its draft treaty on general and complete disarmament. The amendments incorporate the principal provisions of the revised Gromyko proposal of September 19, 1963, for a "nuclear umbrella" i.e., the United States and the Soviet 

Union would retain a limited number of ICBMs, defensive missiles, and nuclear warheads on their own territories until the end of the disarmament process.

February 6, 1964. At the ENDC, ACDA Director Foster says that the United States will have private discussions with the Soviet Union in an effort to reach agreement on a non-dissemination agreement based on the Irish resolution. 

He says that it is the declared policy of the United States not to take any action inconsistent with that resolution. 11 

February 13, 1964. At the ENDC, Brazil tables a working paper for an Agreement on the Application of Savings on Military Expenditures. The paper calls upon all governments to reduce their military expenditures and to credit not less that 20 percent of the savings to a fund for the economic and social development of the less developed areas. The paper also calls for immediate establishment within the ENDC framework of a working group to study and make recommendations regarding the structure and functioning of the fund. 

Soviet Ambassador Tsarapkin notes "with satisfaction" the support given by Brazil to the reduction of military budgets, and says that some savings could be used to help the less developed countries. He rejects the UK suggestion of 

January 30 for establishing a working group to study the question of verifying reductions in military budgets. He adds that the Soviet Union would not object to considering the question of control as soon as there is a "real basis" for agreement. 

February 20, 1964. 

Ambassador Tsarapkin proposes that the ENDC address an appeal to all states to follow the example of the Soviet Union and the United States and reduce their military budgets.

ACDA Director Foster comments that the United States would welcome reduction of military expenditures but that "concrete, verified disarmament agreements - not vague, unenforceable resolutions are the best means of saving 

more funds for the betterment of mankind." 

February 25, 1964. President De Gaulle, in his reply to 

Premier Khrushchev's letter of December 31, 1963, states that adherence to the principle of self-determination of nations would be conducive to the solution of international problems. 

February 26, 1964. FRG Chancellor Erhard, in his reply to Premier Khrushchev's letter of December 31, 1963, states that the principal cause of tension in Europe is not unsettled boundaries but rather the partition of Germany, and he urges adherence to the principle of self-determination as a means of solving the German problem.

The Board of Governors of the International Atomic Energy 

Agency approves extension of the IAEA safeguards system to cover reactors of 100 or more megawatts. 

February 27, 1964. In the ENDC, the Indian representative says that his country does not object to military budget reductions in principle. He adds, however, that it is "not easy for a country facing the threat of aggression to cut down military expenditure" and notes that India has been forced to increase her defense expenditures. 

February 29, 1964. Poland circulates to other governments a memorandum on the Gomulka plan for a nuclear arms freeze in Central Europe. The proposal calls for freezing nuclear arms in Poland, Czechoslovakia, East Germany, and the

Federal Republic of Germany at their present levels, with controls to be exercised by a mixed commission consisting of an equal number of representatives from the Warsaw Pact and NATO countries at frontier rail transportation centers, waterway junctions, and seaports and airports. In the area concerned, the parties would be obligated not to produce, introduce, or import, and not to transfer or accept such weapons. 

Controls would extend to plants which produce or are capable of producing such weapons. 

March 2, 1964. In an interview with an Izvestiya correspondent, Foreign Minister Gromyko asserts that because of the position of the Western Powers the ENDC thus far has accomplished nothing.

March 3, 1964. The British representative on the ENDC 

says he does not understand why seaborne missiles were excluded from the "nuclear umbrella" of the Gromyko proposal, since the invulnerability of such missiles would contribute to "the stability of the balance of mutual deterrence. 

11 He also questions the inclusion of ABMS in the Gromyko proposal, since an effective ABM defense system would upset the stability of the nuclear balance.

March 5, 1964. Deputy ACDA Director Fisher tells the 

ENDC that the United States favors application of IAEA safeguards to all international nuclear transfers for peaceful uses. He announces that the large Yankee Power Reactor at 

Rowe, Massachusetts, will be inspected by the IAEA in order to assist the IAEA in developing effective safeguards and to set an example for other nuclear powers to invite the application of IAEA safeguards to their reactor facilities. 

March 12, 1964. In the ENDC, the Indian representative says that IAEA safeguards should not be applied to nuclear equipment which does not serve military purposes. He believes that extension of IAEA safeguards to equipment which serves only peaceful purposes would widen the gap between the developed and the under-developed countries. 11 Ambassador Tsarapkin criticizes the U.S. proposal for a strategic delivery vehicles freeze because it would not actually reduce armaments and claims that it is an attempt to maintain "enormous destructive power. He says that verification of the freeze would "open up possibilities for intelligence activities on the territory of the Soviet Union" and 'increase mutual suspicion. He also notes that the freeze would not bar the MLF or prevent arming British submarines with Polaris missiles. 11 

March 17, 1964. In the ENDC, Deputy ACDA Director Fisher discusses the Gromyko proposal (see February 4). He says that drastic reduction of nuclear delivery vehicles at the outset of disarmament, combined with equal percentage conventional cuts, would result in radical shifts in the existing military balance. He says that Soviet verification procedures are unsatisfactory and that the abolition of foreign bases would give the USSR a very advantageous military position in Europe. 

He denies that the "nuclear umbrella" is a satisfactory substitute for adequate peacekeeping and asserts that it might be turned into an instrument of blackmail against non-nuclear states. He also denies that the Gromyko proposal could abolish the nuclear threat, for the effectiveness of the "nuclear umbrella" would depend on the threat of nuclear war.

. 

In reply, Ambassador Tsarapkin still maintains that the Gromyko proposal would virtually eliminate the nuclear threat in the first stage of disarmament. He denies that the U.S. plan would do so and says that destruction of 97 or even 99 percent of existing nuclear weapons stocks would not eliminate the threat. He states that the West must accept the Gromyko proposal in principle before discussing the specific numbers of missiles which would be retained. Answering the British criticism of March 3, he argues that submarines are intrinsically offensive and can not be subjected to "permanent control" in the same way as land-based ICBMS, and he maintains that ABMS are inherently defensive. 

March 19, 1964. At the ENDC, Deputy ACDA Director Fisher proposes destruction of 480 U.S. B-47's and 480 Soviet TU-16's drawn from each side's "operational inventory" at the rate of 20 each per month over a two-year period. He says that the 

United States would also be willing to destroy an agreed number of the same type of aircraft now "stored and preserved for emergency mobilization. He believes that this measure would insure that the bombers could not be transferred to other nations and would thus inhibit nuclear proliferation. 11 

March 24, 1964. At the ENDC, the Indian representative urges the West to accept "in principle" the Gromyko proposal for a "nuclear umbrella," i.e., the retention of only a strictly limited number of vehicles until the end of Stage III. In the Indian view, acceptance could lead to a detailed study of the proposal in order to narrow the gap between West and East on the issue. 

March 26, 1964. At the ENDC, the United Kingdom submits a working paper outlining a plan for the establishment of a system of observation posts to guard against war by accident, miscalculation, or surprise attack. The posts would monitor military movements by rail, road, air, and sea and would be manned on an "adversary basis": i.e., posts in the West would be manned by Warsaw Pact representatives, and posts in the East would be manned by NATO representatives. national observers would not be excluded.

Inter- 

Deputy ACDA Director Fisher supports the U.K. plan on observation posts and states that Soviet efforts to link establishment of such posts with foreign troop withdrawals and German denuclearization have prevented the holding of fruitful discussions on the question.

Ambassador Tsarapkin declares that the U.K. working paper offers "nothing new" because it fails to include a reduction of foreign troops stationed in Europe and a denuclearization of Germany. Without being linked to these measures, he argues, the U.K. plan would undermine rather than engender confidence and would merely be another attempt to effect controls without disarmament.

March 31, 1964. At the ENDC, Deputy ACDA Director Fisher says that the West cannot accept "in principle" the Gromyko proposal for a "nuclear umbrella." As he understands it, the 

Gromyko proposal would eliminate 97-99 percent of nuclear delivery vehicles in the first stage, while conventional armaments would be reduced by only 30 percent. He states that the United States will continue to seek further informa tion on the Gromyko proposal but that in its present form it is deficient with respect to verification, peacekeeping machinery, and effect on the military balance. 

April 2, 1964. Ambassador Tsarapkin criticizes the U.S.

B-47/TU-16 bomber proposal as an attempt to pass off modernization as disarmament. Again advocating the Soviet proposal to destroy all bombers, he says that the USSR is willing to adopt a "flexible formula" and agree to destroy the bombers of the major powers first. 

April 9, 1964. suggests: (1) application of the U.S. plan for a verified freeze of strategic nuclear delivery vehicles to the MLF in order to make possible the conclusion of a non-dissemination agreement; (2) a phased program for the destruction of all bombers, beginning with the B-47, TU-16, and other bombers; (3) extension of the test-ban treaty to cover underground tests above a certain threshold, as proposed by Brazil on 

September 19, 1963; and (4) use of the ENDC as a "clearing house" for mutual example reductions of military budgets. 

In the ENDC, the UAR representative 11 

Discussing the difficulty of verifying military budget reductions, Deputy ACDA Director Fisher points out that the published Soviet military budget consists of only "sixteen words and one sum. He asks whether this covers military research and development, maintenance of military installations, stockpiling strategic materials, and defense production. He notes that the Soviet Union, like other governments, does not rely on the trust of its officials but has auditing procedures to insure compliance with fiscal regulations. He feels that other states are entitled to "similar assurance. 

In reply, Ambassador Tsarapkin accuses the West of trying to obtain "economic and financial intelligence" about the 

Soviet economy.

April 16, 1964. In the ENDC, Deputy ACDA Director Fisher elaborates the U.S. proposal for a freeze of strategic nuclear delivery vehicles. The freeze would cover five categories of missiles, bombers, and anti-missile missile systems. Production of these vehicles would be stopped, except as required for maintenance and replacement of missiles expended in allowed confidence and training firings. The freeze would be verified by continuing inspection of declared production facilities, an annual quota of inspections to check undeclared locations for possible violations, observation of space launchings and allowed missile firings, and observation of the destruction of vehicles and launchers being replaced. 

Verification would be less extensive than that required for general and complete disarmament and would not involve a check on existing levels. 

April 20, 1964. President Johnson announces that he has ordered "a further substantial reduction" in the production of enriched uranium. Combined with the reduction announced last January, the overall reduction in the production of enriched uranium will be 40 percent over a four year period. 

Premier Khrushchev announces that construction of two new big atomic reactors will be discontinued, the production of uranium-235 in the next several years will be substantially reduced, and more fissionable materials will be allocated to peaceful uses. 

April 21, 1964.

Prime Minister Douglas-Home announces that U.K. production of military plutonium will gradually be terminated.

April 28, 1964. The ENDC recesses. 

June 9, 1964. The ENDC reconvenes. 

Deputy Soviet Foreign Minister Zorin announces that the Soviet Union is willing to participate in "an appropriate working organ" to discuss concrete problems regarding the elimination of nuclear delivery vehicles, if the Committee will approve, as suggested by India on March 24, the Gromyko proposal for a "nuclear umbrella" as the basis for discussion.

He also proposes "businesslike negotiations" on the collateral measures listed in the Soviet memorandum of January 28, and places the proposal to eliminate bombers at the head of this list.

June 11, 1964. The IAEA Board of Governors approves an agreement between the United States and the Agency whereby four U. S. reactors will be placed under Agency safeguards 

I against diversion to non-peaceful ends. The largest of these 

I reactors is the Yankee Power Reactor at Rowe, Massachusetts.

It is the first power plant with a thermal capacity of over 100 megawatts to be placed under the expanded IAEA safeguards system, approved by the Board on February 26.

June 16, 1964. At the ENDC, ACDA Director Foster says that the United States cannot accept the Gromyko "nuclear umbrella" proposal as the basis for setting up a working group on the problem of nuclear delivery vehicles. That pro- posal, he points out, would require elimination of about 97 to 99 percent of all nuclear delivery vehicles in 18 months and rapidly alter the present military balance in favor of the Soviet Union. The United States cannot accept such unbalanced reductions, particularly without adequate verification procedures or institutions capable of keeping the peace and preventing the use of force in the resolution of disputes. 

The United States agrees that a technical working group should be established to deal with the problem of nuclear delivery vehicles, but it maintains that the terms of reference for this group should be consistent with the agreed procedure for work on general and complete disarmament and that all relevant proposals and matters should be open for discussion. 

June 25, 1964. At the ENDC, the United States presents a plan to provide verification for a cutoff in the production of fissionable materials for weapons. The plan provides for inspection of installations but not of stockpiles, mines, or refineries, and it does not require disclosure of information on the storage and deployment of nuclear weapons. For inspection of some production installations, access to the interior of the facilities would not be necessary. 

June 30, 1964. President Johnson requests Congressional approval of an agreement to provide NATO members with more nuclear information, explaining that the additional information would enable them to use more effectively the nuclear delivery system provided them under existing agreements and make their role in alliance planning more effective.1/ 

1 This agreement was approved by the U.S. and most other 

NATO states but had not entered into force at the end of 1964.

Premier Khrushchev declares in Oslo that the Geneva disarmament negotiations are completely useless unless a peace treaty is signed with the two German regimes. He adds that the Soviet Union will not be the first to withdraw from the disarmament talks but might send representatives of lower rank. 

July 2, 1964. In the ENDC, Mr. Zorin declares that the problem of preventing the proliferation of nuclear weapons cannot be solved unless the Western countries renounce the MLF, which he characterizes as a plan to give the Federal Republic of Germany access to nuclear weapons. 

ACDA Director Foster replies that the MLF would not be inconsistent with measures to prevent the dissemination of nuclear weapons. The missiles in the MLF would be "manned, owned, and controlled multilaterally" and no single member would be able to fire them. Moreover, the decision to fire them in wartime would be made by the United States and "an agreed number" of members, and no participating nation could withdraw its MLF elements and place them under national control. Mr. Foster reminds the Committee that the MLF has been devised in order to enable NATO members to deal with a number of threats with which they might have to deal, including hundreds of Soviet rockets arrayed against Europe. And he observes that the MLF by providing an alternative to national capabilities, ought to increase the incentives and improve the possibilities for limiting national nuclear capabilities. 

July 7. 1964. The ENDC Co-Chairmen (the U.S. and Soviet. 

representatives) announce they will hold bilateral talks in an effort to agree on a basis for an ENDC working group to consider the elimination of nuclear delivery vehicles in the context of a general disarmament agreement. 

July 9, 1964. 

The Algiers Conference for the Denuclearization of the Mediterranean, sponsored by the Communistdominated World Peace Council, issues an appeal for Mediterranean denuclearization that calls for the evacuation of all foreign forces and the elimination of all foreign bases in NATO countries as well as the evacuation of all nuclear weapons and their means of delivery. 

July 10, 1964. In a memorandum submitted to the 

Security Council, the Soviet Union proposes establishment of a UN peacekeeping force under the control of the Security Council. Communist, Western, and neutral countries would be represented in its command, but it would not include forces from the permanent members of the Security Council. The 

Soviet Union offers to pay some of the expenses of such a force but says these expenses should be met by the aggressors against whom the force would be used.

- 

July 11, 1964. In notes delivered to seven NATO countries, the Soviet Union declares that giving the Federal Republic of Germany access to nuclear weapons through the proposed MLF would greatly increase the danger of nuclear war.

July 16, 1964. U.S. Ambassador Timberlake proposes procedures for verifying the destruction of equal numbers of U.S. B-47's and Soviet TU-16 bombers. The host country would supervise and pay for the destruction of its planes at a designated airfield. Destruction would be comprehensive enough to ensure that each bomber could no longer be restored to flyable condition and its engines were no longer usable for propulsion. Before destruction, the host country could remove from the bombers whatever equipment and instruments it wished to retain. Inspectors from the other country would have the right to keep designated bombers under visual observation, witness their destruction, and make records. They would not have the right to examine any bomber before it was destroyed or any of the equipment or instruments removed. They would enjoy the same privileges and immunities accorded by the host country to diplomatic envoys. Observers designated by the nonaligned members of the ENDC and by the UN Secretary-General might also participate.

July 21, 1964. The states of the Organization of African 

Unity, meeting in Cairo July 17-21, pass a declaration on denuclearization in which they: (1) state their readiness to enter into an international agreement under UN auspices not to manufacture or acquire nuclear weapons, (2) call upon all "peace-loving" states to adhere to the agreement, (3) call upon all nuclear powers to respect and abide by the declaration, and (4) invite the General Assembly to approve the declaration and convene an international conference to draw up the agreement. 

July 28, 1964. At the ENDC, Swedish representative

Myrdal suggested that the work plan for dealing with the problem of eliminating nuclear delivery vehicles be turned upside down. Instead of dealing first with the problems involved in 

Stage I, she would have the working group deal first with the problems of Stage III the type of nuclear shield that should exist at the end of the disarmament process -- and only later with the problem of how to set up the shield. 

August 5, 1964. In a joint statement commemorating the first anniversary of the signing of the nuclear test-ban treaty, the United States, the United Kingdom, and the Soviet Union declare that the agreement has strengthened the peace, helped to reduce the arms race, and made the air more pure.

August 13, 1964. In the ENDC, the UAR representative proposes that a technical working group consider whether the MLF would be compatible with a non-dissemination agreement and that MLF plans be suspended in the meantime. 

Soviet Ambassador Tsarapkin criticizes the U. S. proposal for a fissionable materials cutoff on the following grounds: (1) it would require "control without disarmament, (2) verification would affect the security of states by disclosing the location of production facilities and making it possible to ascertain the size of weapons stockpiles, and (3) the withdrawal provisions are not satisfactory. He doubts that this problem can be solved outside the framework of general and complete disarmament. 

In an interview with Lord Thomson, Premier Khrushchev expresses skepticism about banning the use of nuclear weapons, since the losing side would "always use nuclear weapons in the last resort to avoid defeat.

August 20, 1964. In the ENDC, the Ethiopian representative urges the convening of a conference for the purpose of signing a convention prohibiting the use of nuclear weapons. 

Ambassador Tsarapkin admits that there can be no complete guarantee against nuclear war without nuclear disarmament, but he supports the Ethiopian proposal on the ground that it would slow down the arms race, reduce the danger of using nuclear weapons, and prepare the way for nuclear disarmament. 

Discussing the test ban, the British representative says that some on-site verification is still necessary for a ban on underground tests. He denies that there is a threshold at which existing means could not only detect, but also identify, "underground events of a given kiloton magnitude. 

Ambassador Tsarapkin repeats the Soviet offer to conclude an uncontrolled agreement banning underground tests, and he claims that national means can now pinpoint all underground explosions. 

August 25, 1964. In the ENDC, Ambassador Tsarapkin says the time is not ripe for establishing a working group to study the problem of eliminating nuclear delivery vehicles. 

The time for such a study will come, he says, only after the Gromyko proposal for a "nuclear umbrella" is accepted as the basis for the group's work.

-- 
-- · 13 - August 26, 1964. In connection with the commemorations of the outbreaks of both World Wars, Pope Paul VI urges all nations to avoid an arms race.

August 27, 1964, In the ENDC, U.S. Ambassador Timberlake elaborates the verification that would be required for a freeze of strategic nuclear delivery vehicles. There would be continuing inspection of declared production facilities, an annual quota of inspections to check undeclared locations for possible violations, observation of space launchings and allowed missile firings, and observation of the destruction of vehicles and launchers being replaced. These procedures, however, would not involve a check on existing levels, would not disturb the security of sensitive installations, and would be less intrusive than those required for general and complete disarmament.

The Indian representative states that under no circumstances will his country use its nuclear capabilities for non-peaceful purposes. 

August 28, 1964. Replying to the Soviet note of July 11, the United States reaffirms its readiness to conclude an agreement to prevent the dissemination of nuclear weapons and rejects the Soviet charge that the MLF would be incompatible with such an agreement. 

August 29, 1964. Commemorating the first anniversary of the installation of the "hot line," the Department of State says it was "an important first step" in reducing the risk of war by accident. The Department also declares that it was negotiated with the "full backing" of the U.S. allies. 

September 2, 1964. In a report to Congress on U.S. policy and international cooperation in Antarctica, President Johnson says that two inspections conducted by the United States in 1963 uncovered no violations of the Antarctic Treaty of 1959. 

September 3, 1964. In the ENDC, ACDA Director Foster opposes the Ethiopian proposal for a conference to prohibit the use of nuclear weapons. He states that war cannot be prevented by a simple statement of good intentions, for the real threat to peace is the use of force for aggressive purposes regardless of the type of weapons used. A ban on the use of nuclear weapons, if unaccompanied by an agreement to eliminate them, cannot provide real or lasting assurance against nuclear warfare.

He suggests that a more realistic way to reduce the risk of nuclear war would be to prevent the dissemination of nuclear weapons, and he renews the previous U.S. proposal for a non-proliferation agreement based on the Irish resolution. 

He also commends India for its August 27 pledge never to use its nuclear capabilities for non-peaceful purposes.

September 14, 1964. The eight nonaligned members of the 

ENDC submit memoranda summarizing their proposals and suggestions made during the 1964 ENDC sessions, urging all states to adhere to the partial test-ban treaty, and advocating negotiations looking toward a comprehensive test ban. 

September 17, 1964. In a message to the ENDC, President 

Johnson points to the cutbacks in production of fissionable materials for weapons that were announced by the United States, the Soviet Union, and the United Kingdom in 1964. 

He says that more concrete proposals for safeguarded and realistic disarmament were made in 1964 than in any other year since before World War II. 

In the ENDC, the Soviet representative declares that the ENDC balance sheet for 1964, like that for previous years, equals zero. He blames the Western powers for this failure. 

The ENDC adjourns. 

In a speech at Rochester, Minnesota, General Norstad again advocates a system of mobile ground and air inspection on both sides of the iron curtain in order to reduce tensions and the possiblility of surprise attack. 

September 29, 1964. Secretary of State Rusk declares that the Chinese Communists might detonate a nuclear device "in the near future. He emphasizes that setting off a device does not mean acquisition of a stockpile of nuclear weapons and modern delivery systems. He also says that the United 

States has fully anticipated the possibility of Peiping's entering the nuclear weapons field and has taken this fully into account in determining its defense posture. 

October 10, 1964. The Second Conference of the Heads of State or Government of Nonaligned Countries, attended by 57 

nations in Cairo October 5-10, issues resolutions that: (1) call upon all states to accede to the limited test-ban treaty, (2) urge the extension of that treaty to include underground tests, (3) appeal to the great powers to reduce their

15. 

military budgets, (4) request the great powers to refrain :: from policies conducive to the dissemination of nuclear weapons, (5) urge the conclusion of a non-dissemination z treaty, (6) recommend the establishment of denuclearized zones in various areas of the world, and (7) call for the withdrawal of foreign troops and the removal of foreign bases. 

October 14, 1964. At a plenary meeting of the Central

Committee of the Communist Party of the Soviet Union, Leonid 

I. Brezhnev is elected First Secretary.

October 15, 1964. The Presidium of the Supreme Soviet appoints Alexey N. Kosygin as Chairman of the Council of 

Ministers of the Soviet Union, October 16, 1964. 11 

Communist China explodes its first atom bomb. It claims it has been forced to conduct nuclear tests because of "the ever increasing nuclear threat posed by the United States. It asserts that its aim is to break the nuclear monopoly the three nuclear powers sought to perpetuate through the limited test-ban treaty, and to eliminate nuclear weapons. It also proposes a world summit conference to discuss the prohibition and destruction of nuclear weapons. 

President Johnson announces that the United States has detected a low-yield test in Communist China. He stresses that it has "limited significance" and that it will not deter the United States from achieving concrete practical steps on the road that leads away from nuclear armaments and war toward a world of cooperation, development and peace. 11 

October 17, 1964. Communist Chinese Premier Chou En-lai sends a message to the heads of government of all countries reiterating the proposal of October 16 for a summit conference of all countries to discuss the banning and elimination of nuclear weapons. 

October 23, 1964. 

Commenting on suggestions for including Communist China in disarmament negotiations, the Department of State says that existing channels of communication with Peiping are sufficient and that the real problem is Peiping's attitude toward disarmament. The Department notes that all militarily significant states must participate in arms control agreements at some stage in the disarmament process if the agreements are to have any real meaning.

-- 
-. 

October 24, 1964. Dr. Homi J. Bhabha, Chairman of 

India's Atomic Energy Commission, states that India might be compelled to manufacture nuclear weapons unless some important and tangible" steps are made toward general disarmament. It 

October 28, 1964. Replying to Chou's note of October 17, President De Gaulle declares that France remains ready at any time to fully participate in any serious negotiations that might be arranged by the "competent and responsible" powers to discuss the problems of disarmament constructively and on a practical basis.

November 1, 1964. The White House announces that 

President Johnson has appointed former Deputy Secretary of

Defense Roswell L. Gilpatric to head a special panel to study new policies to prevent the spread of nuclear weapons. 

November 5, 1964. Replying to Chou's note of October 17,

Ghanaian President Kwame Nkrumah proposes that the original sponsors of the nonaligned conference--Yugoslavia, Indonesia, the United Arab Republic, India, and Ghana--promote a conference among the nuclear powers to consider practical measures for the 'complete abandonment of nuclear weapons. 

November 8, 1964. In a speech before the All-India Congress Committee, Indian Prime Minister Shastri states that India, rather than compete with the great powers in the manufacture of nuclear weapons or accept protection under a 'nuclear umbrella," should "arouse the conscience of the nations against nuclear weapons, especially in the forum of the United Nations. 11 11 

November 22, 1964. An editorial in the Peiping People's 

Daily rejects the suggestion that Communist China participate in a five-nation nuclear dialog. The editorial criticizes the ENDC for not having solved a single question of substance" and declares China will have nothing to do with the ENDC (which it says is within the framework of the United Nations) as long as the United Nations fails to recognize the Chinese People's Republic as the only lawful government of the 

Chinese people. 

November 23-27, 1964. Seventeen Latin American Republics hold a preliminary meeting at Mexico City on the question of denuclearizing Latin America. They unanimously reaffirm that this is their goal, and they set up a Preparatory Commission to study the question and prepare a preliminary draft treaty.

November 27, 1964. Replying to Chou's note of October 

17, Prime Minister Shastri rejects the Chinese proposal and says that "these intricate issues" should be discussed in smaller committees and often by experts. He also declares that the Chinese nuclear explosion of October 16 was conducted "in total defiance of the opinion of the nations of the world and in complete disregard of the health of its innocent inhabitants" and that it should be regarded as "deplorable and retrograde. 

December 4, 1964.

During a press conference in London Prime Minister Shastri says it is important for the nuclear powers to consider how they can guarantee the safety and security of the non-nuclear countries.

December 7, 1964. The Soviet Union submits to the UN 

General Assembly an 11-point memorandum on disarmament. The memorandum repeats the 9 measures proposed by the USSR on January 28. It also proposes the dismantling of foreign bases and the prohibition of the use of nuclear weapons. Commenting on denuclearized zones, the memorandum endorses the January 24 decision of the Ceylonese Government to prohibit foreign ships carrying nuclear weapons from entering Ceylonese ports and foreign aircraft carrying nuclear bombs from landing at

Ceylonese airfields, and encourages other non-nuclear states to prohibit use of their territories for the stationing of nuclear weapons. 

December 8, 1964. Following talks in Washington, President Johnson and U.K. Prime Minister Wilson issue a communique in which they express agreement on the urgency of a worldwide effort to prevent the proliferation of nuclear weapons and of continuing Western initiatives towards arms control and Disarmament.

They discussed existing proposals for strengthening the strategic nuclear defense of the Atlantic Alliance and new British proposals in this field. They agreed that the legitimate interests of the Alliance members should be met and that existing safeguards on the use of nuclear weapons should be maintained and further proliferation should be prevented. 

December 9, 1964. The Soviet Union announces that its military expenditures in 1965 will be 500 million rubles ($555 million) less than in 1964 and says its decision came after learning that the United States planned to reduce its military expenditures.

- 

December 14, 1964. Speaking to the UN General Assembly,

Polish Foreign Minister Rapacki calls for the convening of a conference of all European states, together with the Soviet Union and the United States, to examine the question of 

European security.

December 16, 1964. Reporting to the House of Commons on proposals he made to President Johnson a week ago, Prime Minister Wilson says that the United Kingdom advocates an Allied Nuclear Force with a single authority, comprising all members of the force, to develop policy on all types of nuclear weapons. He also says that the authority should discuss all possible contingencies anywhere in the world that might give rise to the use of nuclear weapons. 

December 22, 1964. Speaking to the House of Commons, Prime Minister Wilson advocates the establishment of a "nonnuclear zone" in the Middle East and an international agreement to prevent the shipment of arms to the area. 

December 28, 1964. Replying to Chou's note of October 

17, Premier Kosygin expresses "complete agreement and support" for a nuclear summit meeting and declares that his Government will do everything it can to make the meeting a success. 

December 30, 1964. In a New Year's greeting to Premier

Kosygin, President Johnson expresses the hope that practical agreements can be reached soon in the urgent area of arms control. He urges steps to limit the spread of nuclear weapons, to attain a worldwide and verified comprehensive test ban, to achieve a cutoff of production of fissionable materials for weapons together with measures to safeguard the peaceful uses of nuclear energy, and to agree to a verified freeze on strategic nuclear delivery vehicles.

Chronology - April 1965 

Medora M. Holm

Ext. 4583 UNCLASSIFIED

APRIL 1965 

I. HIGHLIGHTS 

The principal development for April 1965 was the reconvening of the UN Disarmament Commission April 21, in response to a request from the Soviet Union. 

At the Commission's April 26 meeting Soviet Ambassador 

Fedorenko suggested consideration of the measures proposed in the Soviet memorandum to the UN of December 7, 1964, particularly the elimination of foreign bases and withdrawal of foreign troops, non-dissemination of nuclear weapons, and a conference to draw up a convention prohibiting the use of nuclear weapons.

U.S. Ambassador Stevenson recommended adoption of the proposals the United States had made in the ENDĈ in 1964, particularly those outlined by President 

Johnson January 21. 

Also of importance were Secretary of Defense McNamara's statement that the use of nuclear weapons in Vietnam was not required "in the current situation" (April 26) and President

Johnson's statement that he had never received a suggestion from any Government employee regarding the use of nuclear weapons in Southeast Asia (April 27).

A Franco-Soviet communique of April 30 stated that Foreign Minister Gromyko had agreed with a French proposal to consider the problem of disarmament in a conference of the five nuclear powers.

II. CHRONOLOGY 

April 1, 1965. President Johnson stated that the gas used in Vietnam caused tears and nausea for a few minutes but was non-lethal. He added that if the U.S. military forces were ever going to use poisonous gas, the Commander-in-Chief would have to be informed and authorize its use in advance. 

April 12, 1965. 

UN Secretary-General U Thant called a meeting of the Disarmament Commission for April 21. 

April 16, 1965. The 14th Pugwash Conference on Science and World Affairs met in Venice, April 11-16. The Pugwash

Standing Committee reaffirmed the view that one way to limit the arms race would be to destroy bombers over the next few years, possibly in sequence and beginning with the major powers. It said that steps toward the gradual elimination of foreign bases would make an important contribution to the relief of tension, and it expressed the hope that the test ban could be extended to underground tests and to all countries. It reaffirmed the conclusions of the 13th conference on the spread of nuclear weapons. Expressing concern about the further development and use of chemical and biological weapons, it stated that it appeared impossible to maintain any lasting distinction between incapacitating and lethal weapons. 

It urged adherence to the Geneva Protocol of 1925 by states that had not adhered, and it advocated measures going beyond the Protocol. It suggested an International Year for the 

Preparation of Disarmament (IYPD) as a way of focussing attention on the necessity for disarmament and on ways for executing it safely under international control. vened. 

April 21, 1965. The UN Disarmament Commission recon 

April 26, 1965. Secretary of Defense McNamara stated that the use of nuclear weapons was not required "in the current situation" in Vietnam and that there was no point in speculating on the remote contingency that they might be used.

7 In the Disarmament Commission, Soviet Ambassador Fedorenko charged the United States with having prevented the 19th General Assembly from considering the report of the ENDC, and said that the results of ENDC negotiations had been "completely unsatisfactory." Much of his address was devoted to an attack on the United States for its role in Vietnam.

Ambassador Fedorenko proposed that the Commission examine the collateral measures set forth in the Soviet memorandum of December 7, 1964, particularly the Soviet proposals for the elimination of foreign bases and the withdrawal of foreign troops, non-dissemination of nuclear weapons, and a conference to draw up a convention prohibiting the use of nuclear weapons. He repeated previous Soviet charges that the proposed multilateral nuclear force was a device to equip the Federal Republic of Germany with nuclear weapons.

In reply, U.S. Ambassador Stevenson declared that the objective of U.S. policy on Vietnam was to preserve the freedom and independence of the Vietnamese people. He stated, moreover, that every step taken by the United States to increase its military power since the end of World War II had been in response to the expansionist policies of the 

Communist world. 

Ambassador Stevenson reaffirmed U.S. determination to work for general and complete disarmament, accompanied by the continued growth of international law and effective peacekeeping machinery. He stated that the measures proposed by President Johnson on January 21, 1964, could be taken now and would advance the world on the road to general and complete disarmament.

He noted that during the 1964 ENDC sessions the United States had also submitted a detailed technical plan for verifying a freeze on the production of strategic nuclear delivery vehicles and tabled a working paper on inspection procedures for a fissionable materials production cutoff. 

He regretted that the Soviet Union had not accepted the U.S. proposal for the phased destruction of U.S. B-47 and 

Soviet TU-16 bombers, which would have provided practical experience in disarmament. He pointed out that the 

United States had now begun to unilaterally deactivate and destroy some B-47s and that over 2,000 of these planes would be inactivated or destroyed by mid-1966.

Criticizing the Soviet proposals of December 7, he declared that a number of them were not so much disarmament measures as efforts to bring about strategic redeployment of forces in a way that would benefit the Soviet Union, given its central location. 

He reviewed actions the United States had taken by itself to slow down the arms race, in the hope they would be reciprocated by the Soviet Union: reductions in military expenditures, reductions in planned production of fissionable materials, and the program to reduce the inventory of B-47 bombers. 

April 27, 1965. President Johnson declared that he had never received a suggestion from any Government employee regarding the use of nuclear weapons in Southeast Asia. 

April 28, 1965. At the Disarmament Commission meeting

Lord Chalfont, the U.K. representative, stressed the importance of preventing the spread of nuclear weapons and urged conclusion of a non-dissemination agreement as a matter of "first priority." 

He insisted that his Government's proposals for an Atlantic Nuclear Force did not contemplate the spread of nuclear weapons in any way. Under these proposals, no state not now controlling nuclear weapons would gain control over them. The ANF nuclear forces would be united under a single control system. And each participating Power would retain a veto over the use of any elements of the force.

Lord Chalfont also urged conclusion of a comprehensive test ban as a further measure to contain the spread of nuclear weapons. He said he noted a conspicuous lack of balance in the proposals suggested by the Soviet Union in the meetings of April 26. He wondered whether progress might not be made by combining various proposals: e.g., the proposal to freeze strategic nuclear delivery vehicles with the proposal to destroy certain bombers. And he called attention to the United Kingdom proposal submitted to the ENDC in March 1964 to establish observation posts as a contribution toward peace and stability. 

April 30, 1965. A communique issued following talks in Paris between Foreign Minister Gromyko and French officials stated that Mr. Gromyko had agreed with a French proposal to consider the problem of disarmament in a conference of the five nuclear powers.

In an encyclical letter, Pope Paul VI called upon public officials to do all they could to preserve the peace and to promote negotiations at all levels in order to halt the use of force.

ORIGINAL: ENGLISH RESOLUTION ADOPTED BY THE DISARMAMENT COMMISSION AT ITS 102ND MEETING ON 15 JUNE 1965 The Disarmament Commission, Having considered the report dated 17 September 1964 of the Eighteen-Nation Disarmament Committee submitted to the United Nations Disarmament Commission and to the nineteenth session of the General Assembly, Reaffirming the ultimate and continuing responsibility of the United Nations for disarmament,

Noting with regret that during 1964 despite the efforts made by the Eighteen- Nation Disarmament Committee no specific agreements were reached either on general and complete disarmament or on measures aimed at the lessening of international tension, or halting and reversing the arms race, Deploring that, notwithstanding General Assembly resolutions 1762 (XVII) and 1910 (XVIII), nuclear weapon tests have taken place and also that no agreement has been reached on the "discontinuance of all test explosions of nuclear weapons for all time", which is one of the stated objectives of the partial test-ban treaty, Considering that the memorandum of 14 September 1964 submitted to the Conference of the Eighteen-Nation Disarmament Committee by the delegations of Brazil, Burma, Ethiopia, India, Mexico, Nigeria, Sweden and the United Arab Republic, represents a fair and sound basis for the conduct of negotiations towards removing the remaining differences for the conclusion of a comprehensive test-ban treaty, Convinced that failure to conclude a universal treaty or agreement to prevent the proliferation of nuclear weapons leads to the most serious consequences, Deeply conscious of the urgency of making early progress towards the goal of general and complete disarmament under effective international control and of reaching agreement on measures which would facilitate the attainment of that goal, 65-14563 /...

REF #392 

July 8, 1965

Disarmament Chronology 

May 1965 

HARVARD LAW SCHOOL LIBRARY LOCATION: 

 There is attached the May 1965 chronology of disarmament developments prepared by the Reference 

Research Staff.

These chronologies are for background information purposes only and do not in any way constitute official statements of policy.

Medora M. Holm 

Documents Officer

Room 5666 NS Ext. 2761 

Attachment: 

Chronology - May 1965 UNCLASSIFIED

I. MAY 1965 

HIGHLIGHTS 

The major development for May 1965 was the explosion by 

Communist China of its second atomic bomb (May 14), condemned by the United States and many other nations. 11 The UN Disarmament Commission continued to meet but made little progress. There were suggestions for varying combinations ("package deals") of several collateral measures. The United States, while cautioning against any attempt to link desirable measures in any "package, proposed a program including a cutoff of production of fissionable materials for weapons use, a transfer of fissionable materials to uses other than in weapons, a comprehensive test ban, a freeze of nuclear delivery vehicles, and a non-proliferation agreement (May 17). At the same time, there were expressions of hope that the Commission would not be reluctant to recommend separate measures. The United States said that accepting the view that unless everything could be achieved at once, nothing could be done, would lead to a prolonged stalemate (May 17). 

The Soviet Union pressed for resolutions calling for the elimination of foreign bases, the withdrawal of foreign troops, a ban on use of nuclear weapons, and the convening of a world disarmament conference (May 24, 27). There was considerable support both for calling such a conference and for reconvening the ENDC.

President Johnson signed a bill extending the life of the Arms Control and Disarmament Agency for three more years (May 27).

II. · CHRONOLOGY 

May 4, 1965. In the UN Disarmament Commission,

Ambassador Fahmy, the UAR representative, declared that the Chinese Communists should participate in disarmament negotiations in order to make them meaningful. If it would be difficult to invite them to the ENDC, he thought that the ENDC should function as a preparatory commission for a world disarmament conference, under UN auspices, to which all countries would be invited.

Ambassador Chakravarty, the Indian representative, said this was not an appropriate time for such a conference and reviewed Indian thinking on the question of the spread of nuclear weapons. He proposed the following integrated program: (1) the non-transfer of nuclear weapons or nuclear weapons technology by nuclear powers; (2) an undertaking not to use nuclear weapons against non-nuclear powers; (3) an undertaking through the United Nations to safeguard the security of non-nuclear countries threatened by powers having a nuclear weapons capability or embarking on a nuclear weapons capability; (4) an undertaking by the non-nuclear powers not to acquire nuclear weapons; and (5) tangible progress toward disarmament, including a comprehensive test ban, a complete freeze on the production of nuclear weapons and the means of their delivery, and a reduction of nuclear stockpiles. He felt that the ENDC should consider the problem of proliferation as a matter of high priority, and he urged all nuclear powers to adhere to the limited testban treaty.

May 7, 1965. In the Disarmament Commission, General 

Burns, the Canadian representative, stated that non-nuclear powers having the potential to become nuclear powers should not be expected to abstain forever from developing their own nuclear weapons unless the nuclear powers took concrete steps to eliminate their nuclear weapons and to accord the non-nuclear powers some guarantee of protection against nuclear attack. At the same time, he assured the Commission that Canada had no intention of producing or seeking to acquire nuclear weapons that would make it an independent nuclear power.

He also recommended a "freeze" of the production of the more significant delivery vehicles to accompany the actual destruction of others in order to meet objections that destruction of existing vehicles would be meaningless if they were replaced by other and perhaps more powerful vehicles. 

May 11, 1965. In the Disarmament Commission, Chief

Adebo, the Nigerian representative, suggested that pending conclusion of an agreement on the elimination of bomber aircraft capable of delivering nuclear weapons, or as part of such an agreement, consideration be given to an agreement not to transfer such aircraft to non-nuclear powers.

· · 

May 12, 1965. At the conclusion of its spring meeting, the NATO Ministerial Council expressed regret that so little progress had been made in disarmament. The Ministers said that they would continue to press for negotiations leading to adisarmament under effective international control and that they attached great importance to preventing the spread of nuclear weapons.

May 14, 1965. Communist China exploded its second atomic bomb. As when it set off the first, it again claimed it was developing nuclear weapons for defensive purposes, to cope with U.S. "nuclear blackmail and threats.' It t recalled its proposal for a conference to prohibit and destroy nuclear weapons, and declared again it would never be the first to use nuclear weapons. 

TEX 

11 A spokesman for the Department of State said the explosion was "deeply regrettable" and "in total disregard of the test-ban treaty. He reaffirmed President Johnson's pledge of October 18, 1964, to give nations not seeking national nuclear weapons U.S. support against the threat of nuclear blackmail.

In the Disarmament Commission, India and Japan crit-o icized Communist China. The Indian representative, Mr.

Trivedi, declared that all must have been shocked at the serious damage done to the quest for peace, security, disarmament, and reduction of tension, by this explosion conducted "with impunity and in total disregard" of all the Commission was working for. It was an attack on humanity, he said, and was in defiance of the Bandung declaration of 1955 (signed by Communist China), the General Assembly resolution of November 6, 1962, the partial test-ban treaty, and the

Cairo declaration of October 10, 1964. The Japanese representative, Mr. Matsui, declared that the second Chinese explosion would not only further contaminate the air but also cast "a very dark and evil cloud" over the prospects for disarmament.

May 17, 1965. In the Disarmament Commission, ACDA 

Director Foster suggested a broad program of measures to halt the proliferation of nuclear weapons: a cutoff of production of fissionable materials for weapons use, a transfer of fissionable materials to uses other than in weapons, a comprehensive test ban, a freeze of nuclear delivery vehicles, and a non-proliferation agreement. He stressed that there should be no delay in enacting any one of these measures if

the entire program could not be agreed upon. In this connection, he said acceptance of the view that "unless we can achieve at one time everything that is desirable, we can do nothing that is necessary" would lead to "a continuing stalemate" in disarmament negotiations. 

Regarding the suggestion that the use of nuclear weapons be banned, Mr. Foster observed that until these weapons were eliminated by effective verifiable agreements, they served to deter potential aggressors, and the adoption of an ineffective arms control measure might create an illusion of progress and misunderstanding of the need for more meaningful agreements.

Turning to the Soviet proposals for eliminating foreign bases and withdrawing foreign troops, he pointed out that these were not so much disarmament measures as measures to redeploy forces in a way highly favorable to the Soviet Union. He declared that the United States had deployed forces abroad and established certain bases in response to Soviet threats and aggressive acts after the United States had gone far toward unilateral disarmament following World War II. 

Mr. Foster endorsed local and regional initiatives to turn back the arms race among the smaller military powers, particularly the effort being made by Latin American countries. The United States favored nuclear-free zones, he asserted, as long as they had regard to the wishes of the countries involved and did not upset the military balance. 

He also urged "the earliest possible" resumption of the ENDC. 

May 19, 1965. In a communiqué issued at the conclusion of Indian Prime Minister Shastri's week-long visit to the Soviet Union, the Soviet Union and India confirmed their agreement on the need for general and complete disarmament under strict international control and stressed the importance of partial measures to limit the arms race and reduce international tension. They also expressed agreement that international disputes, including territorial and border disputes, should be settled through peaceful negotiations and not by the use of force.

-- 
-- · 5 · 

May 20, 1965. At a meeting with the Antarctic Policy 

Group, President Johnson said it was pleasing to hear its encouraging reports of peaceful international cooperation. 

He described the Antarctic Treaty of 1959 as "a most valuable tool of international agreement, and a most useful way of freeing Antarctica from destructive confrontations between nations. He reaffirmed U.S. determination to do everything possible to keep the Antarctic region peaceful and free of international hostility. 

May 24, 1965. In a lengthy statement to the

Disarmament Commission, Soviet Ambassador Fedorenko again severely criticized U.S. foreign policy and reiterated 

Soviet proposals for a convention proscribing the use of nuclear weapons, the elimination of foreign military bases, the withdrawal of foreign troops, and the non-dissemination of nuclear weapons. He said that measures to prevent dissemination of nuclear weapons would be ineffective, however, unless access to these weapons was blocked to non-nuclear powers, whether directly or indirectly, or through military alliances. In this connection, he said the greatest possible danger was the plans to establish a NATO multilateral nuclear force. He also strongly supported the convening of an international disarmament conference, declaring that the matter should be settled "hic et nunc. 

In reply, ACDA Director Foster expressed regret that the Soviet Union seemed determined to engage in polemics and false accusations that flew in the face of the consensus that the ENDC should be reconvened without delay, with the apparent intention of getting the Commission bogged down in fruitless debate. Pointing out that the United States did not have a single nuclear warhead in Europe that was not under strict U.S. custody and control, he noted that in the May military parade in East Berlin, apparently in violation of a four-power agreement, some fourteen Sovietmade missiles were displayed in the hands of the East Germans. He said the United States would welcome from the 

Soviet Union a categorical assurance that the warheads for these and other nuclear-capable weapons made available to East Germany and other Eastern European countries were under 

Soviet control.

May 25, 1965. In the Disarmament Commission, Italian 

Foreign Minister Fanfani urged priority treatment by the ENDC of an agreement on the non-dissemination of nuclear weapons, and an early resumption of the ENDC. 

The Commission concluded its general debate with a statement by the Chinese representative, Mr. Hsueh, opposing participation of Communist China in future disarmament negotiations.

May 27, 1965. The President signed the bill extending the life of the Arms Control and Disarmament Agency for three more years. Discussing some "sober truths" of the day, he stated that a full-scale nuclear war might come and go as swiftly as a Washington thunder shower, yet wreak more destruction than a lifetime of earthquake, fire, and flood, and that modern weapons were not merely symptoms of intention, but also a cause of fear and distrust among nations. After reviewing the Agency's accomplishments, he said the government had repeatedly expressed its conviction that an increasing array of nuclear stocks did not insure this nation's security, and that the spread of nuclear weapons to other countries endangered the security of everyone. 

All nations, he asserted, had a common interest in survival, and it was possible to translate that interest into concrete proposals.

In the Disarmament Commission, the Soviet Union introduced resolutions calling for: (1) the eliminatíon of foreign bases and withdrawal of foreign troops, and (2) a convention prohibiting the use of nuclear weapons and, pending its conclusion, a declaration by the nuclear powers that they would not use them first.

May 31, 1965. At a Paris meeting of NATO Defense Ministers, Secretary of Defense McNamara proposed creation of a committee of four or five NATO members to provide for greater Allied participation in NATO nuclear policy. He said the committee should study ways to increase Allied participation and provide quicker consultation regarding the use of nuclear weapons. Quicker consultation could be provided through construction of "hot lines" between 

NATO country capitals.
