Statement by Mr. Pardo, June 12, 19651/

I think that we have made the position of my delegation on the question of a world disarmament conference perfectly clear.

We believe that the idea of convening a world disarmament conference, put forward originally, if I am not mistaken, by the delegation of Ethiopia and subsequently endorsed in Cairo, is imaginative and holds forth promise of progress. 

As the representative of the Soviet Union said this morning, the convening of a world conference could inject new life in disarmament negotiations. As I said a few days ago, however, we do not believe that the mere fact of holding a conference will necessarily produce useful results. Hence the convening of a world disarmament conference is to a certain extent a gamble; it is imperative that the organization and preparation of the conference be studied in all its aspects and that the areas of possible agreement be defined in advance in order that we may have the maximum chances of success. 

Nothing, or at least as little as possible, should in our view be prejudged at this stage. 

The original thirty-six Power draft resolution appeared to my delegation to launch the idea of a world conference on a controversial basis and hence to diminish rather than to enchance the possibilities of success. My delegation would have preferred a very simple resolution reading more or less as follows: 

"The Disarmament Conference, "Noting with approval the proposals made at the present session of the Commission and elsewhere for the convening of a world conference on disarmament with universal participation, 

¹DC/PV. 97 (prov.), p. 51.

26. 

"Recommends that the ENDC and the General Assembly give urgent consideration to these proposals at its twentieth session.

This formulation would, we believe, have reproduced faithfully the opinion of this Commission and would have given maximum possibilities to fruitful negotiations before the General Assembly. It would also have enabled delegations such as that of the Democratic Republic of the Congo to give expression to their desire for a fresh initiative in the disarmament field.

However the process of debate is a process of compromise, and the revised thirty-six Power draft resolution appears to keep open the consideration of important issues which will have to be considered in connexion with the proposed world conference. The revised draft is consequently a great improvement on the original draft.

NETHERLANDS 

Statement by Mr. Polderman, June 12, 1965 1/

With regard to the thirty-six Power draft resolution on the convening of a world disarmament conference (DC/221) my delegation recognizes that there is a widespread desire for the convening of such a conference and we appreciate the fact that the co-sponsors have confined their draft to a certain extent to a procedural recommendation. Nevertheless, my delegation has reservations with regard to the text as it now stands.

While we agree that under certain conditions a world disarmament conference might indeed provide a useful forum for discussions on disarmament, we consider the ENDC the most appropriate organ for conducting meaningful negotiations on concrete measures of disarmament. We fail to see, as yet, how the cause of disarmament would better be served by a conference of 115 or more States than by the painstaking and expert negotiations in the ENDC. These negotiations have actually set in motion, in the opinion of my delegation, the process of disarmament and have produced certain results. 

It would, in the opinion of my delegation, not be correct to assume, as the thirty-six Power draft resolution seems to do, that a world disarmament conference would be an indispensable instrument to set this process in motion. 

If, however, a world disarmament conference were to be held, it would have to be carefully prepared so as to avoid failure, and it would have to be solidly and expressly based on the agreed principles for multilateral negotiations on disarmament unanimously endorsed by the General Assembly in 1961.

The thirty-five Power draft resolution does not sufficiently take into account these essential considerations. 

Besides, it is confined to the goal of general and complete disarmament and omits to mention the importance of collateral measures of disarmament as a means of reaching this goal and of releasing international tensions. 

1DC/PV. 97 (prov.), p 26-27.

-- 
- Most of my delegation's reservations would be entirely met by the amendments presented by the representative of Malta. We fully support these amendments and if they were accepted or adopted we would vote in favour of the draft resolution. If this should not be the case, we should be obliged to abstain from voting on the draft resolution.

- PAKISTAN 

Statement by Mr. Ali, May 21, 1965 1/ My delegation supports the proposal of the second 

Conference of Non-Aligned Countries held in Cairo in

October 1964 for the convening of a world disarmament conference under the auspices of the United Nations, to which all countries of the world would be invited. We endorse the view expressed at the Cairo Conference that the proposed convocation would provide powerful support to the efforts which are being made to set in motion the process of disarmament and for seeking the steady development of this process.

The argument has been advanced that such a world conference would provide no opportunity for doing what is most needed: the finalization of the details of a treaty. It has been said that this involves the negotiation of intricate issues at the level of experts. not persuaded of the force of this argument against the proposal. The signatories of the Declaration of the Cairo 

Conference, and representatives who have advanced the proposal here, did not, we are certain, anticipate that the world disarmament conference would substitute itself for the experts and attempt to write a treaty in a few days. 

What it could do is what has not been done so far. 

We are

First, it could provide an impetus for the success of the disarmament negotiations.

Second, it could bring these negotiations into relation with other political realities which, if left unchanged, militate against disarmament and, thus, continually frustrate these nego tiations. 

Third, it could decide on a system of firm priorities. 

Fourth, it could consider not only the question of the prohibition of the use of nuclear weapons, but also their complete destruction, together with the related components of disarmament, such as conventional disarmament.

Let us not pretend that a treaty is ready in principle and only its details remain to be finalized through discussions at the expert level. Let us recognize that the 

1/ DC/PV. 86 (prov.), p. 31-32.

-- 
-- · 30 · apparent differences over detail are but the symptoms of a deep-rooted divergence, a divergence both of interest and conception. True, we have already had the enunciation of broad principles of disarmament. True, we have the 

Zorin-Stevenson formula. But disarmament, like all major political issues, is a question where agreement on broad principles is not so much the essence of the matter as action to implement them in agreed ways and means. There are several instances in the experience of the world community, especially at the present stage of history, in which two or more parties have adopted an identical text, and yet have violently disagreed regarding its meaning, its implications and what obligations it imposes on them. Disarmament is perhaps the most prominent example. 

While we firmly believe, for all these reasons, that the deficiencies of the disarmament negotiations cannot be overcome except by the stimulus of a world disarmament conference, we are also acutely aware of the desperate necessity of certain measures to which, we believe, these negotiations must immediately be directed.

SPAIN 

Statement by Mr. Aznar, May 20, 1965 2/ The delegation of Spain wishes to express its firm desire to see negotiations concerning the Moscow Treaty expanded and continued. I should also like to point out that we agree with the Cairo Declaration of 10 October 1964, which appealed to all States to adhere to the Moscow Treaty and that, at the same time, emphasized the need for a ban on underground tests. 

IDC/PV.80 (prov.), p. 7 

2DC/PV.85 (prov.), p. 27

The recent nuclear explosion carried out on the territo of the People's Republic of China is additional proof of the urgency of the measures awaited by the whole world. 

Enormous duties weigh on the shoulders of the major Powers since they are instrumental in the taking of all major decisions in this field. But the middle-sized Powers also have clear obligations, as do even the small Powers, since their action, if carefully controlled and sustained, can exert powerful moral pressure and can make a vital contributi to the achievement of our goals.

A ban on underground testing, which is desired by the majority of States, and the general ratification of the Moscow Treaty would be an excellent point of departure and would encourage progress towards further stages. 

There are those who feel that the convening of a world disarmament conference would show us the way to a brighter future. However, my delegation feels that if the present international situation does not radically change, interesting results cannot be expected from such a conference. 

We are not particularly enthusiastic about the idea of constantly increasing the number of bodies debating this issue. The convening of another conference would be tantamount to admitti failure here, failure in the present United Nations efforts.

It would be tantamount to paralysing once and for all the work of the Eighteen-Nation Committee on Disarmament. That

Committee has told us in its last report that, in spite of everything, there is still hope that: "the useful discussions and exchange of views during the period covered by the report will facilitate agreement in the further work of the Committee"

Statement by Mr. Asha, May 13, 1965 1/

In their earnest desire to contribute to peace, the 

Conference of the Heads of State or Government of Non-Aligned 

Countries, which met in Cairo from 5 to 10 October 1964, called for the convening of a world-wide conference on disarmament at the highest level, under the auspices of the United Nations, to which all countries should be invited as equal sovereigns. 

We believe that this world conference should be convened as soon as possible to discuss most seriously among other things the question of the complete banning and destruction of nuclear weapons, with a view to reaching an agreement on the renunciation of the use of these weapons. 

The world conference on disarmament to which I have already referred, in order to be effective, must be universal and must include all nuclear Powers, including the People's 

Republic of China and France. 

Without the participation of the People's Republic of China, whose population exceeds one-fourth of the human race, we have serious doubts that the conference can attain its objective.

We therefore urge those Powers which have political differences with the People's Republic of China to set them aside and to co-operate selflessly in the service of peace and the welfare of humanity.

Statement by Sir Harold Beeley, June 12, 1965 

1/ I wish to say only a few words about the attitude of the United Kingdom delegation to the draft resolution contained in document DC/221/Rev.1. The United Kingdom delegation sympathizes with the main purposes of the sponsors of the draft resolution, which are, as I understand them, to emphasize how important it is that the Government of the Chinese People's Republic should participate in disarmament negotiations at the earliest practicable date, and to set in motion procedures by which their participation could be ensured. 

The United Kingdom Government fully agrees that progress towards disarmament would become progressively more difficult if a Power of the consequence of the Chinese People's Republic were to be indefinitely absent from the negotiations. Thus 

IDC/PV.98 (prov.), p. 2-5

-. there is no difference of principle, and there has not been since the beginning of the discussion of the draft resolution, between my delegation and the delegations which have sponsored the resolution in so far as its central objective is concerned There were however certain problems arising from the language of the draft resolution which caused us some concern. Even in the amended form in which it has been presented this morning by the representative of Yugoslavia, the draft resolution is not entirely satisfactory to the United Kingdon delegation. 

It still appears to us that the draft resolution does not sufficiently acknowledge the very difficult problems involved in the convening of a world disarmament conference and makes no provision for the careful examination of these problems in advance of the decision to which it looks forward. Take, for example, the phrase "all countries" in operative paragraph This phrase conceals a number of problems in various parts of the world, and it will be clear from what I said at the beginning of my statement that so far as the United Kingdom is concerned we are not thinking primarily of the Chinese People's Republic when we draw attention to these problems. 

The establishment of the list of Governments to which invitatio should be sent would in itself be a most difficult operation. 

Another question which would require a great deal of study and negotiation is the agenda to be proposed for the 

Conference. A third and obviously controversial and delicate question is that of the machinery by which the conference would be organized. It does seem to me that the recommendation contained in operative paragraph 2, that the General Assembly give urgent consideration to the proposal at its twentieth session, gives adequate recognition to these difficulties. 

I would prefer the draft resolution to include some provision for the prior study of the problems involved in a world conferen The most important body for this purpose would seem to be the Eighteen-Nation Disarmament Committee and I take note of the remarks which the representative of Malta made this 

---

morning on the subject and of the subsequent comment by the representative of Yugoslavia. Nevertheless, the revision of the text of this draft resolution has removed the most serious objection which the United Kingdom delegation had to the previous text. Consequently, in spite of the remaining misgivings which I have just outlined, my delegation will vote in favour of this draft resolution. 

UNITED STATES 

Statement by Mr. Foster, June 9, 1965 1/ I should now like to comment on the proposal to convene a world disarmament conference document DC/221 and Add.1. 

the proposal contained in

In his statement of 4 June, the representative of Yugoslavia said that the entire debate in this Commission 11 showed that the proposal to convene a world disarmament conference was not a controversial question". (92nd meeting, page 6)

He added that the sponsors of the draft resolution that he was introducing "expect that it will be adopted unanimously" (Ibid., page 11).

I know how assiduously the representative of Yugoslavia has laboured to produce the document now before the Commission. 

However, with all due respect for his conviction on this matter, I must say that neither the record of our discussions nor what we know of the effort that went into the shaping of this draft resolution supports the view that there is nothing controversial about it. 

1DC/PV.95 (prov.), p. 36 - 42

In my earlier statements I observed that there were number of considerations to take into account when examining the advisability of a world conference. So far as the United 

States is concerned, the primary consideration is whether the convening of such a conference in foreseeable circumstances will in fact facilitate negotiations on arms limitation and reduction agreements. I must say that we are far from convinced that this would be the result. On the contrary, we are concerne that, in the absence of positive indications that such a conference would produce useful results, it might only delay the negotiations that are now possible and indeed essential in the Eighteen-Nation Committee. 

Although the eighteen-nation forum has not made the progress we had all desired, it is nevertheless the most promising forum created since the Second World War for disarmament negotiat Its discussions paved the way for several existing agreements, and they have served to lay the groundwork for future agreements which would be instrumental in reversing the arms race. As I have already stated, we are not aware that the participants in the Eighteen-Nation Disarmament Committee feel that their inability to achieve agreements is due to the absence of one or more Governments, or that the difficulties they have encountered will disappear if the forum is modified. 

We believe, accordingly, that nothing should be allowed to interrupt the negotiations under way in the Eighteen-Nation Disarmament Committee. We also believe that those negotiations and the views of those engaged in them cannot be ignored in considering any new disarmament initiative. 

We do recognize that some Governments hope that a world conference might improve the international atmosphere even if it did not result in concrete agreements. But if it met in unpropitious circumstances it would do no more than reflect discord and serve as a propaganda forum for those whose interest is not in promoting disarmament but in propagating an aggressive point of view wholly out of keeping with the hopes and aspirations of a large majority of nations. Thus, it could serve to worsen

- 3 the atmosphere for the disarmament negotiations now in progress; it could result in a hardening of positions rather than in a wider measure of agreement. As a result, the difficulties that have hampered the work of the Eighteen-Nation Committee might be greatly increased.

That would be particularly true if the conference were to call into question the Joint Statement of Agreed Principles which is not just a "resolution made by two Powers", as was suggested here the other day. That resolution was unanimously endorsed by the General Assembly as a guide to disarmament negotiations. In our view, one of the principal defects of the draft resolution contained in document DC/221 and Add.1 is that it does not refer to the Agreed Principles, but it does express the view, in the second paragraph of the preamble, that the conference would formulate proposals and guidelines "in the spirit of the Declaration adopted at the Second Conference of Non-Aligned Countries". Now, that Declaration expresses many sentiments which we whole-heartedly endorse; but it also contains several recommendations to which my Government and many other Governments are opposed. To suggest that the conference should proceed in the spirit of that Declaration could, therefore, be construed as suggesting a new and controversial set of guidelines, notwithstanding the Agreed Principles which were unanimously approved by the General Assembly. If that were the result, it would represent a backward step and might disrupt the course of disarmament negotiations for years to come.

In his statement the other day, the representative of India offered an explanation of the second paragraph of the preamble which we found helpful as far as it went. 

But we cannot understand why it is not possible to mention the Joint Statement of Agreed Principles if in fact it is not excluded by the text before us.

We are aware that many of the proponents of this draft resolution see it as a means of associating militarily significant States with future disarmament negotiations. But 

I must in all frankness ask whether the proposal now before us is appropriately designed to that end and, if so, whether it is likely to succeed.

-- 
- Moreover, it by no means exhausts the possibilities for associating other States with disarmament negotiations. 

In any event, we have already seen that Governments cannot be compelle to join in disarmament negotiations, no matter how often or by what majority they are invited to do so. 

We are all agreed that sooner or later every militarily significant State must participate in disarmament negotiations if we are to go far down the road of disarmament. But until the time comes when such participation can be achieved on a constructive basis, our primary task is to make sure that we avoid any action which would delay or otherwise increase the difficulty of achieving such agreements as are urgently necessa now and are possible even on a more limited basis. 

I know that some representatives feel that a world conference might be useful even if not all Governments chose to attend. But is this really so? How would such a conference differ from the meeting now in progress here? 

This is not to say that our discussions here and the annua debates in the General Assembly are without value. On the contrary, these discussions offer a valuable opportunity to give fresh impetus to the Eighteen-Nation Disarmament Committee negotiations by clarifying points of view and by enabling all participants to add their weight to any concensus that may emerge to guide future negotiations. But the question is whether a world conference would add anything to these discussio For all these reasons the draft resolution set out in document DC/221 presents serious problems for progress in the field of arms control and disarmament. It cannot be supported simply as an effort to achieve unanimity here, because it is clearly designed to have us all affirm a proposal based on the point of view of certain Governments which is obviously not shared by all.

We regret that the text now before us does not take into account the concerns I have mentioned which I know are shared by many here. By referring to the Joint Statement of Agreed 

Principles and to the Eighteen-Nation Disarmament Committee, the amendments proposed by the representative of Malta would meet some of our concerns and, we understand, those of many other delegations. spread support.

We therefore believe they deserve wide- We, the United States, are now, and always will be, ready to participate in any effort which is genuinely directed toward the solution of the many and difficult problems facing the world in seeking meaningful agreements in the field of arms control and disarmament. However, our experience, and that of many other nations which have in the past sought such arrangements, teaches us that there is no shortcut to agreement, no panacea which will cure this ill from which the world suffers and no easy device which will open the door to agreement. Magic will not help us, but unremitting labour can, and has in the past produced some unity.

In the present circumstances we believe that such progress as is now possible can best be made in the Eighteen-Nation 

Disarmament Committee. 

Statement by Mr. Foster, June 12, 1965 1/ 

For the reasons stated in my intervention at our 95th meeting my delegation will abstain on draft resolution DC/211/ Rev.1.

We have always been ready to participate in any effort which held reasonable hope for a solution to the complex problem of disarmament; but we believe that the Eighteen-Nation 

Disarmament Committee holds greater promise for progress than 

IDC/PV.98 (prov.), p.11

- now a world conference. We believe that a world conference may not be the only or best means of associating all militarily significant States in disarmament discussions, and we believe that such a conference may compound rather than solve the problems of disarmament. However, with the revision before us, the main operative paragraph calls for General Assembly consideration of the problem. My delegation has never objected to that. We regret that the draft resolution, as revised, does not refer to the discussions of the Eighteen- Nation Disarmament Committee. We also regret that it does not refer to the Joint Statement of Agreed Principles, which was adopted unanimously by the General Assembly to guide disarmament negotiations in whatever forum they might meet. 

We recognize that the draft resolution no longer calls for a new set of guidelines for disarmament negotiations. 

We find helpful the statements by the sponsors of the draft resolution indicating that they support the Joint Statement of Agreed Principles; but a reaffirmation of these principles would have made their point clearer.

Taking all these factors into consideration, my delegation therefore, will, as I indicated a moment ago, abstain on draft resolution DC/221/Rev.1, which is before us today.

Statement by Mr. Tsarapkin, June 12, 1965 1/

The Soviet delegation, in its first statement on 26 April already drew the attention of the Commission to the proposals of the Cairo Conference of the Heads of State or Government of the Non-Aligned Countries, as well as to the proposal of the People's Republic of China on the convening of a world disarmament conference. DC/PV. 97 (prov.), pp. 3-10, 12-16

-- · · The wide support given by this Commission to the idea of convening such a world disarmament conference is naturally very satisfying to our delegation. We welcome the initiative of the non-aligned States in submitting a draft resolution on this question.

Some of the previous speakers, in referring to the wording of the thirty-six-Power draft resolution, have mentioned the possibility of amending it. The representative of Malta has submitted amendments which have been supported by the Western Bloc, and in particular by the representative of the United States. I therefore believe it is necessary for me to deal with the arguments advanced by Mr. Foster, the representative of the United States.

Mr. Foster objected to the draft resolution on the convening of a world disarmament conference and lanuched a direct attack on this proposal. He stated that he felt the question was not clear as to the usefulness of convening such a conference and that, in solving this particular aspect of the problem it was necessary to take various other questions into consideration. Having advanced this argument, the 

United States representative then proceeded to build all types of speculations, unfounded guesses and assumptions. Mr. Foster began by raising the following question: Will the convening of such a conference in circumstances that can be forseen facilitate negotiations for the reduction of armaments? Having raised such a question, Mr. Foster supplied the answer himself, and in so doing he decided to assume the role of a seer peering into the future. He said, "We are far from being convinced that this would be the result". When Mr. Foster says "We", he undoubtedly means the United States delegation and those delegations that think the same way. But we believe that the overwhelming majority of delegations will not agree with this opinion by Mr. Foster. In fact, this is not an opinion, but a mere guess on his part which no one can take as a serious argument.

What distorts the matter is the argument advanced by 

Mr. Foster that a world disarmament conference can only delay negotiations on disarmament in the Eighteen-Nation Disarmament Committee. It should be noted first that the world disarma ment conference would represent on its own a most valuable

- form of negotiation on disarmament and, one supposes, at a very high level, where it would be possible to discuss and adopt responsible decisions on the important questions of the problem of disarmament, the reduction of the armaments race and the reduction of international tension. 

Secondly, the adoption by our Commission of a decision to convene a world conference on disarmament could inject new life in the atmosphere that has arisen within the Eighteen- Nation Committee, an atmosphere in which it has unfortunately found itself ever since its inception. Thus far the represent atives of the Western Powers in the Eighteen-Nation Committee have not given us any reason to expect that the Committee is the most promising forum for disarmament negotiations. Equall unfounded is the assertion made by Mr. Foster that the convening of a world disarmament conference · and here I quote him "could worsen the atmosphere of disarmament negotiation could lead to a sharpening of positions rather than to a wide agreement, and that, as a result, the difficulties that have thus far impeded the work of the Eighteen-Nation Committee could only increase". 77 

All these fears have been painted and conjured up by Mr. Foster on the basis of the fact that the question of the convening of a world conference does not fit in with, and is not made dependent upon, the timely adoption by its participants of agreed principles, as a basis, as a guiding principle for the work of that conference. 

Mr. Foster also said that the negotiations on disarmament took place in the Eighteen-Nation Committee and that the views of the participants in those negotiations could not be ignored in considering any new initiative in the field of disarmament. 

This observation on the part of the representative of the United States is also unfounded in that no one intends to ignore the views of the members of the Eighteen-Nation Committee.

But it should be noted that if we do not want our views to be ignored then we, in our turn, should not ignore the views of others. And here I am referring to Mr. Foster's reference to the fact that a particular danger is represented

-- 
- by the possibility that the world conference on disarmament would question the agreed principles for disarmament negotiations. We are not clear here. Why has the representative of the United States decided that a world conference should devote itself to the question of reviewing agreed principles? 

Such a conference will deal with the problem of disarmament, with the problem of stopping the armaments race, and with questions designed to reduce international tension. 

We, Mr. Foster, do not see the grounds for such fears on your part, nor do we see any reason to make the adoption of agreed principles a pre-condition for the participation of States in a world conference on disarmament, the more so since there are States which are not taking part in the working out of these principles and which should take part in the world conference. The States could adhere to such principles, but no one has a right to impose these principles on them.

In this connexion, the Soviet delegation would like to draw the Commission's attention to the fact that in this given case we are speaking of a world conference and not of the usual type of international conference of States where, as is quite often the case, the participants in such a conference agree among themselves on some guiding principles as a preliminary action. But the world conference on disarmament is not one of these cases, not one of the cases referred to by Mr. Foster when he spoke about the desirability of placing the world conference on disarmament within the framework of definite conditions and putting before the future participants in that conference a choice: If you want to take part in the world conference on disarmament then you must declare beforehand that you accept the demands made by the delegation of the United States. You, Mr. Foster, would like to convene the world conference on the basis of conditions agreed to beforehand. But this is an unjustified claim on your part and you, Mr. Foster, have no grounds, nor have you any convincing arguments, in favour of putting the question in such a way, not to mention the fact that it is inadmissible to make any such demands in advance. Only the victor can impose such demands on the vanquished. But it is not admissible when we are dealing with the world conference of sovereign States called upon to deal with the problem of disarmament.

· - 

However, as far as I understood, Mr. Foster, it was not really a question of approach or conditions; the main questio was somewhere else. In fact, Mr. Foster, the representative of the United States, did not raise so much a question of conditions on which in his opinion such a world-wide conferen should be convened, but, rather, raised the question of the usefulness of convening such a conference at all, something which I have already mentioned before. 

The representative of the United States, Mr. Foster, speaking on 9 June, expressed misgivings, saying that, in the absence of positive indications that such a conference would lead to useful results, that conference could in fact impede disarmament negotiations. But what positive indications did Mr. Foster have in mind? What do you want, Mr. 

Foster? Do you know now the positions of the participants?

Do you know in advance that in these positions there will be nothing positive at all? 

Perhaps in this case Mr. Foster is referring to the position of the United States itself. This, of course, we are quite prepared to admit. Perhaps these pronouncements of Mr. Foster should be interpreted to mean that, before the convening of the conference, an agreement should be concluded in the lobbies concerning the results of the conference, and that the conference itself would in fact play the role of a universal carnival or a fair, in which, in the appropriate pompous atmosphere, only signatures would be affixed or a formal vote would be taken somewhere on a text agreed upon in advance.

But that is not what we have in mind when we propose the convening of a world conference on disarmament. What we have in mind are negotiations at the conference itself in a spirit of constructive discussions and with the will to arrive at an agreement.

- 

In the light of those facts, it is quite obvious that the United States would like to transfer that practice which is so promising from the point of view of fruitlessness to the world disarmament conference. The United States representative spoke the day before yesterday with the same ardour against the convening of a world disarmament conference that would take into account the Declaration adopted by the 

Cairo Conference of Heads of State or Government of Non- 

Aligned Countries. He frankly admitted that his Government was against many of the recommendations in the Cairo Declaration, regarding them as backward steps in comparison with the so-called Agreed Principles. But the Soviet delegation sees no contradiction between the Agreed Principles and the provisions of the Cairo Declaration. It therefore cannot agree with the views expressed by the United States representative, Mr. Foster.

In our opinion, all the observations made by the United States representative on the draft resolution submitted by thirty-six non-aligned Powers are dictated by one consideration only: not to allow, or at any rate to impede, the convening of a world disarmament conference in which all the States of the world would participate.

Mr. Foster himself shed light on that when, in his statement at the meeting on Wednesday, he said that sooner or later every militarily significant State would have to participate in disarmament negotiations if we truly wished to make progress. To that end Mr. Foster proposed that certain States should not be invited to participate in the disarmament negotiations until there could be certainty that their participation would be constructive. In other words, the United 

States representative apparently believes that the participation of certain States in the disarmament negotiations at this stage would not be constructive, that those States must wait to participate until Mr. Foster and his Government deign to change their views on the matter. 

It is because the United States does not wish to allow certain States to participate in the disarmament negotiations that the United States representative made the observations which he made concerning the draft resolution of the thirtysix non-aligned States. He even made the bizarre statement that the draft resolution would create serious problems as regards progress in control over disarmament.

- The best answer to criticisms leveled by Mr. Foster at the proposal to convene a world conference on disarmament is given by the statement of the representative of Ethiopia: "Therefore, it seems to us that it would be better for those who are in disagreement with this draft resolution to say, 'We do not want other States to participate and therefore we are against the 

Conference', rather than their saying that all kinds of proposals should be mentioned in the draft resolution."

As regards the amendments submitted by the delegation of Malta to the draft resolution, they merely reproduce what has already been stated by the representative of the United States. The Soviet delegation believes that these amendments are designed greatly to worsen the draft resolution, and without any doubt these amendments would create serious difficulti for the convening of a world conference on disarmament or even would make it completely impossible to convene such a conference. Without any doubt, we shall vote against these amendments if they are ever put to a vote here. One cannot fail to say that delegations which might wish to lend their support to these amendments would, by doing so, undermine the efforts designed to convene a world disarmament conference. 

If as a result of such manoeuvres the cause of the world disarmament conference would be harmed, the responsibility would lie on those who supported these amendments. 

Before the beginning of this meeting we heard unofficiall that the amendments put forward by the delegation of Malta had been withdrawn. If that is the case, then undoubtedly this would have a beneficial and positive influence on the further discussion of this question.

The Soviet delegation for its part believes that in the search for solutions to the problem of disarmament and to the solution of limiting the armaments race and reducing international tension, the draft resolution submitted by the thirty-six non-aligned States, calling for the convening of a world disarmament conference could play a great substantial and positive part. The Soviet delegation will vote in favour of that proposal. 

---

53. 

YUGOSLAVIA Statement by Mr. Lekic, May 7, 1965 1/ 

Many countries have supported the request of the Cairo Conference for the convening of a world conference on disarmament. There is no reason for other countries not to do the same, since the initiative of the non-aligned countries is based solely on the interests of peace and international security. The Cairo Conference adopted a resolution calling for a world conference on disarmament before the process of relaxation of international tensions had been interrupted by the use of force in various parts of the world. This means that the non-aligned countries were not satisfied even at that time because of the lack of progress in disarmament and that they considered the common efforts of all countries would most effectively contribute to and accelerate this process of relaxation. The developments in the international situation following the Cairo Conference are only further confirmations of the wisdom of this request.

The convening of a world conference assumes greater importance at this precise time, when the world is faced with the problem of putting an end to the use of force and of finding common language for a peaceful solution of present problems and for ensuring co-operation among nations. A world disarmament conference would undoubtedly be the most suitable way of summing up the efforts made so far and of determining clear guidelines for solving the disarmament problem.

That does not mean, however, that reaching various agreements on disarmament depends upon the convening of a world conference, or that a world conference excludes the holding of special conferences on measures that are ripe for agreement; that is also pointed out in the Cairo Declaration. Also, the convening of a world conference would not be in contradiction with any other efforts to advance the consideration of the disarmament problem from its present position of standing still. In the examination of this demand, Yugoslavia is ready to support whatever approach is most widely acceptable. 

It would be useful in this respect if the Commission were to make the appropriate recommendations to the General Assembly. 

1/ DC/PV. 76 (prov.), pp. 54-56

In view of the importance which it attaches to the disarmament problem, the Government of the Socialist Federal Republic of Yugoslavia feels that at this moment it is necess to issue a special memorandum on this question; that memorand is being circulated as a Commission document. The memorandum explains the views of the Yugoslav Government on approaching the disarmament problem and presents a programme of a minimum number of measures which can serve as a basis for the initiation of action in the field of nuclear disarmament. We hope that this memorandum will be of help to the Commission in its work.

I can assure the Commission that the Yugoslav delegation will spare no effort necessary for achieving concrete results in our work, in order that the process of disarmament may begin and that we may ensure a lessening of tensions and the stabilization of international relations. That is our sacred responsibility to mankind. * **** * * * * 

Statement by Mr. Lekic, June 4, 1965 1/ 

It is a great honour for me to introduce the draft resolution on the convening of a world disarmament conference, issued as document DC/221 and sponsored by Burma, Burundi,

Cambodia, Cameroon, Central African Republic, Ceylon, Congo 

(Brazzaville), Cyprus, Ethiopia, Ghana, Guinea, India, Iraq, Jordan, Kenya, Kuwait, Lebanon, Liberia, Libya, Malawi, Morocco, Nepal, Nigeria, Saudi Arabia, Somalia, Sudan, Syria,

Tunisia, Uganda, United Arab Republic, United Republic of Tanzania, Yugoslavia and Zambia. 

As is well known, at the Second Conference of Non-Aligned 

Countries, held in Cairo in October 1964, forty-seven Heads of State or Government, with the concurrence of eleven observers, expressed their conviction that a world disarmament conference ..would provide powerful support to the efforts which are being made to set in motion the process 1/ 

DC/PV. 92 (prov.), pp. 2-6, 7-11

. 

of disarmament and for securing the further and steady development of this process". 

The Second Conference of Non-Aligned Countries came to the conclusion that the state of international relations, particularly in the field of disarmament, was such that extraordinary efforts would be necessary on the part of all countries of the world to halt the armaments race and to begin the process of real disarmament.

It is understandable that at the Cairo Conference for the first time in history the need for world-wide action was stressed. The entire course of post-war development, and, in particular, the continuing expansion of the nuclear arms race, has reached a point at which the international community is confronted with two most serious alternatives: self-destruction or peaceful and active co-existance among all States and peoples of the world. Furthermore, not only is there an imperative need to unite the efforts of all the countries of the world in undertaking speedy measures towards general and complete disarmament under effective international control, but also the time is ripe to do so. A universal forum based on the equal participation of all countries, such as was proposed by the Cairo Conference, would be in a position to formulate proposals and the guidelines for speeding up the solution of this problem. Such measures are prerequisites not only for the freeing of mankind from the constant danger of self-destruction, but also for contributing to the speedy and unhindered political, social and economic progress of the world.

Debate in this Commission has recognized the importance of this initiative. It can, in fact, be stated with satisfaction that the entire debate showed that the proposal to convene a world disarmament conference was not a controversial question. The proposal of the Cairo Conference on the convening of a world disarmament conference has, quite naturally, met with the wholehearted support of the non-aligned countries in this Commission. The Cairo proposal was also welcomed and endorsed by socialist countries: the USSR, Poland, Czechoslovakia, Hungary, Romania, Bulgaria, the Byelorussian SSR, the Ukrainian SSR and Mongolia. The proposal also met with support or understanding on the part of a number of other delegations.

During the debate some delegations mentioned the useful role which could be played by the ENDC in the preparation of a world disarmament conference. We also see in this the interest which the proposal of the Cairo Conference has aroused so far.

Any suggestion about how to facilitate the solution of the manifold questions involved in the convening of the conference is most welcome and, at the proper time, should be thoroughly considered. The sponsors of the draft resolution, however, in approaching the General Assembly directly, base their position primarily on the Cairo Conference resolution urging the participating countries to take, at the forthcoming session of the General Assembly, all the necessary steps for the holding of such a conference.

The participants in the Cairo Conference, furthermore, considered that a direct approach to the General Assembly, with the proposal which forty-seven Heads of State or Government had adopted unanimously, would be the most logical way of speeding up the solution of the problem. Naturally, this does not exclude any other assistance which might be lent to these efforts.

In conclusion, I should like to say a few words concerning the text of the draft resolution. This text was evolved during numerous consultations with regional and other groups, as well as with a number of individual delegations. 

The draft is composed of two preambular and two operative paragraphs. In the first paragraph of the preamble the paramount importance and the universal character of the problem of disarmament are emphasized; this has been recognized unequivocally in the course of the debate in this Commission. 

The Second paragraph of the preamble stresses the importance that a world disarmament conference would have for setting in motion the process of disarmament, and it expresses the conviction that the guidelines and the proposals which the conference would adopt would hasten the completion of general and complete disarmament.

Operative paragraph 1, in pointing out that the Disarmament Commission:

-  "affirms the proposal adopted at the Second Con ference of Non-Aligned Countries in October 1964 

for the convening of a world disarmament conference to which all countries would be invited", in effect reflects the support given to this proposal in the course of the Commission's debate. 

Finally, in operative paragraph 2, the Disarmament Commission: "recommends that the General Assembly give urgent consideration to the above-mentioned proposal at its twentieth session".

The submission of the draft resolution by such a large number of delegations demonstrates the readiness of the Member States of the United Nations to take steps to overcome gradually the serious crisis with which the Organization is faced. The adoption of this draft resolution would contribute to the strengthening of the prestige of the United Nations and would confirm once again that it is an irreplaceable instrument of peace and international co-operation. Furthermore, we are convinced that in the light of the aggravation of the world situation, the adoption of the draft resolution and the mobilization of the entire international community in seeking ways to initiate the process of disarmament would have positive implications for the future development of international relations and for the peaceful solution of major international problems.

Statement by Mr. Lekic, June 12, 1965 1/ 

We are most pleased that even at this stage of the debate the proposal to convene a world disarmament conference has met with further understanding and support and that the sponsors may expect draft resolution DC/221 to be adopted by an overwhelming majority.

However, in commenting on that draft resolution, which recommends that the General Assembly should give urgent consideration at its twentieth session to the proposal for the convening of a world disarmament conference, some delegations have expressed certain reservations. 

I am authorized to express, on behalf of the sponsors of draft resolution DC/221, our gratitude to all those delegations which, acting in good faith, have attempted to contribute to the improvement of the text, both by further elaborating the basic concepts of the sponsors and by making it possible, through minor changes, for a wider number of delegations to go along with the proposed draft resolution. 

The sponsors have considered in great detail all the suggestions and comments that have been made in the course of the debate, and, having consulted with a number of other delegations, they have decided, in a spirit of co-operation, to make certain changes in the present text. It is the sincere desire of the sponsors that the proposal should meet with unanimous support. The changes appear in the second paragraph of the preamble and paragraph 1 of the operative part of the draft resolution. 

In the second paragraph of the preamble, the sponsors have agreed to delete the following words: "by formulating proposals and guidelines in the spirit of the Declaration adopted at the Second Conference of Non-Aligned Countries". 

1/ DC/PV. 97 (prov.), pp. 48-50

-- 
- From the very beginning it has been the basic intention of the sponsors to evolve a text that would enable all the countries of the world to endorse the idea of a world disarmament conference and to take an active part in it. In changing this paragraph, the sponsors have been particularly concerned with taking into consideration the formal technical difficulties in the way of supporting the original text of the draft resolution experienced by those delegations whose Governments did not take part in the Cairo Conference.

The sponsors considered that in making the change they have also taken into account the views of the representative of Malta, whose remarks are reflected in the proposed amendments contained in document DC/223. 

Having eliminated the above-mentioned phrase, the sponsors felt it necessary to add, after the words "Convinced that a world disarmament conference", the words "as proposed by the 

Second Conference of Non-Aligned Countries", in order to link the operative part with the preamble to the draft resolution. 

The revised second paragraph of the preamble thus reads 

"Convinced that a world disarmament conference, as proposed by the Second Conference of Non-Aligned

Countries, would provide powerful support to the efforts which are being made to set in motion the process of disarmament and for securing the further and steady development of this process, with a view to speeding up general and complete disarmament under effect international control and thus contributing to the relaxation of international tension". 

With regard to paragraph 1 of the operative part, the sponsors have decided to take into account the views of those delegations which, for various reasons linguistic, technical and logical consider that the word "Affirms" should be replaced by the word "Welcomes". 

By revising the second paragraph of the preamble and paragraph 1 of the operative part, the sponsors earnestly hope that the representative of Malta will, in a similar spirit of conciliation and understanding, withdraw his amendments.

· 

In closing, I should like to express the conviction of the sponsors that, with the introduction of the above-mentioned changes in the text of the draft resolution, thus accomodating the broadest possible number of delegations, the way has been cleared for the unanimous adoption of the draft resolution (DC/22 /Rev.1).

HARVARD LAW SCHOOL LIBRARY Disarmament Chronology 

July-August 1965

There is attached the chronology of disarmamen developments for July and August prepared by the

Reference Research Staff. 

These chronologies are for background information purposes only and do not in any way constitute official statements of policy.

As stated. 

Lorraine S. McCottry

Room 1882 NS 

Ext. 2568 UNCLASSIFIED

JULY 

AUGUST 1965 

I. 

HIGHLIGHTS 

The ENDC convened at Geneva on July 27, 1965. ACDA 

Director Foster tabled the U.S. draft anti-proliferation treaty on August 17. Under article I of the treaty countries now having nuclear weapons undertake not to transfer such weapons, either directly or indirectly through military alliances, into the national control of non-nuclear states, and not to assist such countries to manufacture nuclear weapons. Article II imposes corresponding obligations upon the non-nuclear countries; they may neither request nor receive nuclear weapons by transfer from the nuclear powers, either directly or indirectly through alliances. Non-nuclear countries are also prevented from manufacturing nuclear weapons, and from receiving or granting assistance in such manufacture. Articles I and II of the draft treaty prohibit both nuclear and non-nuclear states from engaging in actions "which would cause an increase in the total number of States and other Organizations having independent power to use nuclear weapons.

Soviet Ambassador Tsarapkin on August 31 rejected the U.S. draft treaty. Claiming that there was nothing in the treaty to prevent West Germany from gaining control over nuclear weapons under projected NATO multilateral forces agreements, he accused the United States and the United Kingdom of "dishonesty" and wanting to "have their cake and eat it.' The United Kingdom supported the draft treaty, but Lord Chalfont noted on August 19 that it left open "one possibility" for proliferation that the British "would prefer to be closed, i.e., articles I and II did not rule out the possibility that an association of states might make the decision to use nuclear weapons by a majority vote, without the veto of an existing nuclear power. 11 During July and August top Indonesian officials repeatedly declared that Indonesia would explode an atomic device "soon, very probably before the end of the year. They also set forth their views regarding the proliferation of nuclear weapons.

At the ENDC on August 17 the United Arab Republic proposed extending the limited test ban treaty to prohibit underground explosions greater than seismic magnitude 4.75 and that the nuclear states voluntarily declare a moratorium on all underground tests pending conclusion of a comprehensive test ban treaty.

II. 

CHRONO LOGY 

July 10-15, 1965. The communist-directed World Peace 

Congress, convened at Helsinki, adopted a resolution on Vietnam and made a general statement advocating, among other things, the destruction of all nuclear weapons and the convening of a world congress of heads of state and governments to discuss this problem, the creation of a system of collective security in Europe to counter West German territorial claims in the East, and the creation of nuclear-free zones in Central Europe, Scandinavia, the Balkans, and the Mediterranean areas. 

July 13 1965. Lord Chalfont, British Disarmament

Minister, delivered a talk to the Foreign Press Association in London in which he stressed the extremely urgent need at the present time for a nuclear weapons non-dissemination aggreement between the powers. "The principal and most urgent problem that faces us, he declared, is the "problem of the further spread of nuclear weapons. He observed that this most critical problem, which had been seriously aggravated by the crisis in Vietnam, had been even further exacerbated within the last few months by Communist China's two nuclear explosions in the atmosphere. These explosions, he said, had put great pressures upon the Indian government to undertake a nuclear weapons program as a protective measure. If the 

Indians should undertake such a project, Lord Chalfont thought, Pakistan would not long delay in developing its own nuclear capacity. "Once this process has started," he observed, "there is no limit to it..."

July 24, 1965. Speaking at a religious rally in Bandung, President Sukarno said that Indonesia would make its own atomic bomb "in the near future" and warned that the Indonesians would use the bomb "if our country is disturbed or attacked." 

July 25, 1965. President Sukarno, addressing a political convention, stated that it might be necessary, in the struggle between the newly emerging states and the imperialist powers, to use the atomic bomb against the imperialist forces. "If necessary," he stated, "we will seize power with an atomic bomb." 

July 26, 1965. At the NATO Council meeting held in Paris the British announced that they had a tentative draft of a non-proliferation agreement but agreed not to table their proposals at Geneva until sufficient agreement had been reached

among the NATO states as to the kind of non-dissemination treaty needed which would not only satisfy the requirements of the Western powers but also be acceptable to the Soviets. 

July 27, 1965. The ENDC convened at Geneva. In a message to the delegates President Johnson declared that the American delegation was instructed to seek to achieve thes objectives at Geneva: (1) "agreements that will limit the perilous spread of nuclear weapons, and make it possible for all countries to refrain without fear from entering the nuclear arms race, (2) "effective limitation of nuclear weapons and nuclear delivery systems... " and (3) a "truly comprehensive test-ban treaty. 11 11 July 27 1965. In a message to the World Congress 

Against Atomic and Hydrogen Bombs, convened at Tokyo during the last week in July, President Sukarno called upon the "anti-imperialist" states to arm themselves with nuclear weapons. "Nuclear weapons in the hands of the new, emerging forces are weapons for the defense of peace and freedom," he declared, "while on the contrary, nuclear weapons in the hands of the imperialists are weapons for war and oppression." 

Brigadier General Hartono, chief of Indonesian army ord nance, stated at a press interview that Indonesia would very probably explode its first atomic bomb following the second 

Afro-Asian conference, slated to convene at Algiers in November 1965. 

July 29, 1965. Lord Chalfont, British Disarmament

Minister, stated in his maiden speech before the ENDC that, although a comprehensive nuclear test ban, a freeze of strategic nuclear weapons delivery vehicles, and a reduction of existing nuclear stockpiles were all matters of high priority, the negotiation of a nuclear weapons non-dissemination agreement was the most urgent and crucial matter now before the conference. He read a message from Prime Minister Wilson saying that the "negotiation of a non-dissemination treaty is the most urgent problem facing us in the disarmament field.'

One procedure which might facilitate agreement on a feasible non-dissemination pact, Lord Chalfont stated, would be to negotiate it concurrently with a comprehensive test-ban proposal and an agreement by the major powers to reduce existing nuclear stockpiles, since the three issues were closely related. 

Agreement on the two latter projects "would be most desirable, 11

ic Et he said, "but in our view the most urgent requirement of all is a non-dissemination agreement." 

July 29, 1965. At the ENDC, Italian Foreign Minister 

Fanfani reminded the delegates that it was extremely urgent that the powers reach a non-dissemination agreement as soon as possible. 

He warned that non-nuclear states were becoming increasingly worried about their security, and that if a non-dissemination pact was not negotiated "within a reasonable time" his government reserved the right to appeal directly to the non-nuclear countries to act on their own initiative. He then outlined a method by which the non-nuclear states could bring pressure on the nuclear powers to reach agreement on non-dissemination. 

Under the Italian plan, certain non-nuclear states, especially those approaching nuclear capacity, would voluntarily fix a period during which they would unilaterally forego taking further steps toward attaining nuclear capacity. At the termination of this period, however, if the nuclear powers had not yet reached agreement banning further proliferation of nuclear weapons, these non-nuclear countries would regain their freedom of action. 

August 2, 1965. President Sukarno told a Moslem convention that although Indonesia would soon have an atomic bomb, it would not be used for aggressive purposes. 

August 4 1965. Dr. Subandrio, Indonesian Foreign 

Minister and First Deputy Prime Minister, stated that his country had no objection if every state possessed atomic and nuclear bombs, as this would guarantee that such weapons would then not be used to threaten another country. "The more countries that are in possession of atomic and nuclear bombs," he declared, "the stronger will be the guarantees that these weapons of the modern world will not be used." According to the Indonesian Foreign Minister, atomic and nuclear bombs were a threat only to the states which did not have them and did not pose a danger to the countries which already possessed such weapons. "In this connection," he observed, "we have no objection to all countries having atomic and nuclear bombs." 

August 8 1965. Pope Paul VI urged mankind to renounce forever use of atomic weapons and prayed that men would "no longer place their trust, their calculations, and their prestige in such fatal and dishonoring weapons." Marking the 20th anniversary of the destruction of Hiroshima on August 6, 1945, which he termed "an outrage to civilization, "the Pontiff stated that "we pray that that homicidal weapon may not have killed peace in the world; may not have injured forever the honor of science, and that it has not extinguished the serenity of life on this earth."

. 

Referring to the ceremonies at Hiroshima on August 6 commemorating the victims of the atomic holocaust in that city, the Pope observed that "the terror, the ruin, the depredation, the pain, and the pity aroused by that tremendous event have justly been commemorated," and stated that "we have many times and in various ways urged a ban on atomic weapons. 

August 17, 1965. At the ENDC Mr. Foster tabled the

U.S. draft antiproliferation treaty. In a statement to the 

Committee announcing the treaty President Johnson declared that both he and President Kennedy regarded the spread of nuclear weapons as the "gravest of all unresolved human issues" and urged the delegates to take prompt action to halt the further dissemination of such weapons. "I speak for all my countrymen in reaffirming our conviction that the peace of the world requires firm limits upon the spread of nuclear weapons, "the President stated, "this has been the policy of the United States for 20 years; the policy is still as right as ever... President Eisenhower and President Kennedy sought, as I seek now, the pathway to a world in which serenity may one day endure; there is no sane description of a nuclear war; there is only the blinding light of man's failure to reason with his fellow man, and then silence."

Article I of the U.S. draft treaty prohibited states now possessing nuclear weapons "to transfer any nuclear weapons into the national control of any non-nuclear State, either directly, or indirectly through a military alliance," and forbade nuclear states "to take any other action which would cause an increase in the total number of States and other Organizations having independent power to use nuclear weapons. 

The article also forbade nuclear states from assisting nonnuclear states "in the manufacture of nuclear weapons. 

Article II prohibited states which do not now possess nuclear weapons from manufacturing, or acquiring through transfer or other means, such weapons. It also forbade non-nuclear states from requesting, receiving, or granting assistance in the manufacturing of nuclear weapons, and prohibited such states from requesting or receiving the transfer of nuclear weapons into their national control, either directly, or indirectly through a military alliance. 

Finally, Article II prohibited the non-nuclear states from taking "any other action which would cause an increase in the total number of States and other Organizations having independent power to use nuclear weapons.

-- 
--  

Article III of the treaty obligated all signatory states 

"to cooperate in facilitating the application of International 

Atomic Energy Agency [TAEA7 or equivalent international safe guards on all peaceful nuclear activities." 

August 17, 1965. Mr. Hassan, UAR delegate to ENDC, recommended that the 1963 limited test ban treaty be extended to prohibit underground explosions greater than seismic magnitude 4.75, "which the admitted capacities and capabilities of the existing national equipments are able to detect and identify, as a further step towards the total ban of all underground tests." He also proposed, "as a complementary measure to the preceding one," that the nuclear Powers declare a "voluntary moratorium" on all further testing until a comprehensive test ban treaty is enacted. 

August 19, 1965. Speaking before the ENDC Lord Chalfont stated that under Articles I and II of the U.S. draft antiproliferation treaty there was a possibility, at least theoretical, of proliferation. The British criticism of the two articles was that they did not prevent the possibility "that an association of States might be set up with the capacity to make use of nuclear weapons by the decision of a majority of its members: in other words, without the veto of an existing nuclear Power." Lord Chalfont added that the United

Kingdom was "irrevocably opposed" to any project or formulation "which might have the effect of making the use of nuclear weapons subject to a majority vote" and that his government desired to interpret dissemination "in the strictest possible way. Nevertheless, he stated that the "tabling of this text has our full support" and that at last the Conference had a draft treaty "upon which we can begin to get down to the serious business of negotiation." 

August 23, 1965. At a press conference with Japanese journalists, President Sukarno said that preparations for 

Indonesia's first atomic explosion were proceeding "smoothly" and that the only thing he could say now was "wait and see."

He also repeated his comment of July 27 regarding nuclear weapons.

August 31, 1965. It was reported that the United States had notified Indonesia that it was willing to extend the 1960 atoms-for-peace agreement for five more years, provided Indonesia agreed to accept periodic inspection of its reactor by the International Atomic Energy Agency in order to insure its use for peaceful purposes exclusively.

. 

August 31, 1965. At the ENDC the Soviet Union rejected the U.S. draft non-dissemination treaty of August 17. Soviet 

Ambassador Tsarapkin declared that the U.S. draft treaty was unacceptable since it would not prevent West Germany from indirectly getting access to nuclear weapons under MLF or ANF arrangements now under consideration by the NATO powers. ACDA/RS: JWSyphax: saw 11/3/65

Resolution on Non-Proliferation of Nuclear Weapons

Soviet Union voted in favor of the resolution.

UNITED STATES MISSION 

рж RS-1) 

FOR RELEASE ON DELIVERY TO THE UNITED NATIONS CHECK TEXT AGAINST DELIVERY Press Release 4715 

November 23, 1965

Statement by Ambassador Arthur J. Goldberg, United States 

Representative to the United Nations, in Committee I, concerning the proposed World Disarmament Conference. + + + + + + + + + + + + + + + + + + + + + + + + + + + + + + + + + I have followed with close attention the debate in the First Committee and, in particular, the discussion concerning the proposal to convene a world disarmament conference. Since we are now nearing a vote, I do not intend to take the Committee's time to restate the position of the United States in any detail. 

As Mr. Foster made clear in his statement of November 18, the desire of the United States to achieve early progress in arms limitations and reductions, particularly in the nuclear field, has been demonstrated by a series of concrete, practical proposals. 

These proposals are now before the Eighteen Nation Disarmament Committee in Geneva, and we strongly believe that nothing should be allowed to dalay or interrupt the process of negotiations at Geneva. We have noted with satisfaction that the sponsors of the resolution now before us take the same view. 

We have also stated frankly our reservations concerning the utility of a world disarmament conference convened in present circumstances. We maintain those reservations. But we also note, as the distinguished representative of Algeria pointed out yesterday, that the resolution now before us would, in effect, have us decide only in principle to convene a conference. The actual decision remains to be taken in the light of the results of the consultations and preparations called for in this resolution.

Mr. Chairman, I should like to take this opportunity to express our appreciation to the sponsors for having added a new operative paragraph three which assures that all countries will be kept informed of the results achieved by the Preparatory Committee. Although we still believe that the resolution could have been improved with a few other changes, we feel that the new operative paragraph three goes a long way to meet our concerns.

Let me turn now to the consultations called for by the second operative paragraph of this resolution with a view to establishing a preparatory committee. In normal circumstances we would expect to see greater precision and more detail in an operative paragraph of this nature. We all understand, however, why the sponsors have preferred to formulate this paragraph in very general terms.

OLDBERG 

-2- Nevertheless, this paragraph deals with a very important matter. As a number of speakers have already stressed, a conference of the kind herein proposed must be carefully prepared if it is to have a reasonable chance of producing constructive results. We are certain that many governments share the view of the United States that they must reserve their decision as to participation in the proposed world conference until they can consider the recommendations prepared by a preparatory committee regarding the agenda, timing, finances, participation, and similar questions relating to the proposed conference. 

As a number of speakers have also pointed out, a preparatory committee will also have to decide what is meant by the clause "to which all countries would be invited". It is clear that all members of the UN and of the specialized agencies must be invited, but it remains to be decided what other countries would be invited.

As we havethought about the preparations required for such a conference, we have been struck by the elementary common sense underlying the approach taken by the distinguished representative of Saudi Arabia and reflected in his amendments. We believe the essential point in his suggestion is that a small group should be asked to explore whether there is in fact a constructive basis for a world disarmament conference. We believe this suggestion deserves careful consideration.

I wish therefore to inform this committee that the United States would be willing to participate in a small, initial group to explore areas of agreement on disarmament questions as a preliminary step in the preparations for convening a world disarmament conference.

There are, however, a number of well known difficulties in establishing a group along the lines suggested by the representative of Saudi Arabia, not the least of which is the fact that one of the participants which he proposed has stated that it would not be prepared to meet with that particular group. 

The representative of Saudi Arabia himself took cognizance of these difficulties in the changes he made yesterday in his proposed amendment. We believe it would be essential to add a few other countries to such a group. These might include several states which have major peaceful nuclear programs, as well as several which have played leading roles in developing the idea for a world disarmament conference. We believe that the results of an initial discussion in such a group would be helpful to the preparatory committee which would be established pursuant to the resolution now before us.

In view of the desire of the sponsors of this resolution to avoid incorporating details which might prejudice the realization of its objective, however, we do not think it necessary to amend the present resolution to incorporate any specific reference to 

MORE

GOLDBERG 

-3- an initial exploratory group. This is a matter which could be arranged in the course of the consultations called for by the resolution. We wish to express our appreciation to the representative of Saudi Arabia for his valuable suggestions which of course, are contained in the records of this Committee. We understand his reasons for not being inclined to press his amendments to a vote.

In conclusion, let me recall that in his statement of 

November 18, Mr. Foster made it clear that my Government's reservations regarding a world disarmament conference are not based on our unwillingness to participate in meaningful talks with anyone if such talks could advance the cause of peace and disarmament. But we do insist that a conference of this kind should be carefully prepared, and a decision as to our participation in such a conference will be made in the light of such preparations.

Since we have concluded that the draft resolution as it now stands before this committee meets our major concerns, and taking into account the stated intention of its sponsors to insist on careful and thorough preparations, the United States will vote in favor of the draft resolution contained in Document A/C.1/L. 340 as revised.
