UNITED STATES MISSION FOR RELEASE ON DELIVERY CHECK TEXT AGAINST DELIVERY TO THE UNITED NATIONS 

Press Release No. 4708 

November 18, 1965

STATEMENT BY AMBASSADOR WILLIAM C. FOSTER U. S. REPRESENTATIVE IN COMMITTEE I ON WORLD DISARMAMENT CONFERENCE Mr. Chairman: 

The "Question of convening a World Disarmament Conference" stems from the resolution adopted by the Disarmament Commission at its 98th meeting on 11 June 1965 (DC/224) which, inter alia, recommended that the General Assembly give urgent consideration at its twentieth session to the proposal for convening such a conference. It is pertinent, therefore, to recall the discussion that led to the adoption of that recommendation and the reasons why the United States abstained in the vote on that resolution.

On June 9 I stated in the Disarmament Commission that there were a number of considerations to take into account when examining the advisability of a world conference. So far as the United States is concerned, I said then, the primary consideration is whether the convening of such a conference in foreseeable circumstances will in fact facilitate agreements on arms limitations and reductions which are possible now. For. such agreements provide the only dependable steps to general and complete disarmament under effective international control.

I said that we are far from convinced that this would be the result. 

On the contrary, we are concerned lest, in the absence of positive indications that such a conference could produce useful results, it would only impair the negotiations that are now possible -- and indeed essential 

in the Eighteen Nation Committee and elsewhere.

Mr. Chairman, we have reread carefully the debate at the Disarmament 

Comission and listened with close attention to the statements so far made in this Committee. I am bound to say that we are still not persuaded that a conclusive case has been made for convening the proposed conference under present and foreseeable circumstances. Let me, therefore, take this opportunity to re-state my Government's position on this question. 

in deed as in word 

The United States has consistently demonstrated its readiness to take urgent and practical action to halt the arms race, to turn it back, and to reduce the dangers of armed conflict. MORE

FOSTER 

-2- Our constant aim is to promote serdous and responsible negotiations to that end. We agree with the special priority accorded by the 

Disarmament Commission, the ENDC, and in turn by this Committee to preventing the spread of nuclear weapons, while continuing to work toward the ultimate objective of general and complete disarmament. In accordance with the non-proliferation resolution adopted by this Committee, the United States is determined to press forward in negotiations to achieve as soon as possible a treaty on non-proliferation, one to which we earnestly hope that all nations of the world can subscribe.

While seeking progress on non-proliferation, we attach equal importance to continued efforts to reach agreement on other, related steps to halt and turn back the nuclear arms race. To facilitate agreement, the United States has introduced new proposals and new elements of flexibility into its earlier proposals and positions. We have called attention to our willingness to take into account recent scientific progress in finding the basis for an agreement on a comprehensive test ban. 

We have reiterated our desire for a verified freeze of the numbers and characteristics of strategic nuclear delivery vehicles and our readiness, if progress is made on a freeze, to consider significant reductions in such vehicles. 

We believe the time is ripe for a cutoff of production of fissionable materials for weapons and for the transfer to peaceful uses of agreed, sizeable quantities of such material. In connection with the latter, we have proposed the demonstrated destruction by the United States and the Soviet Union of thousands of nuclear weapons from their respective stocks. 

I have called these proposals once again to the Committee's attention to emphasize that the United States seeks the achievement of concrete measures as soon as possible. We are prepared to negotiate seriously on such measures now, in Geneva, and elsewhere. I have recalled these proposals also because there seems to be a tendency on the part of some to feel that a government's desire to achieve progress in disarmament is somehow demonstrated by its willingness to participate in a world disarmament conference. 

As far as the United States is concerned, our desire to achieve progress has been demonstrated by concrete proposals. 

The only relevant issue is whether such a conference is likely to facilitate the conclusion of those agreements which are now urgent and feasible, or would it delay them. Would such a large conference be able to deal constructively with the sort of technical and complex measures I have mentioned, or would its time be wasted in listening to polemics designed, to exaggerate, differences, rather than to reconcile them. 

Although the Eighteen Nation forum has not yet made the progress we have all desired, its discussions have paved the way for several existing 

MORE

​for

FOSTER 

-3- agreements.

We are convinced that these discussions have also served to lay the groundwork for future agreements which would be instrumental in halting the arms race. As we have stated before, we are not aware that the participants in the ENDC feel that their inability this year to achieve further agreements is due to the absence of one or more governments, or that the difficulties they have encountered would disappear if the forum were modified.

The question is whether a world disarmament conference in the foreseeable circumstances will help to resolve those difficulties. We doubt it. We continue to believe that such a conference could hamper the work of the ENDC, and that nothing should be allowed to interrupt the process of negotiation there.

We are aware that many supporters of the world conference view it as a means of associating certain militarily significant states with future disarmament talks. All of us agree that if substantial progress toward general and complete disarmament is to be made, Communist China must at an appropriate stage participate directly in the process of negotiation. But it is not at all clear that all states wish to enter into disarmament negotiations. Moreover, until such participation can be achieved on a constructive basis, we must continue our efforts to reach agreement on those urgent measures to which the ENDC, the Disarmament Commission and this Committee have rightly given priority principally non-proliferation and related measures to halt and turn back the nuclear arms race. 

Let me say here and now that my Government's reservations regarding a world disarmament conference are not based on any desire to keep Communist China out of disarmament negotiations or a world disarmament conference. Nor are they based on any unwillingness to participate with its representatives in meaningful talks that could advance the cause of peace and disarmament. On the contrary, we would welcome any serious indications of Communist China's interest in promoting peace and disarmament. Indeed we would be prepared to find appropriate ways to bring such indications to bear on the solution of current problems, be they problems affecting the restoration of peace in Southeast Asia or problems involved in achieving arms limitations and reductions. 

But, where is the evidence that the Chinese Communists are prepared for serious disarmament discussions, let alone negotiations? The channels for discussion with Communist China have remained open. Various governments, including three nuclear powers, are represented in Peiping. MORE

​Tese ise se UTE Ham tr TE

FOSTER 

-4- 

To be

In addition, the United States has engaged in periodic talks with representatives of Communist China in Warsaw over several years. precise, we have held 127 talks thus far in this continuing dialogue. 

These channels have elicited no evidence of serious interest by the Communist Chinese leadership in halting the nuclear arms race or in other meaningful disarmament measures. Instead, they have confirmed their defiance of world opinion in starting atmospheric tests in the face of the treaty banning such tests. Despite numerous appeals, some from the 

General Assembly, they have refused to subscribe to that treaty and continue openly to attack it.

I shall not take the time of this Committee to quote from recent Chinese Communist statements recording their negative views on such questions as a comprehensive test ban and nuclear free zones, or appearing to encourage nuclear proliferation by other nations. 

Quite apart from Communist China's attitude, there are many questions of organization, procedure and substance to be studied before governments can be expected to reach deciaions regarding their attendance. It is this factor which makes any decision to convene a conference inadvisable at this time, to say nothing of a date for such a conference. It would, for example, be necessary to have a clear understanding on such matters as the auspices under which such a conference would be held, where it would convene, when and for how long, what its agenda would be, how its secretariat services would be provided, how much it would cost and who would pay for it, and other questions of a similar character. 

Another major question that needs careful study is that of attendance at such a conference. As other speakers have pointed out, to say that "all countries" should be invited does not solve the problem. The establishment of a list of those to be invited would in itself be a difficult task. It is clear that all members of the United Nations and specialized agencies must be invited, but the further question remains as to how to assure the actual participation of militarily significant countries. It would seem prudent, as one of the steps toward a world conference, to find ways to ascertain if Communist China would in fact attend and make a constructive contribution. In any event, we would see no particular purpose to be served by a conference which might add nothing to what we are able to accomplish in the United Nations. 

If any world conference is to be held, it would seem absolutely necessary, as has already been suggested by the distinguished representative of Canada on October 19, that some sort of preparatory body 

MORE

DO: aut Ern de le DS

FOSTER 

-5- be created to examine the organizational and substantive issues involved in convening such a conference and to report its recommendations to governments. We are certain that many governments share the view that they must reserve their decision regarding participation until they can study the recommendations for dealing with these many issues. 

Unless careful preparations were made to assure that a world conference could meet in propitious circumstances, it would merely reflect discord and serve as a propaganda forum, thus worsening the atmosphere for negotiations. It could result in a hardening of positions, rather than a wider measure of agreement. 

The United States will give careful study to recommendations for dealing with such questions as the organization of such a conference, its agenda, finances, and participation. Until such recommendations are available, however, we must continue to reserve our position regarding our participation.

Mr. Chairman, as our experience teaches us, there is no shortcut to disarmament agreements, no easy device, and no panacea, but only hard work and the dedication reflected here. Let us, therefore, insist on the same hard work in connection with preparations designed to assure that any world conference would facilitate and not hamper progress, especially in those areas where progress is now urgently needed and is possible. 

Those preparations should be undertaken in a manner that would give the greatest confidence to all concerned. We cannot afford to risk a futile propaganda display that would set back the very goals we all wish to achieve.

RESOLUTION ADOPTED BY THE GENERAL ASSEMBLY

Declaration on the denuclearization of Africa 

The General Assembly, Believing in the vital necessity of saving contemporary and future generations m the scourge of a nuclear war, Recalling its resolution 1652 (XVI) of 24 November 1961, which called upon all aber States to refrain from testing, storing or transporting nuclear weapons in rica and to consider and respect the continent as a denuclearized zonë, Recalling its resolution 2028 (XX) of 19 November 1965 on the non-proliferation nuclear weapons,

Observing that proposals for the establishment of denuclearized zones in rious other areas of the world have also met with general approval, Convinced that the denuclearization of various areas of the world would help achieve the desired goal of prohibiting the use of nuclear weapons, Considering that the Assembly of Heads of State and Government of the rganization of African Unity, at its first regular session, held at Cairo from 7 to 21 July 1964, issued a solemn declaration on the denuclearization of Africa in which the Heads of State and Government announced their readiness to undertake, In an international treaty to be concluded under the auspices of the United Nations, not to manufacture or acquire control of nuclear weapons, Noting that this declaration on the denuclearization of Africa was endorsed by the Heads of State or Government of Non-Aligned Countries in the Declaration issued on 10 October 1964, at the close of their Second Conference, held at Cairo, See A/5763.

A/RES/2033 (XX) Page 2 ال Recognizing that the denuclearization of Africa would be a practical step towards the prevention of the further spread of nuclear weapons in the world and towards the achievement of general and complete disarmament and of the objectives of the United Fations, 1. Reaffirms its call upon all States to respect the continent of Africa as a nuclear-free zone; 

2. Endorses the declaration on the denuclearization of Africa issued by the 

Heads of State and Government of African countries; 

3. Calls upon, all States to respect and abide by the aforementioned declaration; 

4. Calls upon all States to refrain from the use, or the threat of use, nuclear weapons on the African continent; of 5. Calls upon all States to refrain from testing, manufacturing, using or deploying nuclear weapons on the continent of Africa, and from acquiring such weapons or taking any action which would compel African States to take similar action; 

6. Urges those States possessing nuclear weapons and capability not to transfer nuclear weapons, scientific data or technological assistance to the national control of any State, either directly or indirectly, in any form which may be used to assist such States in the manufacture or use of nuclear weapons in Africa; 

7. Expresses the hope that the African States will initiate studies, as they deem appropriate, with a view to implementing the denuclearization of Africa, and take the necessary measures through the Organization of African Unity to achieve this end; 

8. Urges the African States to keep the United Nations informed of any further developments in this regard; 9.

Requests the Secretary-General to extend to the Organization of African Unity such facilities and assistance as may be requested in order to achieve the aims of the present resolution.

2032 (XX). Urgent need for suspension of nuclear and thermonuclear tests

December 17, 1965

QUESTION OF GENERAL AND COMPLETE DISARMAMENT 

Cyprus submitted a draft resolution to the 

First Committee of the United Nations General Assembly on the question of general and complete disarmament. 

This draft resolution, as revised, was adopted by the First Committee and was recommended to the General Assembly. 

The General Assembly adopted the draft resolution on December 3, 1965, by a vote of 102 to 0, with 6 austentions (Albania, Algeria, France, Guinea, Mali, United Republic of Tanzania).

For information purposes, there is attached a copy of the resolution. Attachment

RESOLUTION ADOPTED BY THE GENERAL ASSEMBLY 

on the report of the First Committee (A/6129)7 

2031 (XX). Question of general and complete disarmament

The General Assembly, Having received the reports of the Conference of the Eighteen-Nation Committee 

1 Disarmament,

Recalling its resolutions 1378 (XIV) of 20 November 1959, 1722 (XVI) of 

0 December 1961, 1767 (XVII) of 21 November 1962 and 1908 (XVIII) of 

7 November 1963,

Conscious of its responsibility under the Charter of the United Nations for lisarmament and the consolidation of peace, 1. Requests the Conference of the Eighteen-Nation Committee on Disarmament to continue its efforts towards making substantial progress in reaching agreement on the question of general and complete disarmament under effective international control, as well as on collateral measures; 2. 

Decides to refer to the Eighteen-Nation Committee all documents and records of the First Committee concerning all matters related to the disarmament question; 

3. Requests the Eighteen-Nation Committee to resume its work as early as possible and to report to the General Assembly, as appropriate, on the progress achieved.

DISARMAMENT CHRONOLOGY 

SEPTEMBER 1965 There is attached the chronology of disarmament developments for September prepared by the Reference 

Research Staff.

These chronologies are for background information purposes only and do not in any way constitute official statements of policy. Attachment 

As stated.

Room 1882 Ext. 2568 UNCLASSIFIED

SEPTEMBER 1965 

I. HIGHLIGHTS 

The ENDC recessed on September 16, 1965. On September 7 the Soviets proposed extending the partial nuclear weapons test ban treaty to cover underground tests above seismic magnitude 4.75, together with a moratorium on all other underground tests pending negotiation of a comprehensive test ban agreement. The United States immediately opposed the Soviet proposals. On September 14, Italy tabled a proposal by which the non-nuclear states would unilaterally renounce acquisition of nuclear weapons for a fixed period of time pending negotiation of a universal non-proliferation treaty. The eight non-aligned states on September 15 tabled two joint memoranda: the first recommended enactment of measures to halt the spread of nuclear weapons and to reduce and eventually eliminate existing nuclear stockpiles; the second urged the nuclear powers to cease immediately nuclear weapons testing in all environments until a comprehensive test ban treaty could be negotiated.

In his maiden speech before the UN General Assembly on September 23, Ambassador Goldberg stressed the importance of halting the proliferation of nuclear weapons as quickly as possible and called for early action on the U.S. anti-proliferation draft treaty of August 17. He also stated that the 

United States would transfer 60,000 kilograms of weapons grade 

U-235 to peaceful uses if the USSR agreed in turn to transfer 40,000 kilograms, the materials to be obtained by destroying nuclear weapons selected by each nation from its stockpiles. 

On September 24, Foreign Minister Gromyko tabled a

Soviet non-dissemination treaty and reaffirmed Soviet approval of the UAR proposals to extend coverage of the limited test ban treaty to include underground tests stronger than seismic magnitude 4.75 and to declare a moratorium on all underground tests below that magnitude.

! 

II. CHRONOLOGY September 2, 1965. At the ENDC, ACDA Director Foster restated the U.S. position that in the present state of seismic detection capabilities distant seismic instruments could not determine conclusively whether a significant fraction of underground seismic events were caused by earthquakes or by explosions. The United States therefore still held the viewpoint that a comprehensive test ban could be adequately verified only by a limited number of on-site 

September 5, 1965.

The report of the Ackley Committee on the Economic Impact of Defense and Disarmament released today, held that neither a gradual reduction in military expenditures nor a shift from one type of defense spending to another, such as from strategic weapons to weapons of limited warfare, would present serious problems for the nation's economy. "Even general and complete disarmament would pose no insurmountable problems," the report stated, "indeed, it would mainly afford opportunities for a better life for our citizens, We can maintain our national growth and prosperity with the help of the fiscal and monetary tools at our disposal, although reduced expenditures for defense may become a factor to be taken into account in choosing specific fiscal-monetary actions. Sustaining rapid growth and general prosperity will, itself, require major fiscalmonetary decisions in the next few years. General and complete disarmament, or more moderate shifts or cutbacks in defense, would add a new element to the economic equation, but would not be as significant a factor as economic growth itself. 

Yet even when the Nation as a whole is enjoying a fully prosperous economy, certain communities and many individuals may have serious economic problems and require special assistance."

September 7, 1965. At the ENDC, Ambassador Tsarapkin noted the UAR proposals of August 17 and stated that the Soviet Union was willing to extend the scope of the limited test-ban treaty to cover underground tests greater than seismic magnitude 4.75 and to agree to a moratorium on underground nuclear tests of lesser magnitude. He called this a "realistic approach," consistent with the Soviet view that national means of detection were adequate to monitor compliance with an underground test ban.

--  

ACDA Director Foster replied that the United States had long been opposed to an "unverified moratorium." The 

United States sought an adequately verified ban on all tests and did not feel that "half-measures" could create good prospects for a stable agreement. 

September 14, 1965. At the ENDC, the Italian delegation tabled a proposal for a "controlled nuclear moratorium" by which the non-nuclear countries would unilaterally renounce acquisition of nuclear weapons for a specified period of time. If the nuclear powers were unable to agree on a nonproliferation treaty within the specified time limit, the non-nuclear states would resume their "freedom of action" in regard to nuclear weapons. Ambassador Cavalletti explained that Italy still supported the U.S. draft non-dissemination treaty and that the draft declaration was intended to remove the immediate danger of dissemination while efforts continued for a general treaty.

September 15, 1965. At the ENDC the eight non-aligned countries tabled two joint memoranda: In the first memorandum, they urged the major powers, in order to facilitate a comprehensive test ban, to exchange information on advanced seismic detection and identification techniques involving underground explosions, and they appealed for a suspension of nuclear testing "in all environments" until a comprehensive test ban could be negotiated. In the other memorandum, they asked the great powers to "accord special priority" to the enactment of a draft non-dissemination treaty "coupled with or followed by tangible steps to halt the nuclear arms race and to limit, reduce, and eliminate the stocks of nuclear weapons and the means of their delivery."

September 16, 1965. The ENDC adjourned after having been in session since July 27. The delegates agreed to reconvene "as soon as possible after termination of the consideration of the question of disarmament at the twentieth session of the General Assembly" of the United Nations. 

September 23, 1965. At the UN General Assembly, Ambassador

Goldberg stated that the current arms race among nations gravely threatened world order and peace and that "first priority must be given to halting the spread of nuclear weapons. 11 He called upon UN members to "face this problem squarely now," lest the

opportunity to halt the spread of nuclear weapons "disappear forever." He urged early action on the U.S. draft anti proliferation treaty of August 17. 

He also called attention to the Montana Large Aperture Seismic Array (LASA), by which the United States expected to achieve substantial improvement in underground nuclear test detection, and stated that the United States was going to invite many UN member states to send observers to inspect the LASA facilities in Montana. 

He advocated continued exploration of a freeze on the number and characteristics of strategic nuclear delivery vehicles and added that the United States was willing to explore the possibility of reducing such vehicles if progress was made on a freeze. Renewing the U.S. proposals for a cutoff of fissionable materials for weapons purposes and the transfer of fissionable materials to peaceful uses, he stated that the United States now proposed "the demonstrated destruction by the United States and the Soviet Union of a substantial number of nuclear weapons from their respective stocks." 

The United States was willing to transfer 60,000 kilograms of U-235 to non-weapon uses if the Soviet Union would transfer 40,000 kilograms. The amounts of U-235 to be transferred would be obtained by destroying weapons chosen by each nation from its nuclear weapons stockpiles. stockpiles. Moreover, the United States was willing to transfer the plutonium obtained from the destroyed weapons to peaceful uses, the Ambassador stated, under IAEA or equivalent safeguards. 11 

September 24, 1965. At the General Assembly Foreign 

Minister Gromyko tabled a Soviet draft non-dissemination treaty. Article I of the Soviet draft prohibited states from transferring, directly or indirectly through third states or groups of states, nuclear weapons "to the ownership or control of states or groups of states not possessing nuclear weapons.

It also denied to states which do not now have a nuclear capacity "the right to participate in the ownership, control, or use of nuclear weapons," and prohibited the nuclear powers from giving assistance in the manufacturing or testing of nuclear weapons to such states. Article II prohibited non-nuclear states from manufacturing nuclear weapons, either independently or together with other states, "in their own territory or in the territory of other states, and from receiving nuclear weapons in any form, directly or indirectly through third states or groups of states. It also prohibited. non-nuclear states from requesting or receiving assistance from nuclear states in the manufacturing of such 11

weapons or from obtaining information or documentation from the nuclear powers relative to such manufacturing. 

The Soviet Foreign Minister also proposed that the UN consider convening a worldwide disarmament conference in mid-1966 in which all countries without exception could participate. He also reaffirmed Soviet approval and support for the UAR proposals to extend the coverage of the limited test ban treaty to include underground explosions greater than 4.75 seismic magnitude and to declare a moratorium on tests below that threshold until a comprehensive test ban treaty could be negotiated. 

September 24, 1965. Dr. G.A. Siwabessy, head of

Indonesia's national atomic energy agency, stated at the 

International Atomic Energy Agency (IAEA) conference in 

Tokyo that the proliferation of nuclear weapons would serve to prevent nuclear warfare in the future. He expressed the opinion that with an increasing number of countries achieving nuclear weapons capabilities the greater the fear would become on the part of any one state to use its weapons.
